On the classification of multiplicity-free Hamiltonian actions by regular proper symplectic groupoids
Maarten Mol

Abstract
. In this paper we study a natural generalization of symplectic toric manifolds in the context of regular Poisson manifolds of compact types. To be more precise, we consider a class of multiplicity-free Hamiltonian actions by regular proper symplectic groupoids that we call faithful. Given such a groupoid, we classify its faithful multiplicity-free Hamiltonian actions in terms of what we call Delzant subspaces of its orbit space – certain ‘suborbifolds with corners’ satisfying the Delzant condition relative to the integral affine orbifold structure of the orbit space. This encompasses both the classification of symplectic toric manifolds (due to Delzant) in terms of Delzant polytopes and the classification of proper Lagrangian fibrations over an integral affine base manifold (due to Duistermaat) in terms of a sheaf cohomology group. Each Delzant subspace comes with an orbifold version of this cohomology, the degree one part of which classifies faithful multiplicity-free Hamiltonian actions with momentum map image equal to the Delzant subspace, provided there exists such an action. The obstruction to existence is encoded by a degree two class in this cohomology: the Lagrangian Dixmier-Douady class. In addition to the above, we introduce another invariant, which leads to a variation of our classification result involving only classical sheaf cohomology and the group cohomology of certain modules for the isotropy groups of the groupoid.



Introduction

This paper concerns the classification of multiplicity-free Hamiltonian actions of regular proper symplectic groupoids. Recall that the notion of Hamiltonian action for symplectic groupoids (introduced in
[
30
]
) unifies various momentum map theories appearing in Poisson geometry, with the common feature that in each case the momentum map is a Poisson map:

J:(S,\omega)\to(M,\pi)

from a symplectic manifold into a specified Poisson manifold
(M,\pi)
, equipped with a Hamiltonian action of a natural symplectic groupoid integrating
(M,\pi)
(see e.g.
[
46
,
52
]
and the references therein). Here, we focus on such actions for which the Poisson structure is regular and the symplectic groupoid is proper, using the framework developed in
[
7
]
for such symplectic groupoids and their associated Poisson structures. Furthermore, the actions that we consider are multiplicity-free, in the sense of
[
18
]
. The main point of this paper is to give a classification of a large class of such actions, that we call faithful.

To explain this classification we first focus on actions of symplectic torus bundles, which form a class of symplectic groupoids analogous to the class of Lie groups formed by tori. Like tori, these admit a particularly concrete description in terms of lattices. Indeed, a symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
induces an integral affine structure on its base
M
, encoded by a Lagrangian lattice bundle
\Lambda
in the cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
, and
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
is entirely encoded by
\Lambda
. More precisely, it is canonically isomorphic to the symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
over
M
, where
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}:=T^{*}M/\Lambda
denotes the bundle of tori associated to
\Lambda
(a groupoid over
M
with multiplication given by fiberwise addition) and
\Omega_{\Lambda}
is the symplectic form induced by the canonical symplectic form
\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}}
on the cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
(see e.g.
[
7
, Proposition 3.1.6]
). The starting point for our classification in the case of
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces (Theorem
1
below) was to find the connection between two classical results: the classification of symplectic toric manifolds in terms of Delzant polytopes (due to Delzant
[
12
]
) and the classification of proper Lagrangian fibrations via their Lagrangian Chern class (implicit in Duistermaat’s work
[
14
]
), recalled in Examples
1
and
2
below. Inspired by these, we consider Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces
J:(S,\omega)\to M
with the three properties below.

i)

The
\mathcal{T}
-action is free on a dense subset of
S
.

ii)

\text{dim}(S)=2\text{dim}(M)
and the fibers of
J
are connected.

iii)

The momentum map
J:S\to M
is proper as a map into its image.

We call these
faithful toric

(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces. An equivalent set of conditions consists of i) together with the two conditions below (cf. Appendix A).

ii’)

The
\mathcal{T}
-orbits coincide with the fibers of
J
.

iii’)

The map
S/\mathcal{T}\to M
induced by
J
is a topological embedding.

Note that the last condition here is purely topological, as the second condition means that the induced map
S/\mathcal{T}\to M
is injective. Like symplectic toric manifolds, faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces are closely related to integrable systems. For instance, in local coordinates for
M
, the momentum map
J
of such a
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space is an integrable system with only elliptic singularities.

Theorem 1

(Classification of faithful toric spaces)
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
be a symplectic torus bundle over
M
with induced integral affine structure
\Lambda
on
M
.

a)

For any faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
, the image of the momentum map
J
is a Delzant subspace of the integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
(Definition
1.22
; cf. Example
1
).

b)

For any Delzant subspace
\Delta
in
(M,\Lambda)
, there is a canonical bijection:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{ }\text{ }\textrm{Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{ {faithful} toric $(\mathcal{T},\Omega)$-spaces }\\
&\textrm{ with momentum image $\Delta$}\end{aligned}\right\}\longleftrightarrow H^{1}(\Delta,\mathcal{L}),

defined by associating to such a
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space its ‘Lagrangian Chern class’ (cf. Example
2
). Here the right-hand side denotes the degree one cohomology of the sheaf
\mathcal{L}
on
\Delta
consisting of Lagrangian sections of

\mathcal{T}
over
\Delta
(Definition
2.2
).

So, there is a canonical bijection:

(1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{ }\text{ }\textrm{Isomorphism classes of\text{ }}\\
&\textrm{{faithful} toric $(\mathcal{T},\Omega)$-spaces }\end{aligned}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{Pairs $(\Delta,\kappa)$ consisting of a Delzant subspace $\Delta$}\\
&\quad\text{ of $(M,\Lambda)$ and an element $\kappa$ of $H^{1}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})$ }\end{aligned}\right\}.

This theorem is an amalgam of the classifications in the work of Delzant and Duistermaat mentioned before. The Delzant subspaces appearing in part
a
are codimension zero submanifolds with corners that are ‘fully compatible with the integral affine structure’. The Delzant polytopes in Delzant’s classification are examples of these (see Example
1
below). On the other hand, the idea behind the bijection in part
b
is the same as that in the classification in Duistermaat’s work (briefly recalled in Example
2
below). As in those classifications, the integral affine structure plays a key role in Theorem
1
.

Example 1
.

Delzant’s classification of toric symplectic manifolds can be recovered from Theorem
1
, as follows. Let
T
be a torus with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{t}
and character lattice
\Lambda_{T}^{*}
in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. Recall that a Delzant polytope in the integral affine vector space
(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})
is a convex polytope
\Delta
in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
with the property that at each vertex
x
the polyhedral cone
C_{x}(\Delta)
spanned by the edges meeting at
x
is generated by a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
\Lambda_{T}^{*}
. Delzant showed that there is a canonical bijection:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of compact, \text{ }}\\
&\textrm{ connected toric symplectic $T$-spaces }\end{aligned}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} \textrm{Delzant polytopes in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})$}\end{aligned}\right\},

defined by assigning to such a
T
-space the image of its momentum map. This can be recovered from the theorem above, applied to the cotangent groupoid (see e.g.
[
30
, Section 3]
):

(\mathcal{T},\Omega)=(T{\times}\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Omega_{\textrm{can}}),

by means of the following two observations:

•

\{\textrm{Delzant polytopes in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})$}\}=\{\textrm{Compact, connected Delzant subspaces of $(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})$}\}
,

•

for any Delzant polytope
\Delta
the cohomology
H^{1}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})
vanishes, due to convexity of
\Delta
.

Example 2
.

For any integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
,
\Delta:=M
itself is a Delzant subspace. In this case Theorem
1
recovers the aforementioned classification of proper Lagrangian fibrations. To explain this, recall that a proper Lagrangian fibration is a symplectic manifold
(S,\omega)
together with a proper surjective submersion
J:(S,\omega)\to M
with Lagrangian fibers. We will always assume such fibrations to have connected fibers, without further notice. Any such fibration over
M
induces an integral affine structure
\Lambda
on
M
.
Duistermaat implicitly showed that, given any fixed integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
, there is a canonical bijection:

(2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\quad\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\quad\textrm{ proper Lagrangian fibrations over $M$ }\\
&\textrm{ with induced integral affine structure $\Lambda$ }\end{aligned}\right\}\longleftrightarrow H^{1}(M,\mathcal{L})

that associates to
[J:(S,\omega)\to M]
its so-called Lagrangian Chern class (called Lagrangian class in
[
51
,
44
]
). The key insight leading to (
2
) is that fibrations as in (
2
) can be viewed as certain principal
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-bundles, called symplectic
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-torsors in
[
44
,
7
]
. Indeed, any such fibration is a Poisson map into
(M,0)
that admits a (necessarily unique) Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-action along it, which turns it into a symplectic
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-torsor (cf.
[
7
, pg. 23, pg. 88]
). From that point of view the construction of the bijection (
2
) is analogous to that in the usual classification of principal
G
-bundles in terms of Čech cohomology. Given a symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
over
M
, the notion of a symplectic
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-torsor coincides with that of a faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space with momentum image
M
. So, in the setting of Theorem
1
there is a canonical bijection:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\quad\textrm{Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\quad\textrm{proper Lagrangian fibrations over $M$ }\\
&\textrm{ with induced integral affine structure $\Lambda$ }\end{aligned}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }\textrm{Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{ {faithful} toric $(\mathcal{T},\Omega)$-spaces }\\
&\text{}\textrm{ with momentum image $M$}\end{aligned}\right\}.

In this way Theorem
1
recovers the classification of proper Lagrangian fibrations above.

Next, we explain our classification in full generality. Throughout this paper, we use the following as a working definition for the class of actions that we consider.

Definition
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid and let
\mathcal{T}
be the torus bundle over
M
with fibers the identity components of the isotropy groups of
\mathcal{G}
(also see Subsection
1.2.3
). By a
faithful multiplicity-free

(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space we mean a Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
with the following three properties.

i)

The induced
\mathcal{T}
-action is free on a dense subset of
S
.

ii)

The
\mathcal{T}
-orbits coincide with the fibers of
J
.

iii)

The transverse momentum map
\underline{J}:\underline{S}\to\underline{M}
is a topological embedding.

Here and throughout:

•

\underline{S}:=S/\mathcal{G}
denotes the orbit space of the
\mathcal{G}
-action,

•

\underline{M}:=M/\mathcal{G}
denotes the orbit space of
\mathcal{G}
,

•

\underline{J}
denotes the map induced by
J
, which we refer to as the
transverse momentum map
.

Of course, when
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is a symplectic torus bundle this boils down to the notion of faithful toric space considered before. As in that case, the last condition above is purely topological, since
\underline{J}
is injective by the second condition. Moreover, there is an equivalent set of conditions (given in Proposition
A.1
) of the same type as the first set of conditions for faithful toric spaces. From these conditions it is clearer that faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces are closely related to non-commutative integrable systems (e.g. in the sense of
[
25
]
).

For the rest of this introduction, let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
and
\mathcal{T}
be as in the above definition. The fact that symplectic torus bundles induce an integral affine structure on their base generalizes as follows. The symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
induces an integral affine structure, not on
M
, but on its orbit space
\underline{M}
, which has the structure of an orbifold encoded by the orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}\rightrightarrows M
. The induced integral affine structure on this orbifold is encoded by a
\mathcal{B}
-invariant lattice bundle
\Lambda
in the co-normal bundle
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
to the orbits of
\mathcal{G}
. This is shown in
[
7
]
and recalled in Subsection
1.2.3
. Part
a
of Theorem
1
generalizes as follows.

Theorem 2
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid. For any faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
, the image of the transverse momentum map:

\underline{J}(\underline{S})\subset\underline{M}

is a Delzant subspace of the orbit space
\underline{M}
(in the sense of Definition
1.28
, with respect to the integral affine orbifold structure mentioned above).

Here, intuitively, one can think of Delzant subspaces as codimension zero ‘suborbifolds with corners’ that are ‘fully compatible with the integral affine structure’.
We split the generalization of part
b
of Theorem
1
into two theorems. The first explains to which extent the momentum map image determines the isomorphism class of a given faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space.

Theorem 3

(First structure theorem)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid, let
\underline{\Delta}
be a Delzant subspace of the orbit space
\underline{M}
(in the same sense as in Theorem
2
) and let
\Delta
be the corresponding invariant subspace of
M
. If:

(3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }\textrm{Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{ faithful multiplicity-free $(\mathcal{G},\Omega)$-spaces }\\
&\quad\text{ }\text{ }\textrm{with momentum map image $\Delta$}\end{aligned}\right\}\neq\emptyset,

then it is a torsor with abelian structure group:

(4)

H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L}),

the degree one cohomology of the
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
-sheaf
\mathcal{L}
consisting of isotropic sections of
\mathcal{T}
over
\Delta
(defined in Subsection
3.1.1
). This action is natural with respect to Morita equivalences (in the sense of Proposition
3.9
).

The second addresses the condition (
3
).

Theorem 4

(Splitting theorem)
.

In the setting of Theorem
3
, the following are equivalent.

a)

The condition (
3
) holds.

b)

As pre-symplectic groupoid with corners over
\Delta
(see Remark
1.29
and Definition
B.22
),
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
and
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})|_{\Delta}
are Morita equivalent.

c)

The Lagrangian Dixmier-Douady class (see Definition
3.14
):

(5)

\emph{\textrm{c}}_{2}(\mathcal{G},\Omega,\underline{\Delta})\in H^{2}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})

vanishes.

Here
\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}
denotes the semi-direct product groupoid over
M
associated to the
\mathcal{B}
-action along
\mathcal{T}\to M
via conjugation in
\mathcal{G}
. Moreover,
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is the restriction to
\mathcal{T}
of the symplectic form
\Omega
on
\mathcal{G}
.

Remark 1
.

Theorem
3
and the proof of Theorem
4
lead to a sharper conclusion in the setting of Theorem
1
. Indeed, Theorem
2.11
shows that for any Delzant subspace
\Delta
of
(M,\Lambda)
there is a canonically associated faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space
J_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M
with momentum image
\Delta
. So, there is a canonical section of the map:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\textrm{Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{ faithful toric $(\mathcal{T},\Omega)$-spaces }\end{aligned}\right\}\longrightarrow\{\textrm{Delzant subspaces of $(M,\Lambda)$}\}

defined by sending such a
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space to its momentum map image. Combined with Theorem
3
this leads to the bijection (
1
), where a pair
(\Delta,\kappa)
corresponds to the isomorphism class obtained by letting
\kappa
act on the isomorphism class of
J_{\Delta}
. More generally, Theorem
3
leads to such a bijection when
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is a semi-direct product symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
, with
\mathcal{B}
an etale orbifold groupoid acting on a symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
as in Subsection
3.3.2
(by the same reasoning, using Proposition
3.10
in addition to Theorem
2.11
).

Example 3
.

Let
G
be an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group, meaning that its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is abelian. For Hamiltonian
G
-spaces, the outcome of Theorems
3
and
4
can be rephrased in terms of the group
G
. To be more precise, recall that Hamiltonian
G
-spaces correspond to Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces for the cotangent groupoid:

(\mathcal{G},\Omega):=(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},\Omega_{\textrm{can}})

over
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(see e.g.
[
30
]
). A compact and connected Hamiltonian
G
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
corresponds to a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space if and only if
J:(S,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a toric symplectic manifold with respect to the induced action of the identity component
T
of
G
(a torus). So, these are toric symplectic manifolds with additional symmetry, which is reflected by the fact that the Delzant polytope corresponding to such a
T
-space (its momentum map image) is invariant with respect to the induced action of the finite group
\varGamma:=G/T
on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Let us call such Hamiltonian
G
-spaces
maximally toric
. Our theorems lead to the following conclusions, which (somewhat surprisingly) we could not find in the literature. Let
\Delta
be a
\varGamma
-invariant Delzant polytope in
(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})
and let
J:(S,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
be a toric symplectic
T
-space with momentum map image
\Delta
. The latter is unique up to
T
-equivariant symplectomorphism and any isomorphism class of maximally toric Hamiltonian
G
-spaces with momentum map image
\Delta
can be represented by a Hamiltonian
G
-action on
(S,\omega)
with momentum map
J
(cf. Example
1
).

•

The condition (
3
) can be rephrased as requiring that the symplectic
T
-action on
(S,\omega)
extends to such an action of
G
that is compatible with the
\varGamma
-action on
\Delta
. It follows from Theorem
4
that this holds if and only if the short exact sequence of groups:

(6)

1\to T\to G\to\varGamma\to 1

admits a right splitting (i.e. the extension is trivial up to isomorphism). Of course, there is a natural group cohomology class in
H^{2}(\varGamma,T)
that encodes the obstruction to the existence of such a splitting, where
T
is viewed as
\varGamma
-module via the action by conjugation in
G
. This class is recovered by the Lagrangian Dixmier-Douady class via a canonical isomorphism between
H^{2}(\varGamma,T)
and
H^{2}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
(see Proposition
4.30
a
).

•

Theorem
3
explains in which different ways the
T
-action on
(S,\omega)
extends to a
G
-action as above. Namely: if the
T
-action extends, then up to isomorphism of Hamiltonian
G
-spaces there is exactly one such
G
-action for each group cohomology class in
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
. This is because the structure group (
4
) is isomorphic to
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
(see Proposition
4.30
a
). Explicitly, given such a
G
-action, the
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
-action of Theorem
3
twists the
G
-action by a group
1
-cocycle
{c}:\varGamma\to T
:

g\cdot_{{c}}p=g{c}([g])\cdot p,\quad g\in G,\quad p\in S.

So, the
T
-action extends in different ways, each corresponding to an automorphism of the extension (
6
), modulo automorphisms given by conjugation by elements of
T
.

Example 4
.

Example
2
generalizes as follows: for source-connected
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
, faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces with momentum image
\Delta=M
correspond to certain proper isotropic fibrations over
M
. To elaborate, recall that proper isotropic fibrations are defined by replacing ‘Lagrangian’ by ‘isotropic’ in Example
2
, with the additional requirement that the symplectic orthogonal
\textrm{Ker}(\textrm{d}J)^{\omega}
is an involutive distribution (these are symplectically complete isotropic fibrations in the sense of
[
11
]
). Any such fibration
J:(S,\omega)\to M
induces a regular Poisson structure
\pi
on
M
, uniquely determined by the property that
J:(S,\omega)\to(M,\pi)
is a Poisson map (as a consequence of Libermann’s theorem
[
28
]
). Moreover, as shown in
[
11
]
, such a fibration induces a transverse integral affine structure to the foliation
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
on
M
by symplectic leaves of
\pi
, encoded by a lattice bundle
\Lambda
in the co-normal bundle
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
. Suppose now that
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
is of proper type, meaning that its holonomy groupoid
\text{Hol}(M,\mathcal{F}_{\pi})
is proper. It is shown in
[
7
]
that there is a natural source-connected proper symplectic groupoid:

(7)

(\textrm{Hol}_{J}(M),\Omega_{J})\rightrightarrows M

associated to
J:(S,\omega)\to M
, with the following properties.

•

It integrates
(M,\pi)
.

•

The (transverse) integral affine structure induced by
(\textrm{Hol}_{J}(M),\Omega)
is that induced by
J
.

•

The associated orbifold structure on
\underline{M}
is that encoded by
\text{Hol}(M,\mathcal{F}_{\pi})
, because the associated orbifold groupoid is the integration
\textrm{Hol}(M,\mathcal{F}_{\pi})
of
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
.

The map
J:(S,\omega)\to M
admits a canonical Hamiltonian
(\textrm{Hol}_{J}(M),\Omega)
-action, which is makes it a faithful multiplicity-free
(\textrm{Hol}_{J}(M),\Omega)
-space. More generally, for any orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}
integrating
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
, there is a natural integration
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
of
(M,\pi)
associated to
J
, for which the associated orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is the integration
\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{F}_{\pi}
. This is called the
\mathcal{B}
-integration of
(M,\pi)
relative to
J
(
[
7
, pg. 82]
). It also induces the same (transverse) integral affine structure as
J
and acts canonically along
J
, making it a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space. This explains how such proper isotropic fibrations can naturally be viewed as faithful multiplicity-free spaces. For the converse, let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be any source-connected, regular and proper symplectic groupoid, let
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
and let
\pi
be the induced Poisson structure on its base
M
. Then for any faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
with
J(S)=M
, the momentum map is a proper isotropic fibration that induces this same Poisson structure on
M
. In fact,
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is the
\mathcal{B}
-integration of
(M,\pi)
relative to
J
. An isomorphism of two such proper isotropic fibrations:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{1},\omega_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{2},\omega_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{2}}

is automatically
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant, or in other words, it is an isomorphism of Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces. Hence, there is a canonical bijection:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of }\\
&\textrm{proper isotropic fibrations over $(M,\pi)$}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\textrm{with $\mathcal{B}$-integration $(\mathcal{G},\Omega)$}\end{aligned}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\text{ }\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{ faithful multiplicity-free $(\mathcal{G},\Omega)$-spaces }\\
&\quad\quad\quad\textrm{with momentum image $M$}\end{aligned}\right\}.

This explains the relationship between faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces with momentum image
\Delta=M
and proper isotropic fibrations over
M
. Finally, let us point out: by the splitting theorem,
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is the
\mathcal{B}
-integration relative to some proper isotropic fibration if and only if
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is Morita equivalent to
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
. This recovers a result of
[
7
]
.

Our final two main theorems provide an alternative way of listing the elements of (
3
), by means of an additional invariant of faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces that we call the
ext-invariant
(Definition
4.21
). This invariant is a global section of what we call the
ext-sheaf
(Definition
4.18
):

(8)

\mathcal{I}^{1}=\mathcal{I}^{1}_{(\mathcal{G},\Omega,\underline{\Delta})}.

This is a sheaf of sets on
\underline{\Delta}
naturally associated to
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
. Its stalk at an orbit
\mathcal{O}_{x}
of
\mathcal{G}
through a point
x\in\Delta
is the set:

I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x}),

consisting of right-splittings of the short exact sequence of isotropy groups:

(9)

1\to\mathcal{T}_{x}\to\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{B}_{x}\to 1,

modulo the
\mathcal{T}_{x}
-action by conjugation (cf. Definition
1.4
and Proposition
1.11
).

Theorem 5

(Second structure theorem)
.

Suppose that we are in the setting of Theorem
3
and let
e
be a global section of the ext-sheaf (
8
). If:

(10)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\text{ }\text{ }\textrm{Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{ faithful multiplicity-free $(\mathcal{G},\Omega)$-spaces }\\
&\quad\textrm{ with momentum map image $\Delta$}\\
&\quad\quad\textrm{ and with ext-invariant $e$ }\end{aligned}\right\}\neq\emptyset,

then it is a torsor with abelian structure group:

(11)

H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}}),

the degree one cohomology of the sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{L}}
on
\underline{\Delta}
associated to the
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
-sheaf
\mathcal{L}
by considering
\mathcal{B}
-invariant sections (see Subsection
4.1.1
). This action is natural with respect to Morita equivalences (in the sense explained in Subsection
4.1.2
).

The structure groups in the first and second structure theorem are related by a natural injective group homomorphism:

(12)

H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L}),

which is compatible with the actions in these theorems. This leads to:

Theorem 6

(Third structure theorem)
.

In the setting of Theorem
3
, if (
3
) holds, then the image of the map:

(13)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\text{ }\text{}\textrm{Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{ faithful multiplicity-free $(\mathcal{G},\Omega)$-spaces }\\
&\quad\quad\textrm{with momentum map image $\Delta$}\end{aligned}\right\}\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})

that associates to an isomorphism class of such a
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space its ext-invariant, is a torsor with abelian structure group the quotient:

(14)

\frac{H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})}{H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})}.

This action is natural with respect to Morita equivalences as well.

Remark 2
.

The injection (
12
) fits in an exact sequence:

(15)

0\to H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})\to H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})\to\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})\to H^{2}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})\to H^{2}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L}),\quad\mathcal{H}^{1}:=R^{1}(q_{\Delta})_{*}(\mathcal{L}),

which is the exact sequence in low degrees of a Leray-type spectral sequence for the canonical map of topological groupoids
q_{\Delta}
from
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
to the unit groupoid of
\underline{\Delta}
. Here
\mathcal{H}^{1}
denotes the first right-derived functor of the push-forward along
q_{\Delta}
applied to the
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
-sheaf
\mathcal{L}
. In Subsection
4.2.2
we show that there is a natural free and transitive action of the sheaf of abelian groups
\mathcal{H}^{1}
on
\mathcal{I}^{1}
. Moreover, we show that the action of (
14
) on the image of (
13
) in Theorem
6
(which is inherited from the action of (
4
) in Theorem
3
) factors through this
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-action via the middle map in (
15
).

Together, the second and third structure theorem provide a way of listing the isomorphism classes of faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces different from that in the first structure theorem.

Example 5
.

To illustrate this difference we return to Example
3
. From the second and third structure theorem and the splitting theorem we obtain a canonical bijection:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{ Isomorphism classes of compact, connected, }\\
&\quad\text{ {maximally} toric Hamiltonian $G$-spaces }\end{aligned}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{$\varGamma$-invariant Delzant}\\
&\text{polytopes in $(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})$}\end{aligned}\right\}\times I^{1}(G,T),

that associates to a class
[J:(S,\omega)\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}]
the pair consisting of the image
\Delta
of
J
and the germ of its ext-invariant at any
\varGamma
-fixed point in
\Delta
. This is because for any
\varGamma
-invariant Delzant polytope
\Delta
in
(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})
the following hold.

•

The structure group (
11
) is trivial, due to convexity of
\Delta
(see Proposition
4.30
b
).

•

As mentioned before: the structure group (
4
) is naturally isomorphic to the degree one group cohomology
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
.

•

The set of global sections of (
8
) is naturally in bijection with the set
I^{1}(G,T)
consisting of right splittings of (
6
) modulo the
T
-action by conjugation. Moreover, under this and the previous identification the action of (
4
) on the set of global sections of (
8
) is identified with the free and transitive
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
-action on
I^{1}(G,T)
obtained by viewing
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
as the group of automorphisms of the extension (
6
) modulo the subgroup of automorphisms given by conjugation by elements of
T
(see Proposition
4.30
c
).

To further illustrate the use of the second and third structure theorem, in Subsection
4.1.3
we give a more concrete example (Example
4.23
) of a symplectic groupoid and a Delzant subspace to which we apply these theorems, in order to obtain an explicit list of all isomorphism classes of faithful multiplicity-free actions of that symplectic groupoid with that Delzant subspace as their momentum map image.

In fact, the above theorems apply to a more general class of Hamiltonian group actions than that in Example
5
, to give the following classification.

Example 6
.

Let
G
be any compact Lie group. A compact and connected Hamiltonian
G
-space is called multiplicity-free if all its symplectic reduced spaces are points. Let us call a Hamiltonian
G
-space
regular
if its momentum map image is contained in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}}
—the union of coadjoint orbits of maximal dimension. Fix a maximal torus
T
and an open Weyl chamber
\mathfrak{c}
in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. Further, let
N(\mathfrak{c})
be the normalizer of
\mathfrak{c}
in
G
. It follows from the second and third structure theorem and the splitting theorem (see Example
4.31
) that compact, connected, regular, multiplicity-free Hamiltonian
G
-spaces are classified up to isomorphism by triples of:

•

a closed normal subgroup
K
of
N(\mathfrak{c})
that is contained in
T
,

•

an
N(\mathfrak{c})
-invariant polytope
\Delta
in
\mathfrak{c}
that is Delzant as polytope in an integral affine subspace of
(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})
parallel to
((\mathfrak{t}/\mathfrak{k})^{*},\Lambda_{T/K}^{*})
,

•

an element of
I^{1}(N(\mathfrak{c})/K,T/K)
.

Whilst for connected
G
Examples
5
and
6
are a simple case of the existing classification of all multiplicity-free Hamiltonian actions of such Lie groups
[
12
,
13
,
48
,
22
]
(since in that case
N(\mathfrak{c})=T
), for disconnected
G
it seems that there are no such classifications in the literature.

Finally, let us mention that the main theorems above readily generalize to quasi-Hamiltonian actions of regular, proper, twisted pre-symplectic groupoids (in the sense of
[
50
,
5
]
), essentially because every regular twisted pre-symplectic groupoid is Morita equivalent to a regular (non-twisted) symplectic groupoid. In particular, for any compact Lie group
G
, this applies to a class of quasi-Hamiltonian
G
-spaces (in the sense of
[
1
]
) for which the momentum map image is contained in the regular part of
G
. This provides some new insight into the classification problem for multiplicity-free quasi-Hamiltonian
G
-spaces, for in the current results on this
[
23
]
the Lie group
G
is assumed to be simply-connected.

Brief outline:
In Part 1 we introduce Delzant subspaces and prove that the image of the momentum map of a faithful multiplicity-free Hamiltonian action is such a subspace (Theorem
2
). The classification for the case of symplectic torus bundles (Theorem
1
) is proved in Part 2. In Part
3
we prove the first structure theorem and the splitting theorem (Theorem
3
and Theorem
4
) and in Part 4 we introduce the ext-invariant and the ext-sheaf, and prove the second and third structure theorems (Theorem
5
and Theorem
6
). Apart from the section on the third structure theorem, Part 4 can be read independently of Part 3. More detailed outlines can be found at the beginning of each of these parts.

The latter has surely helped me to improve the exposition. Furthermore, I would like to thank Rui Loja Fernandes for sharing a private note on proper isotropic fibrations with me. That short note contains a statement similar to the outcome of the first structure theorem above when applied to the particular case of Example
4
, which was a source of inspiration for the statement of the first structure theorem. This work was supported by NWO (Vici Grant no. 639.033.312) and the Max Planck Institute for Mathematics.

Conventions:
Throughout, we require smooth manifolds (with or without corners) to be both Hausdorff and second countable and we require the same for both the base and the space of arrows of a Lie groupoid (with or without corners). Furthermore, given a groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows X
we use the notation
\underline{X}:=X/\mathcal{G}
for its orbit space and we denote subsets of
\underline{X}
as
\underline{A}
, where
A
denotes the corresponding invariant subset of
X
.

1.
Delzant subspaces and the momentum map image

This part concerns the properties of the image of the momentum map and its relation to those of the Hamiltonian action.

In Section
1.1
we formulate and prove linear versions of the classification results in Example
3
and Example
5
, which illustrate our classification results in a particularly simple case. Moreover, these form the foundation for the construction of the ext-invariant and the proof of Theorem
5
, given in Part 4. In Section
1.2
we introduce Delzant subspaces, present some of their basic features and recall the necessary background on integral affine orbifolds. This can be read independently from Section
1.1
, except for the fact that some of the terminology on linear integral affine geometry used in Section
1.2
is introduced in Subsection
1.1.3
. Lastly, in Section
1.3
we prove Theorem
2
and Proposition
1.43
, which connect the objects studied in Section
1.1
to those in Section
1.2
and explain how to read off some invariants of the Hamiltonian action from the momentum map image.

1.1.
Classification of maximally toric representations

1.1.1.
Statement of the classification theorem

Let us first introduce some terminology.

Definition 1.1
.

By a
symplectic representation

(V,\omega)
of a Lie group
H
we mean finite-dimensional real symplectic vector space
(V,\omega)
together with a morphism
H\to\textrm{Sp}(V,\omega)
into the Lie group of linear symplectic automorphisms of
(V,\omega)
.

For any such representation
(V,\omega)
, the
H
-action is Hamiltonian with equivariant momentum map:

(16)

J_{V}:(V,\omega)\to\mathfrak{h}^{*},\quad\langle J_{V}(v),\xi\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\omega(\xi\cdot v,v).

Definition 1.2
.

We call a Lie group
H

infinitesimally abelian
if its Lie algebra is abelian. Given an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group
H
with identity component
T
, we call a symplectic representation
H\to\textrm{Sp}(V,\omega)

maximally toric
if the induced torus representation
T\to\textrm{Sp}(V,\omega)
is faithful and
\text{dim}(V)=2\text{dim}(T)
.

The representations in this definition are linear versions of the Hamiltonian group actions considered in Example
3
and Example
5
. The theorem below is a linear version of the classification result in Example
5
. It will also be important later for understanding the local properties of general faithful multiplicity-free actions.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
H
be an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group with identity component
T
and let
\varGamma:=H/T
be the group of connected components of
H
. There is a canonical bijection:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\text{Isomorphism classes of\text{ }}\\
&\text{ maximally toric $H$-representations }\end{aligned}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &\text{ \hskip 10.22217pt\hskip 10.22217ptSmooth \& $\varGamma$-invariant }\\
&\text{ pointed polyhedral cones in $(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})$ }\end{aligned}\right\}\times I^{1}(H,T)

defined by sending the class of such an
H
-representation
(V,\omega)
to the pair consisting of the image
\Delta_{V}
of its momentum map (
16
) and its ext-class
\textrm{e}(V,\omega)
(see Definition
1.13
).

In this section we explain and prove this statement, which is a modest extension of a well-known result for representations of tori (Proposition
1.9
recalled below). First, we elaborate on the set
I^{1}(H,T)
. Notice that
T
is a torus and the quotient
{\varGamma}=H/T
is a finite group. Furthermore, we have a canonical short exact sequence of Lie groups:

(17)

1\to T\to H\to\varGamma\to 1,

via which we view
H
as an extension of
\varGamma
by the abelian group
T
and we view
T
both as an
H
-module and as a
\varGamma
-module, equipped with the actions by conjugation in
H
. We denote by
H^{1}(H,T)
the degree one group cohomology of the
H
-module
T
, represented throughout this section as the abelian group of maps
{c}:H\to T
satisfying the cocycle condition:

{c}(h_{1}h_{2})=({c}(h_{1})\cdot h_{2}){c}(h_{2}),\quad h_{1},h_{2}\in H,

modulo the subgroup of those
{c}:H\to T
for which there is a
t\in T
such that
{c}
is given by:

{c}(h)=(t\cdot h)t^{-1},\quad h\in H.

Definition 1.4
.

Let
H
be an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group with identity component
T
. We let
I^{1}(H,T)
denote the subset of
H^{1}(H,T)
consisting of cohomology classes whose representatives restrict to the identity map on
T
.

Remark 1.5
.

If
I^{1}(H,T)
is non-empty, then it is naturally a torsor with abelian structure group
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
(the degree one group cohomology), which makes it amenable to computation in explicit examples. Here, the action is defined by assigning to
[\kappa]\in H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
and
[{c}]\in I^{1}(H,T)
the class
[\kappa]\cdot[{c}]
represented by the
1
-cocycle
H\to T
that maps
h\in H
to
\kappa([h]){c}(h)\in T
.

The set
I^{1}(H,T)
is non-empty if and only if
H
is split (by Proposition
1.11
), in the sense below.

Definition 1.6
.

We will say that an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group
H
is
split
if the short exact sequence of groups (
17
) admits a right-splitting.

In Subsection
1.1.4
we will prove the proposition below, which is a linear version of the forward implication in the Theorem
4
. In view of this proposition, the statement of Theorem
1.3
is trivially true when
H
is not split, since in this case both sets in the bijection are empty.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
H
be an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group. If
H
admits a maximally toric representation, then it is split.

The proof of this will reveal the group cohomology class (
28
). In the coming subsections we will explain the remaining parts of the statement and give a proof of Theorem
1.3
.

1.1.2.
Isomorphism versus equivalence of symplectic representations

Throughout, we will use the following notions of equivalence between symplectic representations.

Definition 1.8
.

An
isomorphism
of symplectic
H
-representations is an
H
-equivariant symplectic linear isomorphism. Given two Lie groups
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, by an
equivalence
between a symplectic
H_{1}
-representation
(V_{1},\omega_{1})
and a symplectic
H_{2}
-representation
(V_{2},\omega_{2})
we mean a pair of maps:

(18)

(\varphi,\psi):(H_{1},(V_{1},\omega_{1}))\to(H_{2},(V_{2},\omega_{2}))

consisting of an isomorphism of Lie groups
\varphi:H_{1}\to H_{2}
and a symplectic linear isomorphism
\psi:(V_{1},\omega_{1})\to(V_{2},\omega_{2})
that are compatible with the actions, in the sense that:

\varphi(h)\cdot\psi(v)=\psi(h\cdot v),\quad h\in H_{1},\text{ }v\in V_{1}.

Notice that the momentum map (
16
) is an invariant of a symplectic representation, in the sense that an equivalence (
18
) induces an identification:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{1},\omega_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{2},\omega_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{V_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{V_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}

1.1.3.
Maximally toric representations of tori

If an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group is connected, then it is simply a torus. In this case, Theorem
1.3
boils down to:

Proposition 1.9

(
[
12
]
)
.

Let
T
be an
n
-dimensional torus. Then:

a)

A symplectic representation of
T
is maximally toric if and only if its weight-tuple forms a basis of the character lattice
\Lambda_{T}^{*}
in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
.

b)

The map that associates to each unordered basis
\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}\}
of
\Lambda_{T}^{*}
the polyhedral cone generated by
(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
is a bijection from the set of such
n
-tuples to the set of smooth pointed polyhedral cones in
(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})
.

Therefore we have a bijection:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\text{ Isomorphism classes of }\\
&\text{ {maximally} toric $T$-representations }\end{aligned}\right\}\longleftrightarrow\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\text{ Smooth pointed}\\
&\text{ polyhedral cones in }(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T}^{*})\text{ }\end{aligned}\right\}

that associates to such a
T
-representation
(V,\omega)
the image
\Delta_{V}
of the momentum map
(\ref{quadsympmommap})
.

Let us briefly recall the meaning of the various notions appearing in this statement. The character lattice
\Lambda_{T}^{*}
of
T
in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
is the dual of the full rank lattice
\Lambda_{T}:=\textrm{Ker}(\exp_{T})
in
\mathfrak{t}
. This gives
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
the structure of an
integral affine vector space
, by which we mean a pair
(V,\Lambda)
consisting of a finite-dimensional real vector space
V
and a full rank lattice
\Lambda
in
V
. Any such lattice
\Lambda
admits a
basis
(that is, a basis of
V
consisting of vectors that
\mathbb{Z}
-span
\Lambda
).

Recall that symplectic
T
-representations are classified by their weight-tuples, as follows. Associated to
\alpha\in\Lambda_{T}^{*}
is the irreducible symplectic representation
T\to\textrm{Sp}(\mathbb{C},\omega_{\textrm{st}})
defined via the character:

(19)

\chi_{\alpha}:T\to\mathbb{S}^{1},\quad\chi_{\alpha}(\exp_{T}(\xi))=e^{2\pi i\langle\alpha,\xi\rangle},

and the
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-action on
\mathbb{C}
by complex multiplication. Here
(\mathbb{C},\omega_{\textrm{st}})
is viewed as real vector space equipped with the standard linear symplectic form:

\omega_{\textrm{st}}(w,z)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}(w\overline{z}-\overline{w}z)\in\mathbb{R},\quad w,z\in\mathbb{C}.

We denote this symplectic
T
-representation associated to
\alpha\in\Lambda_{T}^{*}
as:

(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha},\omega_{\textrm{st}}).

The
weight-tuple
of a symplectic
T
-representation
(V,\omega)
is the unique unordered tuple
(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
such that:

(20)

(V,\omega)\cong(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\oplus...\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{n}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})

as symplectic
T
-representation (for details on this, see for instance the appendices of
[
27
,
21
]
). This explains the terminology in part
a
of the proposition. Turning to part
b
, by a
polyhedral cone
in a finite-dimensional real vector space
V
, we mean a subset of the form:

\textrm{Cone}(v_{1},...,v_{k}):=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}s_{i}v_{i}\mid s_{i}\geq 0\right\},

with
v_{1},...,v_{k}\in V
. The tuple
(v_{1},...,v_{k})
is said to
generate
the polyhedral cone. We call a polyhedral cone
pointed
if it does not contain a line through the origin in
V
. Finally, we call a polyhedral cone
C
in an integral affine vector space
(V,\Lambda)

smooth
if there is a basis
\{v_{1},...,v_{n}\}
of the lattice
\Lambda
in
V
and a
k\leq n
such that:

C=\textrm{Cone}(v_{1},...,v_{k},v_{k+1},-v_{k+1},...,v_{n},-v_{n}).

This explains the terminology in part
b
. For the remainder, notice that under an identification of symplectic
T
-representations (
20
) the momentum map
J_{V}
is identified with:

(21)

J_{V}(z_{1},...,z_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|z_{i}|^{2}\alpha_{i},

where
z_{i}
denotes the standard complex linear coordinate on
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{i}}
. Therefore, the image
\Delta_{V}
of
J_{V}
is the polyhedral cone in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
generated by the weight-tuple
(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
, which is smooth and pointed if the symplectic
T
-representation is maximally toric.

Remark 1.10
.

Let
H
be an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group with identity component
T
and let
(V,\omega)
be a symplectic
H
-representation. For future reference, let us point out that the representation
H\to\textrm{Sp}(V,\omega)
is maximally toric if and only if the map of Lie groups induced by the weights of the induced representation
T\to\textrm{Sp}(V,\omega)
:

(22)

(\chi_{\alpha_{1}},...,\chi_{\alpha_{n}}):T\to\mathbb{T}^{n}

is an isomorphism.

1.1.4.
Splittings of abelian group extensions

The main point of this subsection will be to prove Proposition
1.7
. It will be useful to have some alternative descriptions of right splittings of abelian group extensions. Let
A
and
K
be groups and suppose that
A
is abelian. Recall the following.

•

An extension of
K
by
A
is a short exact sequence of groups:

1\to A\to G\to K\to 1.

When the maps are clear, we simply refer to
G
as an extension of
K
by
A
. Given any such extension, conjugation yields an action of
G
on
A
by group automorphisms, which descends to an action of
K
on
A
.

•

A morphism of two extensions of
K
by
A
:

\displaystyle 1\to A\to G_{1}\to K\to 1

\displaystyle 1\to A\to G_{2}\to K\to 1

is a morphism of groups
\varphi:G_{1}\to G_{2}
that makes the diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

commute. By a version of the five-lemma, any such morphism
\varphi
must be an isomorphism.

•

A right splitting
\sigma:K\to G
of an extension as above is a group homomorphism that is a section of the map
G\to K
.

•

Given an action of
K
on
A
by group automorphisms, the semi-direct product
K\ltimes A
is canonically an extension of
K
by
A
.

•

Given an extension as above, a
1
-cocycle (with respect to the right action of
G
on
A
by conjugation) is a map
{c}:G\to A
satisfying:

{c}(g_{1}g_{2})=({c}(g_{1})\cdot g_{2}){c}(g_{2}),\quad g_{1},g_{2}\in G.

We can now give the desired characterizations of right splittings.

Proposition 1.11
.

Consider an extension

1\to A\to G\to K\to 1

of a group
K
by an abelian group
A
. There are natural bijections between:

i)

The set of right splittings
K\to G
.

ii)

The set of isomorphisms of extensions:

K\ltimes A\to G,

where
K
acts on
A
via conjugation in
G
.

iii)

The set of
1
-cocycles
G\to A
that restrict to the identity map on
A
.

iv)

The set of subgroups
K_{G}
in
G
such that the composite
K_{G}\hookrightarrow G\to K
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We will define a square of maps:
(i)
(ii)
(iv)
(iii)

Firstly, given a right splitting
\sigma:K\to G
, the corresponding isomorphism of extensions is:

(23)

K\ltimes A\to G,\quad(k,a)\mapsto\sigma(k)a.

Secondly, given an isomorphism of extensions
\varphi:K\ltimes A\to G
, the corresponding
1
-cocycle is the composition:

G\xrightarrow{\varphi^{-1}}K\ltimes A\xrightarrow{\textrm{pr}_{A}}A.

Thirdly, given a
1
-cocycle
{c}:G\to A
that restricts to the identity map on
A
, the subset:

(24)

K_{{c}}:=\{g\in G\mid{c}(g)=1\}

is a subgroup of
G
and, because
{c}
restricts to the identity map on
A
, the composite
K_{{c}}\hookrightarrow G\to K
is an isomorphism. Finally, given a subgroup
K_{G}
of
G
with the property that the composite
K_{G}\hookrightarrow G\to K
is an isomorphism, the inverse to this map yields the corresponding right splitting:

K\to K_{G}\hookrightarrow G,

which completes the square. We leave it to the reader to verify that each of the four maps in this triangle is inverse to the composition by the other three, so as to complete the proof.
∎

With this and the lemma below, we are ready to prove Proposition
1.7
.

Lemma 1.12
.

A maximally toric representation of a torus has a unique decomposition into irreducible symplectic subrepresentations.

Proof.

The existence of such a decomposition holds for all symplectic representations of tori. Now suppose
T\to\text{Sp}(V,\omega)
is a maximally toric representation of an
n
-dimensional torus. Let
V=V_{\alpha_{1}}\oplus...\oplus V_{\alpha_{n}}
be a decomposition into irreducible symplectic subrepresentations indexed by the corresponding weights
(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
. By Proposition
1.9
a
the weights are linearly independent. So, since after identifying
V_{\alpha_{i}}
with
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{i}}
as symplectic
T
-representations the momentum map is given by (
21
), each subspace
V_{\alpha_{i}}
coincides with
(J_{V})^{-1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}\cdot\alpha_{i})
. Hence, the decomposition is indeed unique.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.7
.

Let
H\to\text{Sp}(V,\omega)
be a maximally toric representation. After a choice of isomorphism of symplectic
T
-representations, we can assume that:

(25)

(V,\omega)=(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\oplus...\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{n}},\omega_{\textrm{st}}),

as symplectic
T
-representations. Let
h\in H
. The pair of maps:

(C_{h},m_{h}):(T,(V,\omega))\to(T,(V,\omega)),

consisting of conjugation and multiplication by
h
, is a self-equivalence of the induced symplectic representation of
T
on
(V,\omega)
. Therefore,
[h]\in\varGamma
(which acts on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
via the coadjoint
H
-action) permutes the weights
(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
and, by uniqueness of the decomposition into irreducibles, it follows that
h
acts as a symplectic
\mathbb{R}
-linear map
\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{i}}\to\mathbb{C}_{[h]\cdot\alpha_{i}}
for each
i
. Since
J_{V}
is given by (
21
) and is
H
-equivariant, any
h
maps the unit circle
(J_{V})^{-1}(\alpha_{i})
to the unit circle
(J_{V})^{-1}([h]\cdot\alpha_{i})
, and it follows that
h:\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{i}}\to\mathbb{C}_{[h]\cdot\alpha_{i}}
acts by a rotation determined by an element
h_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. So, we can associate to every
h\in H
an element
(h_{1},...,h_{n})\in\mathbb{T}^{n}
. Via the isomorphism (
22
) we obtain a map
{c}:H\to T
. This is a
1
-cocycle for the extension (
17
) that restricts to the identity map on
T
. So, in view of Proposition
1.11
,
H
is split.
∎

1.1.5.
The classification of maximally toric representations

We will now address the associated group cohomology class appearing in Theorem
1.3
. Let
(V,\omega)
be a maximally toric
H
-representation. As in the proof of Proposition
1.7
, the choice of an isomorphism of symplectic
T
-representations:

(26)

\psi:(V,\omega)\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\oplus...\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{n}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})

induces a
1
-cocycle
{c}_{\psi}:H\to T
, determined by the fact that for each
h\in H
,
v\in V
and each
\alpha_{i}
:

(27)

\chi_{\alpha_{i}}({c}_{\psi}(h))\cdot\psi(v)_{\alpha_{i}}=\psi(h\cdot v)_{[h]\cdot\alpha_{i}}.

This
1
-cocycle restricts to the identity map on
T
and for different choices of
\psi
the resulting cocycles are cohomologous. Hence, the class:

(28)

e(V,\omega):=[{c}_{\psi}]\in I^{1}(H,T)

is independent of the choice of
\psi
.

Definition 1.13
.

We call (
28
) the
ext-class
of the maximally toric
H
-representation
(V,\omega)
.

In fact, this class is the same for any two maximally toric representations that are isomorphic as symplectic
H
-representations.
This explains the remaining part of the statement of Theorem
1.3
. We now turn to its proof.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

For injectivity, let
(V_{1},\omega_{1})
and
(V_{2},\omega_{2})
be maximally toric
H
-representation such that
\Delta_{V_{1}}=\Delta_{V_{2}}
and
e(V_{1},\omega_{1})=e(V_{2},\omega_{2})
. Then since
\Delta_{V_{1}}=\Delta_{V_{2}}
, it follows from Proposition
1.9
that the two symplectic representations have the same weight-tuple, say
(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
. Hence, there are isomorphisms of symplectic
T
-representations:

(V_{1},\omega_{1})\xrightarrow{\psi_{1}}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\oplus...\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{n}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\xleftarrow{\psi_{2}}(V_{2},\omega_{2}).

Since
e(V_{1},\omega_{1})=e(V_{2},\omega_{2})
, there is a
t\in T
such that for each
h\in H
:

(29)

{c}_{\psi_{1}}(h)=(t\cdot h)t^{-1}{c}_{\psi_{2}}(h).

Consider:

\psi_{t}:(V_{1},\omega_{1})\to(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\oplus...\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{n}},\omega_{\textrm{st}}),\quad\psi_{t}(v)=t^{-1}\cdot\psi_{1}(v),

which is again an isomorphism of symplectic
T
-representations. As one readily verifies, it follows from (
29
) that
\psi_{2}^{-1}\circ\psi_{t}:(V_{1},\omega_{1})\to(V_{2},\omega_{2})
is
H
-equivariant, and hence it is an isomorphism of symplectic
H
-representations. This proves injectivity.

For surjectivity, let
\Delta
be a
\varGamma
-invariant and smooth pointed polyhedral cone in
(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T})
, and let
{c}:H\to T
be a
1
-cocycle that restricts to the identity on
T
. Let
(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
be a tuple that generates
\Delta
and forms a basis of
\Lambda_{T}^{*}
. Consider the maximally toric
T
-representation:

(V,\omega):=(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\oplus...\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{n}},\omega_{\textrm{st}}).

Since
\varGamma
leaves both
\Delta
and
\Lambda_{T}^{*}
invariant, by Proposition
1.9
b
it must permute the ordered tuple
(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n})
. This induces an action of
\varGamma
on
V
by permuting the components indexed by this tuple. Using this, the
T
-representation extends to a representation
r:\varGamma\ltimes T\to\textrm{Sp}(\mathbb{C}^{n},\omega_{\textrm{st}})
by setting
(\gamma,t)\cdot z=\gamma\cdot(t\cdot z)
. By construction, this representation is maximally toric and has momentum image
\Delta
. Next, consider the map:

\varphi_{{c}}:H\to\varGamma\ltimes T,\quad\varphi_{{c}}(h)=([h],{c}(h)).

This is the isomorphism of extensions of
\varGamma
by
T
corresponding to
{c}
via the bijection in Proposition
1.11
. Composing
\varphi_{{c}}
with
r
, we obtain a maximally toric
H
-representation for which
\Delta
is the momentum image. Moreover, the cohomology class associated to this is represented by the
1
-cocycle
{c}_{\psi}
where we may pick
\psi
to be the identity map on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Using (
27
), this
1
-cocycle is readily seen to coincide with the given
1
-cocycle
{c}
. So we have constructed a maximally toric
H
-representation with momentum image
\Delta
and associated group cohomology class
[{c}]
.
∎

Remark 1.14
.

The analogue of Example
3
holds as well in this linear setting: by Theorem
1.3
and Remark
1.5
the set of isomorphism of class of maximally toric
H
-representations with momentum image a prescribed
\Delta
is a torsor with structure group
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
, provided
H
is split, and (using the lemma below) it is readily verified that this action can be described more explicitly, as follows. Consider the isomorphism between the abelian group of
1
-cocycles
{c}:\varGamma\to T
and the group of automorphisms of the extension (
17
) (with group structure given by composition of maps), that associates to a
1
-cocycle
{c}
the automorphism:

\varphi_{{c}}:H\to H,\quad h\mapsto h{c}([h]).

This descends to an isomorphism between
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
and the group of automorphisms of (
17
) modulo the subgroup of automorphisms given by conjugation by elements of
T
. Now, the
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
-action on the set of isomorphism classes of maximally toric
H
-representations is given by:

[{c}]\cdot[(V,\omega)]:=[(V_{{c}},\omega)],

where
(V_{{c}},\omega)
is equal to
(V,\omega)
as symplectic vector space, but equipped with the linear symplectic action given by
h\cdot_{{c}}v=\varphi_{{c}}(h)\cdot v
, for
h\in H
and
v\in V
, where the right-hand dot denotes the original action of
H
on
V
.

In the above remark we referred to:

Lemma 1.15
.

Suppose that we are given an equivalence of maximally toric representations of infinitesimally abelian compact Lie groups:

(\varphi,\psi):(H_{1},(V_{1},\omega_{1}))\to(H_{2},(V_{2},\omega_{2})).

Then the induced isomorphism:

\varphi_{*}:I^{1}(H_{1},T_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}I^{1}(H_{2},T_{2})

sends
e(V_{1},\omega_{1})
to
e(V_{2},\omega_{2})
.

This lemma is straightforward to verify. It will also be useful for later reference.

1.2.
Delzant subspaces of integral affine orbifolds

1.2.1.
Background on orbifolds

Following
[
7
]
, we use the terminology below for orbifolds.

•

An
orbifold groupoid
is a proper foliation groupoid (that is, a proper Lie groupoid with discrete isotropy groups).

•

By an
orbifold atlas
on a topological space
B
we mean an orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows M
, together with a homeomorphism
p
between the orbit space
\underline{M}
and
B
.

•

By an
orbifold
we mean a pair consisting of a topological space
B
together with an orbifold atlas
(\mathcal{B},p)
on
B
.

•

We call two orbifold atlases on
B

equivalent
if there is a Morita equivalence between the given orbifold groupoids that intertwines the respective homeomorphisms between their orbit spaces and
B
.

This approach to orbifolds (using groupoids instead of atlases of charts) is in the spirit of
[
34
]
.

1.2.2.
Background on integral affine structures on orbifolds

An integral affine atlas on a manifold
M
is one for which the coordinate changes are (restrictions of) integral affine transformations of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. A maximal such atlas is called an
integral affine structure
on
M
. Such a structure can be encoded globally, as follows. Given a vector bundle
E\to M
, a smooth lattice in
E
is a subbundle
\Lambda
with the property that, for each
x_{0}\in M
, there is a local frame
e
of
E
defined on an open neighbourhood
U
of
x_{0}
, such that for all
x\in U
:

\Lambda_{x}=\mathbb{Z}(e_{1})_{x}\oplus...\oplus\mathbb{Z}(e_{n})_{x}.

In particular,
\Lambda_{x}
is a full rank lattice in
E_{x}
for each
x\in M
. The data of an integral affine structure on
M
is equivalent to that of a smooth lattice
\Lambda
in the cotangent bundle
T^{*}M
satisfying the integrability condition that
\Lambda
is locally spanned by closed
1
-forms, or equivalently, that
\Lambda
is Lagrangian as submanifold of
(T^{*}M,\Omega_{\textrm{can}})
. The corresponding lattice is locally spanned by the coframe associated to any integral affine chart. See
[
7
]
for more details.

Example 1.16
.

The
n
-torus
\mathbb{T}^{n}
has an integral affine structure with lattice
\mathbb{Z}\textrm{d}\theta_{1}\oplus...\oplus\mathbb{Z}\textrm{d}\theta_{n}
.

Remark 1.17
.

Besides the
2
-torus, the only other compact surface that admits an integral affine structure is the Klein bottle
[
2
,
31
]
and the various such structures on these have been classified
[
32
,
43
]
. There are also classification results for integral affine structures on three-dimensional compact manifolds
[
24
]
.

The global description is well-suited for a generalization of integral affine structures to orbifolds. First of all, the notion of vector bundle generalizes: a vector bundle over an orbifold
(B,\mathcal{B},p)
is a representation of
\mathcal{B}
, meaning that it is a vector bundle
E\to M
(in the sense of manifolds) equipped with a fiberwise linear action of
\mathcal{B}
. For example, the tangent bundle of an orbifold
(B,\mathcal{B},p)
is the canonical representation of
\mathcal{B}
on the normal bundle
\mathcal{N}\mathcal{F}
to the foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
M
by connected components of the orbits of
\mathcal{B}
. Explicitly, this representation is given by:

(30)

g\cdot[v]=[\textrm{d}t(\widehat{v})],\quad g\in\mathcal{B},\quad v\in\mathcal{N}_{s(g)}\mathcal{F},

where
\widehat{v}\in T_{g}\mathcal{B}
is any choice of tangent vector such that
\textrm{d}s(\widehat{v})=v
. If
\mathcal{B}
is source-connected, then this coincides with the linear holonomy representation. The cotangent bundle of the orbifold is the dual representation of
\mathcal{B}
on the co-normal bundle:

\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}=T\mathcal{F}^{0}\subset T^{*}M.

Now, the definition of an integral affine structure generalizes to orbifolds, as follows.

Definition 1.18
.

An integral affine structure on an orbifold
(B,\mathcal{B},p)
is a smooth lattice
\Lambda
in
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
with the property that
\Lambda
is Lagrangian as submanifold of
(T^{*}M,\Omega_{\textrm{can}})
and
\Lambda
is invariant with respect to the co-normal representation of
\mathcal{B}
. We call
(B,\mathcal{B},p,\Lambda)
an
integral affine orbifold
.

Example 1.19
.

For a proper action of a countable discrete group
\Gamma
on a manifold
M
, the data of an integral affine structure on the orbit space
M/\Gamma
(viewed as orbifold with orbifold groupoid the action groupoid
\Gamma\ltimes M
) is that of a
\Gamma
-invariant integral affine structure on
M
.

Example 1.20
.

Given a foliated manifold
(M,\mathcal{F})
, the data of a smooth Lagrangian lattice in
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
is the same as that of a transverse integral affine structure on
(M,\mathcal{F})
. If an orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows M
is source-connected, then every smooth Lagrangian lattice in
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
is automatically
\mathcal{B}
-invariant, so that in this case the data of an integral affine structure on
(B,\mathcal{B},p)
is simply that of a transverse integral affine structure on the associated foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
M
.

1.2.3.
Background on the integral affine orbifold associated to a regular proper symplectic groupoid

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid. Let
\mathcal{F}
denote the foliation of
M
by connected components of the
\mathcal{G}
-orbits, and let
\underline{M}:=M/\mathcal{G}
denote the orbit space of
\mathcal{G}
. It follows from
[
35
, Proposition 2.5]
that there is a canonical short exact sequence of Lie groupoids over
M
:

(31)

1\to\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{B}\to 1

where
\mathcal{T}
is the bundle of Lie groups with fiber
\mathcal{T}_{x}
the identity component of the isotropy group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
of
\mathcal{G}
at
x\in M
, and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is an orbifold groupoid over
M
. The fibers of
\mathcal{T}
are in fact tori and the orbifold
(\underline{M},\mathcal{B},\textrm{Id}_{\underline{M}})
comes with a natural integral affine structure. To see this, recall first that, as for any symplectic groupoid, the conormal space
\mathcal{N}^{*}_{x}\mathcal{F}
at
x\in M
can be canonically identified with the isotropy Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
of
\mathcal{G}
at
x
via the isomorphism of Lie algebroids:

(32)

\rho_{\Omega}:T_{\pi}^{*}M\to\text{Lie}(\mathcal{G}),\quad\iota_{\rho_{\Omega}(\alpha)}\Omega_{1_{x}}=(\textrm{d}t_{1_{x}})^{*}\alpha,\quad\alpha\in T^{*}_{x}M,\text{ }x\in M,

where
T^{*}_{\pi}M
denotes the cotangent bundle equipped with the Lie algebroid structure associated to the Poisson structure
\pi
on
M
induced by
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
. Since the Poisson structure
\pi
is regular, its isotropy Lie algebras are abelian. Hence, so are the isotropy Lie algebras of
\mathcal{G}
. Since
\mathcal{G}
is proper, its isotropy groups are compact. Therefore,
\mathcal{T}
is a bundle of tori and the kernel of each exponential map
\mathfrak{g}_{x}\to\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}=\mathcal{T}_{x}
determines a full rank lattice
\Lambda_{x}
in
\mathcal{N}^{*}_{x}\mathcal{F}
. All together, this yields a map of Lie groupoids:

(33)

\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{T}

with kernel the desired smooth lattice
\Lambda
in
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
.

Remark 1.21
.

The map (
33
) factors through an isomorphism of Lie groupoids:

(34)

\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}:=\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}/\Lambda\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{T},

and the co-normal representation of
\mathcal{B}
on
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
descends to an action of
\mathcal{B}
on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
, which under the above isomorphism is identified with the action of
\mathcal{B}
on
\mathcal{T}
by conjugation. The symplectic form
\Omega
on
\mathcal{G}
restricts to a pre-symplectic form
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
on
\mathcal{T}
, which makes:

(35)

(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})\to M

into a pre-symplectic torus bundle. On the other hand, the canonical symplectic form on
T^{*}M
restricts to a pre-symplectic form on
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
, which in turn descends to a pre-symplectic form
\Omega_{\Lambda}
on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
. The map (
34
) identifies
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
with
\Omega_{\Lambda}
. So, (
35
) is fully encoded by the integral affine orbifold associated to
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
.

1.2.4.
Delzant subspaces

We define Delzant subspaces of integral affine manifolds as follows.

Definition 1.22
.

A
Delzant subspace
\Delta
of an integral affine manifold

(M,\Lambda)
is a subset of
M
with the property that for every
x\in\Delta
and every (or equivalently some) integral affine chart
(U,\chi)
around
x
into
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, there is a smooth polyhedral cone
C^{\chi}_{x}(\Delta)
in
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{Z}^{n})
(in the sense of Subsection
1.1.3
) such that the germ of
\chi(U\cap\Delta)
at
\chi(x)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is that of
\chi(x)+C^{\chi}_{x}(\Delta)
at
\chi(x)
.

As mentioned in the introduction: Delzant subspaces are embedded submanifolds with corners of codimension zero (as in Definition
B.11
), Delzant polytopes are examples of Delzant subspaces, and so are integral affine manifolds (being Delzant subspaces of themselves). Below we give a few other examples.

Example 1.23
.

Let
(M,\Lambda):=(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{Z}\textrm{d}x\oplus\mathbb{Z}\textrm{d}\theta)
—a cylinder equipped with its standard integral affine structure. The cylinder with boundary
\Delta:=[0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a Delzant subspace.

Example 1.24
.

Let
(M,\Lambda):=(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{Z}(\textrm{d}x+(\theta-\frac{1}{2})\textrm{d}\theta)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\textrm{d}\theta)
—a cylinder equipped with a non-standard integral affine structure. The subspace:

\Delta:=\{(x,e^{2\pi i\theta})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\mid x+\theta(\theta-1)/2\geq 0,\text{ }\theta\in[0,1[\}

is a Delzant subspace with a single vertex and a single stratum of codimension one (see Figure
1
).

Figure 1.
A sketch of the boundary
\partial\Delta
(coloured in green) of the Delzant subspace in Example
1.24
.

To extend this definition to orbifolds (in the sense of Subsection
1.2.1
) it is convenient to have a coordinate-free description of Delzant subspaces of integral affine manifolds. To this end, notice that around each point in an integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
there is one natural choice of integral affine ‘chart’. More precisely, around each
x\in M
there is unique map germ:

\log_{x}\in\textrm{Germ}_{x}(M;T_{x}M)

induced by an integral affine isomorphism
\iota
from
(U,\Lambda)
onto an open in
(T_{x}M,\Lambda_{x}^{*})
, that maps
x
to the origin in
T_{x}M
and the derivative of which at
x
is the identity map on
T_{x}M
. Given a subset
\Delta
of
M
and an
x\in\Delta
, there is an associated set germ
\log_{x}(\Delta)
at the origin in
T_{x}M
, defined as the germ of
\iota(U\cap\Delta)
at the origin, which is independent of the choice of
\iota
as above. Now,
\Delta
is a Delzant subspace of
(M,\Lambda)
if and only if for every
x\in\Delta
the set germ
\log_{x}(\Delta)
is the germ of a smooth polyhedral cone in the integral affine vector space
(T_{x}M,\Lambda_{x}^{*})
(in the sense of Subsection
1.1.3
). To define Delzant subspaces of integral affine orbifolds we will now generalize this characterization, starting with:

Proposition 1.25
.

Let
(M,\mathcal{F},\Lambda)
be a foliated manifold with a transverse integral affine structure and let
x\in M
. There is a unique map germ:

\log_{x}\in\textrm{Germ}_{x}(M;\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F})

induced by a submersion
\nu
defined on an open
U
around
x
in
M
, with the following properties.

i)

The tangent distribution to the fibers of
\nu
coincides with that of the foliation
\mathcal{F}
over
U
.

ii)

It maps
x
to the origin in
\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
and its differential at
x
is the projection
T_{x}M\to\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
.

iii)

It is compatible with the integral affine structure, in the sense that for each
y\in U
:

\textrm{d}\nu_{y}:(\mathcal{N}_{y}\mathcal{F},\Lambda_{y}^{*})\to(\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F},\Lambda_{x}^{*})

is an isomorphism of integral affine vector spaces.

Proof.

First, we prove existence. Since
\Lambda
is a smooth lattice in
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
, we can choose a local frame
\alpha
of
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
, defined on an open
U
around
x
, such that:

\Lambda|_{U}=\mathbb{Z}\text{ }\alpha_{1}\oplus...\oplus\mathbb{Z}\text{ }\alpha_{n}.

Since
\Lambda
is Lagrangian in
T^{*}M
, all of its local sections are closed
1
-forms. So, by the Poincaré Lemma, we can (after shrinking
U
) arrange that
\alpha_{i}=\textrm{d}f_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in C^{\infty}(U)
such that
f_{i}(x)=0
. Consider:

f=(f_{1},...,f_{n}):U\to\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Then
(\underline{\textrm{d}f})_{x}:\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a linear isomorphism, so that we can define

\nu=(\underline{\textrm{d}f})_{x}^{-1}\circ f:U\to\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}.

As is readily verified, this has the desired properties. To prove uniqueness, let
\nu_{1}:U_{1}\to\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
and
\nu_{2}:U_{2}\to\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
be two submersions as above. Since both
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
satisfy property i), we can find an open neighbourhood
U
of
x
in
U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, together with a connected transversal
\Sigma
to
\mathcal{F}
through
x
, with the property that:

•

\Sigma
is contained in
U
and every leaf of the foliation on
U
induced by
\mathcal{F}
intersects
\Sigma
,

•

both
\nu_{1}|_{\Sigma}
and
\nu_{2}|_{\Sigma}
are open embeddings into
\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
.

The transversal
\Sigma
inherits an honest integral affine structure
\Lambda_{\Sigma}
from the transverse integral affine structure
\Lambda
, and by property iii) both
\nu_{1}|_{\Sigma}
and
\nu_{2}|_{\Sigma}
are isomorphisms of integral affine manifolds onto their image in
(\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F},\Lambda_{x}^{*})
with respect to
\Lambda_{\Sigma}
. Therefore,
\nu_{1}|_{\Sigma}\circ(\nu_{2}|_{\Sigma})^{-1}
is a morphism of integral affine manifolds between connected opens in the integral affine vector space
(\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F},\Lambda_{x}^{*})
. By the lemma below, this means that it must be the restriction of an integral affine transformation of
(\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F},\Lambda_{x}^{*})
. So, it is determined by its value and its derivative at the origin in
\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
. By property ii),
\nu_{1}|_{\Sigma}\circ(\nu_{2}|_{\Sigma})^{-1}
fixes the origin and its derivative at the origin is the identity map. Hence,
\nu_{1}|_{\Sigma}\circ(\nu_{2}|_{\Sigma})^{-1}
must be the restriction of the identity map on
\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
to
\nu_{2}(\Sigma)
, so that
\nu_{1}|_{\Sigma}=\nu_{2}|_{\Sigma}
. It follows from this and property i) that in fact
\nu_{1}|_{U}=\nu_{2}|_{U}
, because every leaf of the foliation on
U
induced by
\mathcal{F}
intersects
\Sigma
. So,
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
have the same germ at
x
, as was to be shown.
∎

Lemma 1.26
.

Let
(V_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
(V_{2},\Lambda_{2})
be integral affine vector spaces. Then every morphism of integral affine manifolds from a connected open in
(V_{1},\Lambda_{1})
into
(V_{2},\Lambda_{2})
is of the form
v\mapsto Av+b
for some linear map
A:V_{1}\to V_{2}
that maps
\Lambda_{1}
into
\Lambda_{2}
and some
b\in V_{2}
.

Proof.

After a choosing bases for
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
we can assume that
(V_{1},\Lambda_{1})=(\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}},\mathbb{Z}^{n_{1}})
and
(V_{2},\Lambda_{2})=(\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}},\mathbb{Z}^{n_{2}})
. Let
f
be a morphism of integral affine manifolds from a connected open
U_{1}
in
(\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}},\mathbb{Z}^{n_{1}})
into
(\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}},\mathbb{Z}^{n_{2}})
, meaning that its partial derivatives take values in
\mathbb{Z}
. Since
U_{1}
is connected and
\mathbb{Z}
is a discrete subspace of
\mathbb{R}
, the Jacobian of
f
must be constant. Fix a
u_{1}\in U_{1}
. Since any two points in
U_{1}
can be connected to
u_{1}
by a smooth path, it follows by integrating along such paths that
f(v)=A(v)+b
for all
v\in U_{1}
, where
A
is the constant value of the Jacobian of
f
and
b=f(u_{1})-A(u_{1})
.
∎

Now, let
(M,\mathcal{F},\Lambda)
be a foliated manifold with a transverse integral affine structure, let
\Delta
be a subset of
M
and let
x\in\Delta
. Then, as for integral affine manifolds, there is an associated set germ
\log_{x}(\Delta)
at the origin in
\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
. To define this, let us call a submersion
\nu:U\to\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
representing
\log_{x}

\Delta
-adapted
if:

\nu^{-1}(\nu(U\cap\Delta))=U\cap\Delta.

The set-germ of
\nu(U\cap\Delta)
at the origin in
\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
is independent of the choice of
\Delta
-adapted submersion
\nu:U\to\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
representing
\log_{x}
. Moreover, if
\Delta
is
\mathcal{F}
-invariant, we can always find a small enough open
U
around
x
in
M
that admits a
\Delta
-adapted submersion
\nu:U\to\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
representing
\log_{x}
. Therefore, it makes sense to define:

Definition 1.27
.

Let
(M,\mathcal{F},\Lambda)
be a foliated manifold with a transverse integral affine structure, let
\Delta
be an
\mathcal{F}
-invariant subset of
M
and let
x\in\Delta
. We define
\log_{x}(\Delta)
to be the set germ of
\nu(U\cap\Delta)
at the origin in
\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
, for any
\Delta
-adapted submersion
\nu:U\to\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
representing
\log_{x}
.

We are now ready to define Delzant subspaces of integral affine orbifolds.

Definition 1.28
.

Let
(B,\mathcal{B},p,\Lambda)
be an integral affine orbifold. A
Delzant subspace

\underline{\Delta}
is a subset of
B
with the property that for every
x\in\Delta
(the corresponding invariant subset of
M
), the set germ
\log_{x}(\Delta)
is the germ of a smooth polyhedral cone in the integral affine vector space
(\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F},\Lambda_{x}^{*})
(in the sense of Subsection
1.1.3
). For each
x\in\Delta
, we denote this polyhedral cone in
\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
(which is necessarily unique) by
C_{x}(\underline{\Delta})
and call it the
cone of
\underline{\Delta}
at
x
.

Remark 1.29
.

Let
(B,\mathcal{B},p,\Lambda)
be an integral affine orbifold and let
\underline{\Delta}
be a Delzant subspace. Then
\Delta
is an embedded submanifold with corners of
M
of codimension zero (as in Definition
B.11
), with tangent cone
C_{x}(\Delta)
the pre-image of
C_{x}(\underline{\Delta})
under the projection
T_{x}M\to\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{F}
. The restriction
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
is a Lie groupoid with corners (as in Definition
B.18
; cf. Example
B.14
).
If
\underline{M}
is the orbit space of a regular and proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
(equipped with the associated integral affine orbifold structure), then
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
is a symplectic groupoid with corners (as in Definition
B.22
).

Example 1.30
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows(M,\Lambda)
be an etale integral affine orbifold groupoid. For any
\mathcal{B}
-invariant Delzant subspace
\Delta
of the integral affine manifold
(M,\Lambda)
(in the sense of Definition
1.22
), the quotient
\underline{\Delta}
is a Delzant subspace of
\underline{M}
. In fact, any Delzant subspace of
\underline{M}
is of this form.

Example 1.31
.

Let
G
be a compact Lie group. Consider the open and dense subset
\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
consisting of coadjoint
G
-orbits of maximal dimension and consider the regular and proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega):=(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
over
M:=\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}}
(the restriction of the cotangent symplectic groupoid of
G
). Fix a maximal torus
T
in
G
and let
\mathfrak{c}
be the interior of a Weyl chamber in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. As is common, we identify
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
with the
T
-fixed point set in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Let
N(\mathfrak{c})
be the normalizer of
\mathfrak{c}
in
G
. Delzant subspaces of
\underline{M}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}}/G
are naturally in bijection with Delzant subspaces of the integral affine vector space
(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T})
that are contained in
\mathfrak{c}
and that are invariant with respect to the natural action of the finite group
N(\mathfrak{c})/T
on
\mathfrak{c}
. If
G
is connected, then
N(\mathfrak{c})=T
, so that the invariance condition becomes vacuous. A conceptual explanation for this correspondence will be given in Example
1.42
, using Corollary
1.40
below.

We think of a Delzant subspace as what should be an integral affine suborbifold with corners, without making this precise. In particular, these objects should be well-behaved with respect to equivalences of the ambient integral affine orbifold that respect the integral affine structure. The latter we will make precise, for besides its conceptual value it will be of use throughout.

Definition 1.32
.

By an
integral affine orbifold groupoid

\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows(M,\Lambda)
we mean an orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows M
with an integral affine structure
\Lambda
on the orbifold (
\underline{M},\mathcal{B},\textrm{Id}_{\underline{M}}
) (its orbit space). We call a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}
of this integral affine orbifold simply a Delzant subspace of
\underline{M}
.

Definition 1.33
.

By an
integral affine Morita equivalence
between integral affine orbifold groupoids
