\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightrightarrows(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}\rightrightarrows(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})
we mean a Morita equivalence:
\mathcal{B}_{1}
M_{1}
P
M_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{2}
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

with the additional property that
\alpha_{1}^{*}(\Lambda_{1})=\alpha_{2}^{*}(\Lambda_{2})
as subbundles of
\alpha_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{1})=\alpha_{2}^{*}(\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{2})
.

Remark 1.34
.

A Morita equivalence as above is integral affine if and only if for each
p\in P
, writing
x_{1}=\alpha_{1}(p)
and
x_{2}=\alpha_{2}(p)
, the induced linear isomorphism:

(36)

\psi_{p}:\mathcal{N}_{x_{1}}\mathcal{F}_{1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{N}_{x_{2}}\mathcal{F}_{2},\quad[v]\mapsto[\textrm{d}\alpha_{2}(\widehat{v})],

where
\widehat{v}\in T_{p}P
is any tangent vector with the property that
\textrm{d}\alpha_{1}(\widehat{v})=v
, is an isomorphism of integral affine vector spaces:

\psi_{p}:(\mathcal{N}_{x_{1}}\mathcal{F}_{1},(\Lambda_{1})^{*}_{x_{1}})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{N}_{x_{2}}\mathcal{F}_{2},(\Lambda_{2})^{*}_{x_{2}}).

In particular, for each such
p\in P
there is an induced isomorphism of tori:

(\psi_{p})_{*}:(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{1}})_{x_{1}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{2}})_{x_{2}}.

Remark 1.35
.

Given a Morita equivalence between orbifold groupoids
\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightrightarrows M_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}\rightrightarrows M_{2}
, and an integral affine structure
\Lambda_{1}
on the orbifold
(\underline{M}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{1},\textrm{Id}_{\underline{M}_{1}})
, there is a unique integral affine structure
\Lambda_{2}
on the orbifold
(\underline{M}_{2},\mathcal{B}_{2},\textrm{Id}_{\underline{M}_{2}})
with respect to which the given Morita equivalence becomes integral affine.

Example 1.36
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows(M,\Lambda)
be an integral affine orbifold groupoid and
\Sigma
a transversal for
\mathcal{B}
(by which we mean a transversal to the foliation
\mathcal{F}
by connected components of
\mathcal{B}
-orbits, of complementary dimension). There is a canonical Morita equivalence:
\mathcal{B}|_{\widehat{\Sigma}}
\widehat{\Sigma}
s_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\Sigma)
\Sigma
\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma}
\text{ }\text{ }t_{\mathcal{B}}
s_{\mathcal{B}}

where
\widehat{\Sigma}:=t(s^{-1}(\Sigma))
denotes the saturation of
\Sigma
with respect to
\mathcal{B}
(which is open in
M
). The manifold
\Sigma
inherits an honest integral affine structure
\Lambda_{\Sigma}
from
\Lambda
,
\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma}\rightrightarrows(\Sigma,\Lambda_{\Sigma})
is an etale integral affine orbifold groupoid and the above Morita equivalence becomes integral affine.

Example 1.37
.

Let
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})\rightrightarrows M_{1}
and
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})\rightrightarrows M_{2}
be regular and proper symplectic groupoids and let
\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightrightarrows(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}\rightrightarrows(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})
be the associated integral affine orbifold groupoids (as in the previous subsection). A symplectic Morita equivalence:
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
M_{1}
(P,\omega_{P})
M_{2}
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

induces an integral affine Morita equivalence:
\mathcal{B}_{1}
(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})
\underline{P}
(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})
\mathcal{B}_{2}
\text{ }\text{ }\underline{\alpha}_{1}
\underline{\alpha}_{2}

where
\underline{P}=P/\mathcal{T}_{1}=P/\mathcal{T}_{2}
. To see this, let
p\in P
and denote
x_{1}=\alpha_{1}(p)
and
x_{2}=\alpha_{2}(p)
. The given Morita equivalence induces an isomorphism of Lie groups:

(37)

\varphi_{p}:(\mathcal{G}_{1})_{x_{1}}\to(\mathcal{G}_{2})_{x_{2}},

uniquely determined by the property that for each
g\in(\mathcal{G}_{1})_{x_{1}}
:

g\cdot p=p\cdot\varphi_{p}(g).

Since
\varphi_{p}
is an isomorphism of Lie groups, it identifies the identity component of
(\mathcal{G}_{1})_{x_{1}}
with the identity component of
(\mathcal{G}_{2})_{x_{2}}
. In other words, it identifies
(\mathcal{T}_{1})_{x_{1}}
with
(\mathcal{T}_{2})_{x_{2}}
and hence it follows that the
\mathcal{T}_{1}
-orbit through
p
coincides with the
\mathcal{T}_{2}
-orbit through
p
. This shows that
P/\mathcal{T}_{1}=P/\mathcal{T}_{2}
. From the lemma below, it is clear that the induced Morita equivalence is integral affine.

Lemma 1.38
.

Suppose that we are given a symplectic Morita equivalence:
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
M_{1}
(P,\omega_{P})
M_{2}
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

For each
p\in P
, writing
x_{1}=\alpha_{1}(p)
and
x_{2}=\alpha_{2}(p)
, we have a commutative square:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{x_{2}}{\mathcal{O}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{x_{1}}{\mathcal{O}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathfrak{g}_{2})^{*}_{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathfrak{g}_{1})^{*}_{x_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{p}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\Omega_{2}}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\varphi_{p}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\Omega_{1}}^{*}}

with horizontal arrows defined as in (
36
), respectively (
37
), and vertical arrows defined as in (
32
).

Proof.

Notice (by dualizing and unravelling the definition of the upper horizontal map) that we ought to prove the commutativity of the diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{p}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{x_{1}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{N}_{x_{2}}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathfrak{g}_{1})_{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathfrak{g}_{2})_{x_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textrm{d}\alpha_{1})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textrm{d}\alpha_{2})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\rho_{\Omega_{1}})^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{p})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\rho_{\Omega_{2}})^{-1}}

First notice that for all
\xi\in(\mathfrak{g}_{1})_{x_{1}}
:

(38)

\exp(\xi)\cdot p=p\cdot\exp((\varphi_{p})_{*}(\xi)).

Now consider the respective Lie algebroid actions:

a_{L}:\alpha_{1}^{*}(T^{*}M_{1})\to TP\quad\&\quad a_{R}:\alpha_{2}^{*}(T^{*}M_{2})\to TP

induced by the
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
-action via
\rho_{\Omega_{1}}
and by the
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
-action via
\rho_{\Omega_{2}}
. By definition of
a_{L}
and
a_{R}
, for every
\xi\in(\mathfrak{g}_{1})_{x_{1}}
and
\eta\in(\mathfrak{g}_{2})_{x_{2}}
we have:

a_{L}\left((\rho_{\Omega_{1}})^{-1}(\xi)\right)=\left.\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}\exp(t\xi)\cdot p\quad\&\quad a_{R}\left((\rho_{\Omega_{2}})^{-1}(\eta)\right)=\left.\frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}\right|_{t=0}p\cdot\exp(-t\eta),

which combined with (
38
) gives:

(39)

a_{L}\left((\rho_{\Omega_{1}})^{-1}(\xi)\right)=-a_{R}\left((\rho_{\Omega_{2}})^{-1}((\varphi_{p})^{*}(\xi))\right).

Since the (left)
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
-action and the (right)
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
-action are Hamiltonian,
a_{L}
and
a_{R}
satisfy the momentum map condition. This means that for all
\beta_{1}\in\Omega^{1}(M_{1})
and
\beta_{2}\in\Omega^{1}(M_{2})
:

\iota_{a_{L}(\beta_{1})}\omega_{P}=\alpha_{1}^{*}(\beta_{1})\quad\&\quad\iota_{a_{R}(\beta_{2})}\omega_{P}=-\alpha_{2}^{*}(\beta_{2}).

Combined with (
39
) this implies the desired commutativity, which concludes the proof.
∎

Below we give a precise meaning to the statement that Delzant subspaces are well-behaved with respect to integral affine Morita equivalences of the ambient integral affine orbifold.

Proposition 1.39
.

Suppose that we are given an integral affine Morita equivalence:
\mathcal{B}_{1}
M_{1}
P
M_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{2}
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

that relates a given subset
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
of
\underline{M}_{1}
to a subset
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
of
\underline{M}_{2}
. Then for each
p\in P
such that
x_{1}:=\alpha_{1}(p)\in\Delta_{1}
, and
x_{2}:=\alpha_{2}(p)\in\Delta_{2}
, it holds that:

\psi_{p}(\log_{x_{1}}(\Delta_{1}))=\log_{x_{2}}(\Delta_{2}),

where
\psi_{p}
is defined as in (
36
).

Corollary 1.40
.

In the setting of Proposition
1.39
,
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
is a Delzant subspace of
\underline{M}_{1}
if and only if
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
is a Delzant subspace of
\underline{M}_{2}
. In this case, for each
p\in P
such that
x_{1}:=\alpha_{1}(p)\in\Delta_{1}
, and
x_{2}:=\alpha_{2}(p)\in\Delta_{2}
, their cones at
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are related as:

\psi_{p}(C_{x_{1}}(\underline{\Delta}_{1}))=C_{x_{2}}(\underline{\Delta}_{2}),

where
\psi_{p}
is defined as in (
36
).

Proof of Proposition
1.39
.

First notice that the respective foliations
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
pull back along
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
to the same foliation
\mathcal{F}
on
P
. Moreover, since the Morita equivalence is integral affine, the smooth lattices
\Lambda_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}
pull back to the same smooth lattice
\Lambda
in
\mathcal{N}^{*}\mathcal{F}
. One readily verifies that this lattice
\Lambda
is Lagrangian in
(T^{*}P,\Omega_{\textrm{can}})
. So, it defines a transverse integral affine structure on the foliated manifold
(P,\mathcal{F})
. Being related by the Morita equivalence, the respective invariant subsets
\Delta_{1}
in
M_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
in
M_{2}
, corresponding to
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
and
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
, have the same pre-image
\Delta
in
P
under
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
. Let
p\in\Delta
. Notice that, to prove the proposition, it is enough to show that for both
i\in\{1,2\}
the linear isomorphism
(\underline{\textrm{d}\alpha}_{i})_{p}:\mathcal{N}_{p}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{F}_{i}
identifies the set germ
\log_{p}(\Delta)
with the set-germ
\log_{x_{i}}(\Delta_{i})
. To see that this is indeed the case, observe that if
\nu_{i}:U_{i}\to\mathcal{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{F}_{i}
is a
\Delta_{i}
-adapted submersion as in Proposition
1.25
, with respect to
(M_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i},\Lambda_{i})
, then the composition:

\alpha_{i}^{-1}(U_{i})\xrightarrow{\nu_{i}\circ\alpha_{i}}\mathcal{N}_{x_{i}}\mathcal{F}_{i}\xrightarrow{(\underline{\textrm{d}\alpha}_{i})^{-1}_{p}}\mathcal{N}_{p}\mathcal{F}

is a
\Delta
-adapted submersion as in Proposition
1.25
, with respect to
(P,\mathcal{F},\Lambda)
.
∎

Corollary 1.41
.

Let
(B,\mathcal{B},p,\Lambda)
be an integral affine orbifold and let
\underline{\Delta}
be a Delzant subspace. For each
x\in\Delta
the cone
C_{x}(\underline{\Delta})
of
\underline{\Delta}
at
x
is
\mathcal{B}_{x}
-invariant.

Proof of Corollary
1.41
.

Apply Corollary
1.40
to the identity equivalence.
∎

Example 1.42
.

The description of the Delzant subspaces in Example
1.31
can be obtained as follows. Recall that
\mathfrak{c}
is a complete transversal for the foliation of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}}
by coadjoint
G^{0}
-orbits, where
G^{0}
denotes the identity component of
G
. The transverse integral affine structure
\Lambda_{\mathcal{G}}
induced by
(\mathcal{G},\Omega):=(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
restricts to the same integral affine structure on
\mathfrak{c}
as that inherited from the integral affine vector space
(\mathfrak{t}^{*},\Lambda_{T})
(of which
\mathfrak{c}
is an open subset). Moreover, the restriction of the orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
to
\mathfrak{c}
is canonically isomorphic to the action groupoid
N(\mathfrak{c})/T\ltimes\mathfrak{c}
(cf.
[
15
, Proposition 3.15.1]
). So, via Example
1.35
we obtain an integral affine Morita equivalence:
\mathcal{B}
(\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}},\Lambda_{\mathcal{G}})
s_{\mathcal{B}}^{-1}(\mathfrak{c})
(\mathfrak{c},\Lambda_{T})
N(\mathfrak{c})/T\ltimes\mathfrak{c}

Applying Corollary
1.40
to this leads to the bijection in Example
1.31
.

1.3.
On the image of the momentum map

1.3.1.
The symplectic normal representations and the momentum image

The objects in the previous two sections are related via the proposition below, which we prove in the next subsection along with Theorem
2
.

Proposition 1.43
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid and let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a faithful multiplicity-free Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space. Further, let
p\in S
and
x:=J(p)
.

a)

The symplectic normal representation
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})
at
p
is maximally toric (Definition
1.2
).

b)

The inclusion of the isotropy group
\mathcal{G}_{p}
of the action into the isotropy group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
of
\mathcal{G}
induces an isomorphism between their groups of connected components:

(40)

\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{p}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}.

c)

The cone at
x
of
\underline{\Delta}:=\underline{J}(\underline{S})
(which is a Delzant subpace by Theorem
2
) is given by:

(41)

C_{x}(\underline{\Delta})=(\rho_{\Omega})_{x}^{*}(\pi_{\mathfrak{g}_{p}^{*}}^{-1}(\Delta_{J_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p}}})),

where:

–

(\rho_{\Omega})_{x}:\mathcal{N}_{x}^{*}\mathcal{F}\to\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is defined as in (
32
),

–

\pi_{\mathfrak{g}_{p}^{*}}:\mathfrak{g}_{x}^{*}\to\mathfrak{g}_{p}^{*}
denotes the canonical projection (dual to the inclusion
\mathfrak{g}_{p}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}_{x}
),

–

J_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p}}:(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})\to\mathfrak{g}_{p}^{*}
denotes the quadratic momentum map of the symplectic normal representation at
p
, as defined in (
16
), and
\Delta_{J_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p}}}
denotes its image.

Recall here that, given a Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
, for each
p\in S
there is a naturally associated symplectic representation:

(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})\in\textrm{SympRep}(\mathcal{G}_{p}),

of the isotropy group
\mathcal{G}_{p}
of the action at
p
. We call this the
symplectic normal representation
at
p
. Explicitly, this consists of the symplectic normal space to the
\mathcal{G}
-orbit
\mathcal{O}\subset(S,\omega)
through
p
:

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p}:=\frac{T_{p}\mathcal{O}^{\omega}}{T_{p}\mathcal{O}\cap T_{p}\mathcal{O}^{\omega}},

equipped with the linear symplectic form induced by
\omega
and with the
\mathcal{G}_{p}
-action:

g\cdot[v]=[\textrm{d}m_{(g,p)}(0,v)],\quad g\in\mathcal{G}_{p},\quad v\in T_{p}\mathcal{O}^{\omega},

where
m:\mathcal{G}\ltimes S\to S
denotes the action map. Here
T_{p}\mathcal{O}^{\omega}
denotes the
\omega
-orthogonal to the tangent space
T_{p}\mathcal{O}
of the orbit at
p
. This generalizes the so-called symplectic normal (or slice) representations for Hamiltonian Lie group actions. For further details on this definition in the generality of symplectic groupoid actions we refer to
[
38
]
.

Remark 1.44
.

As for symplectic toric manifolds, there is a natural way to read off the isomorphism type of the isotropy groups (and so, also the canonical stratification of the orbit space) of a faithful multiplicity-free Hamiltonian action from its momentum image. Indeed, in view of Proposition
1.7
and Proposition
1.43
, the isotropy group
\mathcal{G}_{p}
at
p\in S
is isomorphic to the semi-direct product of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
with the
\Gamma_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
-invariant subtorus of
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda})_{x}
with Lie algebra given by the annihilator in
T^{*}_{x}M
of the face of the polyhedral cone
C_{x}(\underline{\Delta})
through the origin.

1.3.2.
Proofs of Theorem
2
and Proposition
1.43

For torus actions, Theorem
2
and Proposition
1.43
boil down to the known facts below about symplectic toric manifolds (which follow from an argument using the Marle-Guillemin-Sternberg normal form; see
[
12
]
and
[
21
, Lemma B.3]
).

Proposition 1.45
.

Let
T
be a torus and let
J:(S,\omega)\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
be a possibly non-compact and non-connected symplectic toric manifold for which
J
descends to a topological embedding
S/T\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. Let
p\in S
and
x=J(p)
.

a)

The symplectic normal representation
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})
at
p
is maximally toric.

b)

The isotropy group
T_{p}
of the
T
-action is connected.

c)

The germ at
x
of the image of
J
is equal to that of the subset
x+C_{x}
, where
C_{x}
is the pre-image of the polyhedral cone
\Delta_{J_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p}}}\subset\mathfrak{t}_{p}^{*}
under the canonical projection
\mathfrak{t}^{*}\to\mathfrak{t}_{p}^{*}
.

Our strategy of proof will be to reduce to this case. For this we will use:

Lemma 1.46
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows M
be a proper etale Lie groupoid. Every
x\in M
admits a connected open neighbourhood
U
such that for each
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}_{x}
there exists a (necessarily unique) smooth local section
\sigma_{\gamma}:U\to\mathcal{B}|_{U}
of the source-map that sends
x
to
\gamma
, and such that the map:

\mathcal{B}_{x}\times U\to\mathcal{B}|_{U},\quad(\gamma,y)\mapsto\sigma_{\gamma}(y)

is surjective. This defines an isomorphism of Lie groupoids between the action groupoid
\mathcal{B}_{x}\ltimes U
of the
\mathcal{B}_{x}
-action on
U
given by
\gamma\cdot y:=t(\sigma_{\gamma}(y))
and
\mathcal{B}|_{U}
. In particular,
\mathcal{B}|_{U}
is source-proper.

Proof.

See
[
36
, Proposition 5.30]
.
∎

Lemma 1.47
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be regular and proper symplectic groupoid and let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space. If the associated orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale, then
J:(S,\omega)\to M
is a faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-space with respect to the induced
\mathcal{T}
-action.

Proof.

Note that if
\mathcal{B}
is etale, then
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
is symplectic. To see that the induced Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-action is faithful toric, the only thing to show is that the map
\underline{J}_{\mathcal{T}}:S/{\mathcal{T}}\to M
is a topological embedding. This map clearly being a continuous injection, it remains to show that
\underline{J}_{\mathcal{T}}:S/{\mathcal{T}}\to\Delta
is closed, or equivalently, that
J:S\to\Delta
is closed. For this, it suffices to show that every
x\in\Delta
admits an open neighbourhood
U
in
\Delta
such that
J:J^{-1}(U)\to U
is closed. Let
x\in\Delta
, take
U_{M}
to be an open in
M
around
x
as Lemma
1.46
and let
U=U_{M}\cap\Delta
. Then
\mathcal{B}|_{U_{M}}
is source-proper, hence so is
\mathcal{G}|_{U_{M}}
and so is the action groupoid of the restriction of the
\mathcal{G}
-action to
U_{M}
. Therefore, the quotient map
q_{S}:S\to\underline{S}
restricts to a proper map from
J^{-1}(U)
onto its image in
\underline{S}
. Combined with the fact that
\underline{J}:\underline{S}\to\underline{M}
is a topological embedding, it follows that
J:J^{-1}(U)\to U
is proper. Because any continuous proper map into a first-countable and Hausdorff space is closed (see e.g.
[
41
]
), we conclude that
J^{-1}(U)\to U
is indeed closed.
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof.

End of the proof of Theorem
2
and Proposition
1.43
.

First suppose that the orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale, so that the integral affine structure
\Lambda
induced by
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is an honest integral affine structure on
M
and
(by Lemma
1.47
)
J:(S,\omega)\to M
is a faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-space with respect to the induced action. Let
p\in S
,
x:=J(p)
and fix an integral affine isomorphism
\iota
from an open
U
in
(M,\Lambda)
into an open
V
in
(T_{x}M,\Lambda^{*}_{x})
that maps
x
to the origin and the derivative of which at
x
is the identity map on
T_{x}M
. This induces an isomorphism of symplectic torus bundles:

(42)

(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})|_{U}\xrightarrow{(\ref{exptorbun2})}(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})|_{U}\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}(T\ltimes\mathfrak{t}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})|_{V},\quad T:=(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda})_{x}.

Consider the Hamiltonian
(T\ltimes\mathfrak{t}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})|_{V}
-action along
\iota\circ J:(J^{-1}(U),\omega)\to V
corresponding to the restriction to
U
of the Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-action along
J
. The data of such a Hamiltonian action is the same as that of a Hamiltonian
T
-action with momentum map:

(43)

\iota\circ J:(J^{-1}(U),\omega)\to T_{x}M=\mathfrak{t}^{*}.

Since
J
is a faithful multiplicity-free Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space, (
43
) is a Hamiltonian
T
-space as in Proposition
1.45
. Further notice that
T_{p}=\mathcal{G}_{p}\cap\mathcal{T}_{x}
and that the symplectic normal representation at
p
of the Hamiltonian
T
-space (
43
) coincides with the symplectic
T_{p}
-representation induced by the symplectic normal representation
\mathcal{G}_{p}\to\textrm{Sp}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})
of the
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J
. So, it follows from parts
a
and
b
of Proposition
1.45
that the symplectic normal representation at
p
of the
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J
is maximally toric as well and that
\mathcal{G}_{p}\cap\mathcal{T}_{x}
is connected. The latter means that
\mathcal{G}_{p}\cap\mathcal{T}_{x}
is the identity component of
\mathcal{G}_{p}
, or in other words, (
40
) is injective. For surjectivity, note that given
g\in\mathcal{G}_{x}
, both
p
and
g^{-1}\cdot p
belong to the same fiber of
J
over
x
. So, since the
J
-fibers coincide with the
\mathcal{T}
-orbits, there is a
t\in\mathcal{T}_{x}
such that
g^{-1}\cdot p=t\cdot p
. Then
gt\in\mathcal{G}_{p}
, and
[gt]\in\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{p}}
is send to
[g]\in\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
. This proves parts
a
and
b
of Proposition
1.43
. From part
c
of Proposition
1.45
it follows that the germ of
\iota(U\cap\Delta)
at the origin in
T_{x}M
is that of the polyhedral cone
\pi_{\mathfrak{g}_{p}}^{-1}(\Delta_{J_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p}}})
, where
\Delta:=J(S)
. This polyhedral cone in
(T_{x}M,\Lambda_{x}^{*})
smooth, since the polyhedral cone
\Delta_{J_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p}}}
is smooth in
(\mathfrak{g}_{p}^{*},\Lambda_{T_{p}})
and
\mathfrak{g}_{p}^{0}\cap\Lambda_{x}
is a full-rank lattice in
\mathfrak{g}_{p}^{0}
(because
\mathfrak{g}_{p}
is the Lie algebra of a subtorus of
\mathcal{T}_{x}
). So, Theorem
2
and Proposition
1.43
hold when
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale.

When
\mathcal{B}
is not etale, we reduce to the etale case as follows. As before, let
p\in S
and
x:=J(p)
. Choose a transversal
\Sigma
for
\mathcal{B}
through
x
. Then
\Sigma
is a Poisson transversal in
(M,\pi)
, where
\pi
is the Poisson structure on
M
induced by
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
. Therefore, any Poisson map from a symplectic manifold into
(M,\pi)
is transversal to
\Sigma
and the pre-image of
\Sigma
under such a map is a symplectic submanifold of its domain. So,
J^{-1}(\Sigma)
is a symplectic submanifold of
(S,\omega)
and
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Sigma}
is a symplectic subgroupoid of
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
. The
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-action along
J
restricts to a Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Sigma}
-action along:

J_{\Sigma}:(J^{-1}(\Sigma),\omega|_{J^{-1}(\Sigma)})\to\Sigma.

This is again a faithful multiplicity-free Hamiltonian action and its symplectic normal representation at
p
is canonically isomorphic to that of the
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-action along
J
. The symplectic groupoids
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
and
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Sigma}
are Morita equivalent via a bibundle like that in Example
1.36
and the induced the integral affine Morita equivalence (Example
1.37
) is that in Example
1.36
. In view of Corollary
1.40
, since the orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma}
associated to
\mathcal{G}|_{\Sigma}
is etale, this reduces the proof of Theorem
2
and Proposition
1.43
to the etale case.
∎

2.
Classification in the case of symplectic torus bundle actions

In this part we complete the proof of Theorem
1
(the classification in the case of symplectic torus bundle actions), for which it remains to prove part
b
. One reason for treating this case separately is that the classification simplifies, whilst the most of the main ideas of the classification are already present. The orbifold structure plays no role in this case (it is trivial) and the three structure theorems boil down to one and the same theorem, that we prove in Section
2.1
. In Section
2.2
we complete the proof of Theorem
1
by showing that for any Delzant subspace of an integral affine manifold, there is a canonical faithful toric space with that Delzant subspace as the image of its momentum map. This construction (and its naturality, in particular) will be key as well in the proofs of Theorem
3
(the first structure theorem) and Theorem
4
(the splitting theorem) in the next part.

2.1.
The sheaf of Lagrangian sections and the structure theorem

2.1.1.
The sheaves of automorphisms and Lagrangian sections

In this subsection we explain and prove the theorem below, which will be key for the proof of Theorem
1
b
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
be a symplectic torus bundle and let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space with associated Delzant subspace
\Delta:=J(S)
. There is an isomorphism of sheaves on
\Delta
—induced by the map (
45
) below —between the sheaf
\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J,\omega)
of automorphisms of the
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
and the sheaf
\mathcal{L}
of Lagrangian sections of
\mathcal{T}|_{\Delta}
(as in Definition
2.2
below).

The sheaf of Lagrangian sections referred to here and in Theorem
1
is defined as follows.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)\to M
be a symplectic torus bundle with induced integral affine structure
\Lambda
on
M
and let
\Delta
a Delzant subspace of
(M,\Lambda)
. We denote by:

•

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})
the sheaf on
\Delta
of smooth sections of the bundle
\mathcal{T}|_{\Delta}\to\Delta
(where smooth is meant as maps between manifolds with corners),

•

\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
the subsheaf of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})
consisting of those sections
{\tau}
that are Lagrangian, in the sense that
{\tau}^{*}\Omega=0
.

Remark 2.3
.

Given a submersion
f:M\to N
between manifolds without corners and an embedded submanifold with corners
\Delta
in
N
(see Definition
B.11
), a local section
\sigma:U\to f^{-1}(\Delta)
of
f
, defined on an open
U
in
\Delta
, is smooth as map of manifolds with corners if and only if for every
x\in U
there is a smooth local
section

U_{x}\to M
of
f
, defined on an open
U_{x}
in
N
around
x
, that coincides with
\sigma
on
U\cap U_{x}
(also see Remark
B.9
). This can be taken as a working definition.

Remark 2.4
.

The sheaf
\mathcal{L}
is a sheaf of
abelian
groups (with group structure induced by that on the fibers of
\mathcal{T}
). So, automorphisms of a faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space commute.

To explain the definition of the isomorphism in Theorem
2.1
, consider the sheaf of groups
\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J)
on
\Delta
that assigns to an open
U
in
\Delta
the group:

(44)

\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J)(U)

consisting of
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant diffeomorphisms:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{-1}(U)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}

The sheaf
\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J,\omega)
in Theorem
2.1
is the subsheaf of
\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J)
that assigns to such an open the subgroup of
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant
symplectomorphisms
. To relate these to the sheaves in Definition
2.2
, consider the map of sheaves (of groups) on
\Delta
:

(45)

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})\to\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J),\quad{\tau}\mapsto\psi_{{\tau}},

where, for
{\tau}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})(U)
, we define:

\psi_{{\tau}}:J^{-1}(U)\to J^{-1}(U),\quad\psi_{{\tau}}(p)={\tau}(J(p))\cdot p.

Notice that (because the
\mathcal{T}
-action is free on a dense subset):

Proposition 2.5
.

The map of sheaves (
45
) is injective.

Furthermore, we have:

Proposition 2.6
.

A section
{\tau}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})(U)
is Lagrangian if and only if
\psi_{{\tau}}
is a symplectomorphism.

Proof.

Using that the
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-action is Hamiltonian, we deduce:

\displaystyle(\psi_{{\tau}})^{*}\omega

\displaystyle=({\tau}\circ J,\textrm{id}_{S})^{*}(m_{S})^{*}\omega

\displaystyle=({\tau}\circ J,\textrm{id}_{S})^{*}\left((\textrm{pr}_{S})^{*}\omega+(\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}})^{*}\Omega\right)

\displaystyle=\omega+J^{*}({\tau}^{*}\Omega).

Therefore,
\psi_{{\tau}}
is a symplectomorphism if and only if
J^{*}({\tau}^{*}\Omega)=0
. If the
\mathcal{T}
-action is free at a point
p\in J^{-1}(U)
, then
J
is a submersion at
p
, so that
J^{*}({\tau}^{*}\Omega)_{p}=0
if and only if
({\tau}^{*}\Omega)_{J(p)}=0
. As the set of points where
\mathcal{T}
acts freely is dense in
S
, it follows from continuity that
J^{*}({\tau}^{*}\Omega)=0
if and only if
{\tau}^{*}\Omega=0
. This proves the proposition.
∎

In view of these propositions, the map (
45
) induces injective maps of sheaves:

(46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\to\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J),

(47)

\displaystyle\mathcal{\mathcal{L}}_{\Delta}

\displaystyle\to\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J,\omega).

Theorem
2.1
says that (
47
) is an isomorphism. To prove this, by Proposition
2.6
it suffices to show:

Theorem 2.7
.

The map (
46
) is an isomorphism of sheaves.

Proof of Theorem
2.7
(and hence of Theorem
2.1
).

It remains to show that (
46
) is stalk-wise surjective. Let
x\in\Delta
and let
\psi\in\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J)(U)
for some open
U
around
x
in
\Delta
. We have to show that there is a smooth section
{\tau}
of
\mathcal{T}|_{\Delta}
, defined on an open neighbourhood of
x
in
\Delta
, such that the germ of
\psi_{{\tau}}
at
x
coincides with that of
\psi
. The proof of this reduces to the case in which
M=\mathfrak{t}^{*}
,
x
is the origin and
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
is the symplectic torus bundle
(T\times\mathfrak{t}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
associated to a torus
T
, by using an isomorphism (
42
) as in the proof of Theorem
2
and Proposition
1.43
. In that case, the data of a faithful complexity
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
is the same as that of a (possibly non-compact and non-connected) symplectic toric
T
-manifold
(S,\omega)
with momentum map
J
that descends to a topological embedding
\underline{J}:S/T\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. By appealing to the standard normal form theorem for such Hamiltonian
T
-spaces (see e.g.
[
21
, Lemma B.3]
) and using that any
T
-invariant open in
S
is the pre-image under
J
of an open in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
(
\underline{J}
being a topological embedding), the proof further reduces to the subcase in which:

•

T=\mathbb{T}^{n}
(the standard torus),

•

S
is an open in
\mathbb{T}^{n-k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\times\mathbb{C}^{k}
of the form
\mathbb{T}^{n-k}\times B^{n-k}\times B^{k}
, where
B^{n-k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-k}
and
B^{k}\subset\mathbb{C}^{k}
are open balls of the same radius and centered around the respective origins, and
\mathbb{T}^{n-k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\times\mathbb{C}^{k}
is equipped with the standard symplectic form,

•

the
T
-action on
S
is given by:

(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{n})\cdot(t_{1},...,t_{n-k},x_{1},...,x_{n-k},z_{1},...,z_{k})=(\lambda_{k+1}t_{1},...,\lambda_{n}t_{n-k},x_{1},...,x_{n-k},\lambda_{1}z_{1},...,\lambda_{k}z_{k}),

•

J
is the restriction of the map:

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{n-k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k}\times\mathbb{C}^{k}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}^{n},

\displaystyle(t_{1},...,t_{n-k},x_{1},....,x_{n-k},z_{1},...,z_{k})

\displaystyle\mapsto(|z_{1}|^{2},...,|z_{k}|^{2},x_{1},...,x_{n-k}),

•

\psi
is a
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-equivariant self-diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{T}^{n-k}\times B^{n-k}\times B^{k}
such that
J\circ\psi=J
.

To conclude the proof we will show that there is map
{\tau}:\Delta\to\mathbb{T}^{n}
, defined on the image
\Delta
of
\mathbb{T}^{n-k}\times B^{n-k}\times B^{k}
under
J
, such that
\psi
is given by:

(48)

\psi(t,x,z)={\tau}(J(t,x,z))\cdot(t,x,z),\quad(t,x,z)\in\mathbb{T}^{n-k}\times B^{n-k}\times B^{k},

and such that
{\tau}
extends to a smooth map from an open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
into
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. For this we use a variation of the argument used to prove
[
12
, Lemma 2.6]
. Let
\varphi_{j}
and
\psi_{j}
denote the
j^{\textrm{th}}
component of
\psi
in
\mathbb{T}^{n-k}
and
\mathbb{C}^{k}
, respectively. Using equivariance of
\psi
and the fact that
\psi
preserves
J
, we find:

(49)

\psi(t,x,z)=(t_{1}\cdot\varphi_{1}(1,x,z),...,t_{n-k}\cdot\varphi_{n-k}(1,x,z),x,\psi_{1}(1,x,z),...,\psi_{k}(1,x,z)).

Using equivariance of
\psi
once more, it further follows that for all
(x,z)\in B^{n-k}\times B^{k}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{T}^{k}
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{j}(1,x,\lambda\cdot z)=\varphi_{j}(1,x,z),

\displaystyle\psi_{j}(1,x,z)=\lambda_{j}\psi_{j}(1,x,z).

In particular,
(x,u)\mapsto\psi_{j}(1,x,u)
restricts to a smooth function on
B^{n-k}\times(B^{k}\cap\textrm{Re}(\mathbb{C}^{k}))
, which is odd in the
u_{j}
variable and even in the other
u
-variables, while
(x,u)\mapsto\varphi_{j}(1,x,u)
restricts to a smooth function on this domain as well, but is even in all
u
-variables. Therefore, a theorem due to Whitney
[
47
]
(a particular case of Schwarz’ theorem for finite groups
[
3
,
42
]
) implies that there are continuous functions:

f_{j},g_{j}:\Delta\to\mathbb{C}

that satisfy:

\displaystyle\varphi_{j}(1,x,u)=f_{j}(u_{1}^{2},...,u_{k}^{2},x)

\displaystyle\psi_{j}(1,x,u)=u_{j}g_{j}(u_{1}^{2},...,u_{k}^{2},x),

for all
(x,u)\in B^{n-k}\times(B^{k}\cap\textrm{Re}(\mathbb{C}^{k}))
, and extend to smooth functions on some open neighbourhood of
\Delta
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The fact that
\psi
preserves
J
implies that:

u_{j}^{2}|g_{j}(u_{1}^{2},...,u_{k}^{2},x)|^{2}=|\psi_{j}(1,x,u)|^{2}=u_{j}^{2}

for all
(x,u)\in B^{n-k}\times(B^{k}\cap\textrm{Re}(\mathbb{C}^{k}))
. Therefore
g_{j}
takes values in
\mathbb{S}^{1}
on the interior of
\Delta
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, hence it must do so on all of
\Delta
by a density argument. Note that
f_{j}
does so as well, since
\varphi_{j}
does. Finally, observe that for
(x,z)\in B^{n-k}\times B^{k}
, writing
z_{j}=e^{i\theta_{j}}|z_{j}|
one finds:

(50)

\displaystyle\psi_{j}(1,x,z)

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta_{j}}\psi_{j}(1,x,|z_{1}|,...,|z_{k}|)

\displaystyle=z_{j}g_{j}(|z_{1}|^{2},...,|z_{k}|^{2},x),

by equivariance of
\psi
. Similarly:

(51)

\varphi_{j}(1,x,z)=f_{j}(|z_{1}|^{2},...,|z_{k}|^{2},x).

Now define:

f:\Delta\to\mathbb{T}^{n-k}\quad\&\quad g:\Delta\to\mathbb{T}^{k}

to have
j^{\textrm{th}}
component
f_{j}
and
g_{j}
, respectively, and consider:

{\tau}:=(g,f):\Delta\to\mathbb{T}^{k}\times\mathbb{T}^{n-k}.

Combining (
49
), (
50
) and (
51
) we find that (
48
) holds.
Moreover, by construction of
f
and
g
,
{\tau}
extends to a smooth map into
\mathbb{C}^{n}
on an open neighbourhood of
\Delta
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since none of its components vanish on a small enough such neighbourhood, by normalizing them we can find such an extension that maps smoothly into
\mathbb{T}^{n}
. So,
{\tau}
has the desired properties.
∎

2.1.2.
The structure theorem

For symplectic torus bundle actions the three structure theorems boil down to the statement below, which we prove in this subsection.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
be a symplectic torus bundle over
M
with induced integral affine structure
\Lambda
and let
\Delta
be a Delzant subspace of
(M,\Lambda)
. There is a natural free and transitive action of the group
H^{1}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})
(as in Theorem
1
) on the set of isomorphism classes of faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces with momentum image
\Delta
.

To prove Theorem
2.8
, we will first show the following.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
be a symplectic torus bundle and suppose that we are given faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces
J_{1}:(S_{1},\omega_{1})\to M
and
J_{2}:(S_{2},\omega_{2})\to M
. Let
x\in J_{1}(S_{1})\cap J_{2}(S_{2})
. Then there is an open neighbourhood
U
of
x
in
M
and a
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant symplectomorphism:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{1}^{-1}(U),\omega_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{2}^{-1}(U),\omega_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{2}}

if and only if the germs at
x
in
M
of
J_{1}(S_{1})
and
J_{2}(S_{2})
are equal.

For this we use the theorem below, which is a generalization of a well-known theorem due to Marle
[
29
]
and Guillemin and Sternberg
[
19
]
for Hamiltonian actions of compact Lie groups.

Theorem 2.10

(
[
38
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a proper symplectic groupoid. Suppose we are given two Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces
J_{1}:(S_{1},\omega_{1})\to M
and
J_{2}:(S_{2},\omega_{2})\to M
. Let
p_{1}\in S_{1}
and
p_{2}\in S_{2}
be such that
J_{1}(p_{1})=J_{2}(p_{2})
,
\mathcal{G}_{p_{1}}=\mathcal{G}_{p_{2}}
and
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{1}},\omega_{p_{1}})\cong(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{2}},\omega_{p_{2}})
are isomorphic symplectic representations. Then there are
\mathcal{G}
-invariant open neighbourhoods
U_{i}
of
p_{i}
in
S_{i}
and an equivariant symplectomorphism that sends
p_{1}
to
p_{2}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{1},\omega_{1},p_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{2},\omega_{2},p_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{2}}

For actions of symplectic torus bundles, Theorem
2.10
can be proved by reducing to the case of Hamiltonian torus actions via a trivialization of the symplectic torus bundle as in (
42
).

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

The implication from left to right is straightforward. For the other implication, let
x\in J_{1}(S_{1})\cap J_{2}(S_{2})
such that the germs at
x
of
J_{1}(S_{1})
and
J_{2}(S_{2})
coincide. Since
\underline{J}:\underline{S}\to M
is a topological embedding, every invariant
\mathcal{T}
-invariant open in
S
is of the form
J^{-1}(U)
where
U
is some open in
M
. So, in view of Theorem
2.10
, it remains to show that there are
p_{1}\in J_{1}^{-1}(x)
and
p_{2}\in J_{2}^{-1}(x)
such that
\mathcal{T}_{p_{1}}=\mathcal{T}_{p_{2}}
and the symplectic representations
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{1}},\omega_{p_{1}})
and
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{2}},\omega_{p_{2}})
are isomorphic. Fix any
p_{1}\in J_{1}^{-1}(x)
and
p_{2}\in J_{2}^{-1}(x)
. Since the germs at
x
of
J_{1}(S_{1})
and
J_{2}(S_{2})
coincide, Proposition
1.43
c
implies that:

(52)

(\pi_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{p_{1}}})^{-1}(\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{1}}})=(\pi_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{p_{2}}})^{-1}(\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{2}}}).

Since the symplectic normal representations at
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are maximally toric (Proposition
1.43
a
), the polyhedral cones
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{1}}}
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{2}}}
are pointed. So, (
52
) implies that
\textrm{Ker}(\pi_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{p_{1}}})=\textrm{Ker}(\pi_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{p_{2}}})
. Seeing as
\textrm{Ker}(\pi_{\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{p_{i}}})
is the annihilator of
\mathfrak{t}_{p_{i}}=\textrm{Lie}(\mathcal{T}_{p_{i}})
in
\mathfrak{t}_{x}^{*}
, this means that
\mathfrak{t}_{p_{1}}=\mathfrak{t}_{p_{2}}
, and hence
\mathcal{T}_{p_{1}}=\mathcal{T}_{p_{2}}
. In view of
(\ref{eqpolcon})
, we further conclude that
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{1}}}=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{2}}}
. So, it follows from Proposition
1.9
that the symplectic normal representations at
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are isomorphic.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

The construction of the action uses a Čech model for sheaf cohomology, in much the same way as in the usual classification of principal torus bundles. Since the natural group homomorphism from Čech into sheaf cohomology:

(53)

\check{H}^{1}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})\to H^{1}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})

is an isomorphism, given
\textrm{c}\in H^{1}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})
we can consider a Čech
1
-cocycle
{\tau}\in\check{C}^{1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})
with respect to some open cover
\mathcal{U}
that represents
c
via (
53
). Write
\psi_{UV}:=\psi_{{\tau}(U,V)}
as in (
45
). Furthermore, let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space with momentum image
\Delta
. Then we can define another
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space
J_{{\tau}}:(S_{{\tau}},\omega_{{\tau}})\to M
, as follows. As topological space, define:

S_{{\tau}}:=\frac{\left(\bigsqcup_{U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}J^{-1}(U)\right)}{\sim_{{\tau}}}

where
(p,U)\sim_{{\tau}}(q,V)
if and only if
J(p)=J(q)
and
p=\psi_{UV}(q)
. This indeed defines an equivalence relation, because
{\tau}
is a
1
-cocycle. Furthermore, because
\psi_{UV}\in\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J,\omega)(U\cap V)
for all
U,V\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
, there is a unique smooth structure on
S_{{\tau}}
, a unique symplectic structure
\omega_{{\tau}}
on
S_{{\tau}}
and a unique
\mathcal{T}
-action along the canonical map
J_{{\tau}}:S_{{\tau}}\to M
such that, for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
, the canonical inclusion:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J^{-1}(U),\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{{\tau}},\omega_{{\tau}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{{\tau}}}

is a smooth, symplectic and
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant embedding. Clearly,
J_{{\tau}}:(S_{{\tau}},\omega_{{{\tau}}})\to M
is a faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-space with momentum image equal to
\Delta
. Now, define:

\textrm{c}\cdot[J:(S,\omega)\to M]:=[J_{{\tau}}:(S_{{\tau}},\omega_{{\tau}})\to M].

As one readily verifies, this does not depend on the choice of representative
{\tau}
or cover
\mathcal{U}
,
and defines an action of
H^{1}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})
. To see that this action is free, suppose that:

[J_{{\tau}}:(S_{{\tau}},\omega_{{\tau}})\to M]=[J:(S,\omega)\to M],

meaning that there is an isomorphism of
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{{\tau}},\omega_{{\tau}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{{\tau}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}

For each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
, consider:

\psi_{U}:=\psi|_{J^{-1}(U)}\in\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J,\omega)(U).

Since
\psi
is well-defined, for each
q\in J^{-1}(U\cap V)
it must hold that
\psi_{V}(q)=\psi_{U}(\psi_{UV}(q))
. Hence:

\psi_{UV}=\psi_{U}^{-1}\circ\psi_{V}.

Letting
\sigma(U):U\to\mathcal{T}
be the Lagrangian section such that
\psi_{\sigma(U)}=\psi_{U}
(which exists by Theorem
2.1
), we find that:

{\tau}(U,V)=\sigma(V)-\sigma(U),

hence
{\tau}
is a Čech
1
-coboundary. This shows that the action is indeed free. To verify transitivity, let
J_{1}:(S_{1},\omega_{1})\to M
and
J_{2}:(S_{2},\omega_{2})\to M
be faithful toric
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces with momentum image
\Delta
. By Proposition
2.9
we can find an open cover
\mathcal{U}
of
M
, with for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
an isomorphism of
(\mathcal{T},\Omega)
-spaces:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{1}^{-1}(U),\omega_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{2}^{-1}(U),\omega_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{2}}

Due to Corollary
2.1
, there are unique Lagrangian sections
{\tau}(U,V):U\cap V\to\mathcal{T}
satisfying
\psi_{{\tau}(U,V)}=\psi_{U}\circ\psi_{V}^{-1}
on
J_{2}^{-1}(U\cap V)
. As is readily verified,
{\tau}
defines a Čech
1
-cocycle and:

[J_{1}:(S_{1},\omega_{1})\to M]=\textrm{c}\cdot[J_{2}:(S_{2},\omega_{2})\to M],

where
\textrm{c}\in H^{1}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})
is the class represented by
{\tau}
via (
53
). Hence, the action is indeed transitive.
∎

2.2.
Constructing a (natural) toric space out of a Delzant subspace

2.2.1.
The result of the construction

The aim of this section is to prove and explain the construction behind the theorem below. As explained in Remark
1
, the assignment of a toric space in this theorem and the action in Theorem
2.8
yield the bijection in Theorem
1
b
.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
(M,\Lambda)
be an integral affine manifold. For each Delzant subspace
\Delta
of
(M,\Lambda)
, there is a canonically associated faithful toric
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-space:

J_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M

with momentum map image
\Delta
. This depends naturally and locally on
(M,\Lambda,\Delta)
with respect to locally defined isomorphisms, in the sense explained below.

Here, by the statement that this depends
naturally and locally
on
(M,\Lambda,\Delta)
with respect to locally defined isomorphisms we mean the following. If
(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})
are integral affine manifolds with respective Delzant subspaces
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
, then for any two opens
U_{1}
in
\Delta_{1}
and
U_{2}
in
\Delta_{2}
, and any diffeomorphism of manifolds with corners
\varphi:U_{1}\to U_{2}
such that
\varphi^{*}\Lambda_{2}=\Lambda_{1}|_{U_{1}}
, there is an associated symplectomorphism:

(54)

\varphi_{*}:(J_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}(U_{1}),\omega_{\Delta_{1}})\to(J_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}(U_{2}),\omega_{\Delta_{2}})

that fits into a commutative square:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}(U_{1}),\omega_{\Delta_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}(U_{2}),\omega_{\Delta_{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{\Delta_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{\Delta_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

and that is compatible with the actions, in the sense that for every
p\in S_{\Delta_{1}}
and every
[\alpha]\in(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{1}})_{x}
with
x=J_{\Delta_{1}}(p)
it holds that:

\varphi_{*}([\alpha]\cdot p)=[(\textrm{d}\varphi^{-1})^{*}\alpha]\cdot\varphi_{*}(p).

Furthermore, this association satisfies the following.

i)

It is
natural
: given another integral affine manifold
(M_{3},\Lambda_{3})
with a Delzant subspace
\Delta_{3}
and a diffeomorphism of manifolds with corners
\psi:U_{2}\to U_{3}
onto an open
U_{3}
in
\Delta_{3}
such that
\psi^{*}\Lambda_{3}=\Lambda_{2}|_{U_{2}}
, it holds that:

(\psi\circ\varphi)_{*}=\psi_{*}\circ\varphi_{*}.

ii)

It is
local
: if
V_{1}
is another open in
\Delta_{1}
such that
V_{1}\subset U_{1}
, then:

(\varphi|_{V_{1}})_{*}=(\varphi_{*})|_{J_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}(V_{1})},

where
\varphi|_{V_{1}}:V_{1}\to\varphi(V_{1})
denotes the restriction of
\varphi:U_{1}\to U_{2}
.

In the remainder of this section we give the construction behind Theorem
2.11
. The essential idea behind this construction is the same as that behind the proof of
[
21
, Theorem 1.3.1]
.

2.2.2.
The topology and the action

Throughout this and the next subsection, let
(M,\Lambda)
be a fixed integral affine manifold with a fixed Delzant subspace
\Delta
. As topological space, we define
S_{\Delta}
as follows. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}
be the set-theoretic bundle of groups over
\Delta
with isotropy group at
x
the torus:

(\mathcal{F}_{\Delta})_{x}:=\frac{F_{x}(\Delta)^{0}}{\Lambda_{x}\cap F_{x}(\Delta)^{0}},

where
F_{x}(\Delta)^{0}
denotes the annihilator in
T_{x}^{*}M
of the tangent space
F_{x}(\Delta)
to the open face of
\Delta
through
x\in\Delta
(see Example
B.12
). The groupoid
\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}
includes canonically into
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}
as a set-theoretic wide normal subgroupoid and as such it acts along the bundle projection of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}
. We let
S_{\Delta}
be the orbit space of this action:

S_{\Delta}:=\frac{\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}}{\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}},

equipped with the quotient topology. The bundle projection descends to a continuous map:

(55)

J_{\Delta}:S_{\Delta}\to M,

with image
\Delta
. Since it commutes with the
\mathcal{F}_{\Delta}
-action defined above, the canonical left
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}
-action along the bundle projection of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}
descends to a continuous left
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action along (
55
). This defines the topological space and the action underlying the toric
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-space in Theorem
2.11
.

Proposition 2.12
.

The
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action along (
55
) defined above has the following properties.

a)

The action is free on the open and dense subset
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(\mathring{\Delta})
of
S_{\Delta}
, with
\mathring{\Delta}
as in Example
B.12
.

b)

The orbits coincide with the
J_{\Delta}
-fibers.

c)

The transverse momentum map
\underline{J}_{\Delta}:\underline{S}_{\Delta}\to M
is a homeomorphism onto
\Delta
.

Proof.

Parts
a
and
b
follow from straightforward verifications. It follows from part
b
that
\underline{J}_{\Delta}
a continuous injection so that to prove part
c
it remains to show that it is closed as map into its image
\Delta
. To this end, notice that the bundle projection
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}\to M
is proper, because it is a fiber bundle with compact fibers. This implies that
\underline{J}_{\Delta}
is proper as map into
\Delta
. Since any continuous proper map into a first-countable and Hausdorff space is closed, this concludes the proof.
∎

2.2.3.
The smooth and symplectic structure

Next, we define a structure of symplectic manifold on
S_{\Delta}
that is compatible with the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action along
J_{\Delta}:S_{\Delta}\to M
. First, we construct a local model for this. By a
\Delta
-admissible
triple we will mean a triple
(x_{0},U,\chi)
consisting of a point
x_{0}\in\Delta
and an integral affine chart
(U,\chi)
for
(M,\Lambda)
around
x_{0}
with the property that
\chi(x_{0})=0
,
U
is connected and:

(56)

\chi(U\cap\Delta)=\chi(U)\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k},

where
n=\text{dim}(M)
and
k=\textrm{depth}_{\Delta}(x_{0})
. Every
x_{0}\in\Delta
belongs to such a triple. For each such triple, the symplectic torus bundle:

(57)

(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\sum_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{d}\theta_{j}\wedge\textrm{d}x_{j})|_{\chi(U)}\xrightarrow{\textrm{pr}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}\chi(U)

comes with a faithful toric space with momentum image (
56
), that we denote as:

(58)

J_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}:(S_{(x_{0},U,\chi)},\omega_{(x_{0},U,\chi)})\to\chi(U).

The symplectic manifold
(S_{(x_{0},U,\chi)},\omega_{(x_{0},U,\chi)})
is that obtained by symplectic reduction at the origin of the diagonal (right) Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{k}
-action with momentum map:

(59)

\textrm{pr}_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\circ\textrm{pr}_{\chi(U)}-J_{\mathbb{C}^{k}}\circ\textrm{pr}_{\mathbb{C}^{k}}:(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\chi(U),\sum_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{d}\theta_{j}\wedge\textrm{d}x_{j})\times(\mathbb{C}^{k},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\to\mathbb{R}^{k},

where the
\mathbb{T}^{k}
-action on
\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\chi(U)
is by translation on the first
k
components of
\mathbb{T}^{n}
and the
\mathbb{T}^{k}
-action on
\mathbb{C}^{k}
is the anti-standard one, which is Hamiltonian with momentum map
-J_{\mathbb{C}^{k}}
, where:

J_{\mathbb{C}^{k}}:\mathbb{C}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k},\quad(z_{1},...,z_{k})\mapsto(|z_{1}|^{2},...,|z_{k}|^{2}).

The map (
58
) is that induced by the projection to
\chi(U)
and the action of the torus bundle (
57
) is that induced by the action on itself by translation. Viewed as Hamiltonian
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-space, (
58
) is the standard local model for symplectic toric
\mathbb{T}^{n}
-manifolds. The proposition below shows that (
58
) is a local model for the topological
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-space
J_{\Delta}
.

Proposition 2.13
.

The map:

(60)

\displaystyle h_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}:J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)

\displaystyle\to S_{(x_{0},U,\chi)},

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta_{j}\textrm{d}\chi^{j}_{x}\mod\Lambda_{x}\right]

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[\left(e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}},...,e^{2\pi i\theta_{n}},\chi(x),\sqrt{\chi^{1}(x)},...,\sqrt{\chi^{k}(x)}\right)\right],

is a homeomorphism and it is compatible with the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action along
J_{\Delta}
in the sense that it intertwines (
58
) with:

\chi\circ J_{\Delta}:J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)\to\chi(U),

and for each
p\in J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)
and
t\in(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda})_{x}
with
x=J(p)
it satisfies:

h_{(x_{0},\chi,U)}(t\cdot p)=\Phi_{\chi}(t)\cdot h_{(x_{0},\chi,U)}(p),

where
\Phi_{\chi}
is the isomorphism of symplectic torus bundles given by:

(61)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\chi}:(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})|_{U}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathbb{T}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\sum_{j=1}^{n}\textrm{d}\theta_{j}\wedge\textrm{d}x_{j})|_{\chi(U)},

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta_{j}\textrm{d}\chi^{j}_{x}\mod\Lambda_{x}

\displaystyle\mapsto(e^{2\pi i\theta_{1}},...,e^{2\pi i\theta_{n}},\chi(x)).

Proof.

The bijectivity and the compatibility of (
60
) with the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action follow from a straightforward verification. Furthermore, (
60
) is continuous and closed, because its composition with the canonical map
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{U}\to J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)
(which is a topological quotient map) is continuous and closed (since it factors as a continuous and closed map into the fiber of (
59
) over the origin, composed with the quotient map from this fiber onto
S_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}
, which is closed due to compactness of
\mathbb{T}^{k}
) . So, (
60
) is indeed a homeomorphism.
∎

Corollary 2.14
.

The space
S_{\Delta}
is Hausdorff and second countable.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.13
, for each
\Delta
-admissible triple
(x_{0},U,\chi)
the open subspace
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)
of
S_{\Delta}
is Hausdorff and second countable. In view of this, the fact that
B
is Hausdorff implies that
S_{\Delta}
is Hausdorff and the fact that
B
is second countable implies that
S_{\Delta}
is second countable.
∎

Next, we show that the local smooth and symplectic structures obtained via the homeomorphisms (
60
) patch to a smooth and symplectic structure on all of
S_{\Delta}
.

Proposition 2.15
.

The topological space
S_{\Delta}
admits a smooth and symplectic structure, both uniquely determined by the property that for each
\Delta
-admissible triple
(x_{0},U,\chi)
the induced homeomorphism (
60
) is a symplectomorphism onto the symplectic manifold
(S_{(x_{0},U,\chi)},\omega_{(x_{0},U,\chi)})
defined above.

Proof.

We ought to show that any two given
\Delta
-admissible triples
(x_{0},U,\chi)
and
(y_{0},V,\varphi)
induce the same smooth and symplectic structure on the intersection of
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)
and
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(V)
(via the homeomorphisms
h_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}
and
h_{(y_{0},V,\varphi)}
). Throughout, let
k=\textrm{depth}_{\Delta}(x_{0})
and
l=\textrm{depth}_{\Delta}(y_{0})
.

First we address the smooth structure, starting with the case in which
(x_{0},U)=(y_{0},V)
. In this case,
\chi\circ\varphi^{-1}
is the restriction of an element of
A\in\textrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
that maps
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
onto
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
(because any linear map that maps an open neighbourhood of the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
to another such open must map
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
onto
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
, as
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
is invariant under scaling by positive real numbers). So,
A
is of the form:

A=\begin{pmatrix}A_{k,k}&0\\
A_{n-k.k}&A_{n-k,n-k}\end{pmatrix},\quad A_{k,k}\in\textrm{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{Z}),\quad A_{n-k,k}\in\textrm{M}_{n-k,k}(\mathbb{Z}),\quad A_{n-k,n-k}\in\textrm{GL}_{n-k}(\mathbb{Z}),

where
A_{k,k}
maps
[0,\infty[^{k}
onto
[0,\infty[^{k}
. Any element of
\textrm{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{Z})
that maps
[0,\infty[^{k}
onto
[0,\infty[^{k}
must permute the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
(this is a version of Proposition
1.9
b
and is readily verified). Therefore, there is a permutation
\sigma
of
\{1,...,k\}
such that
\chi^{j}=\varphi^{\sigma(j)}
for all
j\in\{1,...,k\}
. The map
h_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}\circ h_{(x_{0},U,\varphi)}^{-1}
is given by:

S_{(x_{0},U,\varphi)}\to S_{(x_{0},U,\chi)},\quad[(t,x,z_{1},...,z_{k})]\mapsto[(A_{*}(t),A(x),...,A(x),z_{\sigma(1)},...,z_{\sigma(k)})],

which is smooth. The inverse of this map is obtained by reversing the roles of
\chi
and
\varphi
. So, it is a diffeomorphism, which means that
(x_{0},U,\chi)
and
(x_{0},U,\varphi)
indeed induce the same smooth structure on
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)
.

Next, we address the smooth structure in general, by reducing to the previous case. It is enough to show that for every
w_{0}\in U\cap V\cap\Delta
there is an open neighbourhood
W
of
w_{0}
in
U\cap V
such that the smooth structures on
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)
and
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(V)
induced by the respective triples
(x_{0},U,\chi)
and
(y_{0},V,\varphi)
restrict to the same smooth structure on
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(W)
. To do so, let such
w_{0}
be given and let
m=\textrm{depth}_{\Delta}(w_{0})
. After possibly permuting the first
k
components
\chi
and the first
l
components of
\varphi
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\chi^{j}(w_{0})=0
if
j\leq m
,
\chi^{j}(w_{0})>0
if
m<j\leq k
,
\varphi^{j}(w_{0})=0
if
j\leq m
and
\varphi^{j}(w_{0})>0
if
m<j\leq l
. Indeed, by the previous case, changing the given
\Delta
-admissible triples by such permutations leaves the induced smooth structures invariant. Now, choose a connected open neighbourhood
W
of
w_{0}
in
U\cap V
such that for every
x\in W
:

(62)

\displaystyle\chi^{j}(x)>0\quad

\displaystyle\textrm{if}\quad m<j\leq k,

(63)

\displaystyle\varphi^{j}(x)>0\quad

\displaystyle\textrm{if}\quad m<j\leq l.

Consider the charts
\widetilde{\chi}:=\chi|_{W}-\chi(w_{0})
and
\widetilde{\varphi}:=\varphi|_{W}-\varphi(w_{0})
. By construction, the triples
(w_{0},W,\widetilde{\chi})
and
(w_{0},W,\widetilde{\varphi})
are
\Delta
-admissible and by the previous case these induce the same smooth structure on the open
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(W)
. The smooth structure on
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)
induced by
h_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}
restricts to that on
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(W)
induced by
h_{(w_{0},W,\widetilde{\chi})}
, since the homeomorphism:

h_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}\circ h_{(w_{0},W,\widetilde{\chi})}^{-1}:S_{(w_{0},W,\widetilde{\chi})}\to J_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}^{-1}(W)

is a diffeomorphism, for this map is given by:

[(t,x,z_{1},...z_{m})]\mapsto\left[\left(t,x,z_{1},...,z_{m},\sqrt{\chi^{m+1}(x)},...,\sqrt{\chi^{k}(x)}\right)\right],

and its inverse is given by:

\left[\left(t_{1},...,t_{m},t_{m+1}\left(\frac{z_{m+1}}{\sqrt{\chi^{m+1}(x)}}\right),...,t_{k}\left(\frac{z_{k}}{\sqrt{\chi^{k}(x)}}\right),t_{k+1},...,t_{n},x,z_{1},...,z_{m}\right)\right]\mapsfrom[(t,x,z_{1},...,z_{k})],

which are both smooth by (
62
). Similarly, it follows from (
63
) that the smooth structure on
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(V)
induced by
h_{(y_{0},V,\varphi)}
restricts to that on
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(W)
induced by
h_{(w_{0},W,\widetilde{\varphi})}
. All together, this shows that the smooth structures on
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U)
and
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(V)
induced by the respective triples
(x_{0},U,\chi)
and
(y_{0},V,\varphi)
indeed restrict to the same smooth structure on
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(W)
. This proves the part of the proposition regarding the smooth structure.

For the part regarding the symplectic structure, it is enough to show (by continuity) that the symplectic forms induced by
h_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}
and
h_{(y_{0},V,\varphi)}
coincide on the open
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U\cap V)\cap J_{\Delta}^{-1}(\mathring{\Delta})
, which is dense in
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U\cap V)
. To see that these indeed coincide, notice that the quotient map
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}\to S_{\Delta}
restricts to a diffeomorphism:

(64)

\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\mathring{\Delta}}\to J_{\Delta}^{-1}(\mathring{\Delta})

and the symplectic forms on the open
J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U\cap V)\cap J_{\Delta}^{-1}(\mathring{\Delta})
induced by
h_{(x_{0},U,\chi)}
and
h_{(y_{0},V,\varphi)}
both coincide with the push-forward of the symplectic form
\Omega_{\Lambda}
along (
64
).
∎

Remark 2.16
.

This proof shows that (
64
) is a symplectomorphism:

(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\mathring{\Delta}},\Omega_{\Lambda})\xrightarrow{\sim}(J_{\Delta}^{-1}(\mathring{\Delta}),\omega_{\Delta}).

In particular,
(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})
is simply
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
when
\Delta=M
.

Next, we show that this structure of symplectic manifold on
S_{\Delta}
is compatible with the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action.

Proposition 2.17
.

The
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action along (
55
) (defined in the previous subsection) is smooth and Hamiltonian with respect to the smooth and symplectic structure in Proposition
2.15
.

Proof.

This follows from the part of Proposition
2.13
on compatibility of (
60
) with the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action.
∎

Henceforth, we consider
S_{\Delta}
as smooth manifold with the smooth structure in Proposition
2.15
and we let
\omega_{\Delta}
denote the symplectic structure in this proposition. Combining Proposition
2.12
and Proposition
2.17
, we conclude that (equipped with the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action defined in the previous subsection):

J_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M

is a faithful toric
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-space.

Example 2.18
.

Consider the standard integral affine cylinder
(M,\Lambda):=(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z}\textrm{d}\theta\oplus\mathbb{Z}\textrm{d}x)
with Delzant subspace the compact cylinder
\Delta:=\mathbb{S}^{1}\times[-1,1]
. In this case,
(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})
is the symplectic manifold
(\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2},\omega_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\oplus\omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}})
, with
\omega_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}
and
\omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
the standard area forms (up to a factor
2\pi
).

2.2.4.
The natural and local dependence

To complete the proof of Theorem
2.11
it remains to address the natural and local dependence of the construction given in the previous subsections. Let
(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})
be integral affine manifolds with respective Delzant subspaces
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
, and let
\varphi:U_{1}\to U_{2}
be a diffeomorphism of manifolds with corners between respective opens
U_{1}
in
\Delta_{1}
and
U_{2}
in
\Delta_{2}
, such that
\varphi^{*}(\Lambda_{2})=\Lambda_{1}|_{U_{1}}
. Then
\varphi
induces a map:

(65)

\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{1}}|_{U_{1}}\to\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{2}}|_{U_{2}},\quad[\alpha]\mapsto[(\textrm{d}\varphi^{-1})^{*}\alpha].

Since
\varphi
is a diffeomorphism of manifolds with corners between opens in
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
, it holds that:

\textrm{d}\varphi_{x}(F_{x}(\Delta_{1}))=F_{\varphi(x)}(\Delta_{2}),

for all
x\in U_{1}
. Therefore, (
65
) descends to a map:

J_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}(U_{1})\to J_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}(U_{2}).

Definition 2.19
.

We define (
54
) to be the map induced by (
65
).

Proposition 2.20
.

This map is indeed a symplectomorphism
(J_{\Delta_{1}}^{-1}(U_{1}),\omega_{\Delta_{1}})\xrightarrow{\sim}(J_{\Delta_{2}}^{-1}(U_{2}),\omega_{\Delta_{2}})
.

Proof.

Let
x_{0}\in U_{1}
and let
k=\textrm{depth}_{\Delta_{1}}(x_{0})
and
n=\text{dim}(M_{1})
. Since
\varphi
is a diffeomorphism of manifolds with corners from an open in
\Delta_{1}
onto an open in
\Delta_{2}
, it holds that
\textrm{depth}_{\Delta_{2}}(\varphi(x_{0}))=k
and
\text{dim}(M_{2})=n
. Now, fix integral affine charts
(V_{1},\chi_{1})
around
x_{0}
and
(V_{2},\chi_{2})
around
\varphi(x_{0})
with the following properties:

•

(x_{0},V_{1},\chi_{1})
is
\Delta_{1}
-admissible and
(\varphi(x_{0}),V_{2},\chi_{2})
is
\Delta_{2}
-admissible, as in (
56
),

•

V_{1}\cap\Delta_{1}\subset U_{1}
,
V_{2}\cap\Delta_{2}\subset U_{2}
and
\varphi(V_{1}\cap\Delta_{1})\subset V_{2}\cap\Delta_{2}
,

•

\chi_{1}(V_{1})
is an open ball around the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

and consider the coordinate representation:

(66)

\chi_{2}\circ\varphi\circ\chi_{1}^{-1}:\chi_{1}(V_{1})\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}\to\chi_{2}(V_{2})\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}.

Using the same arguments as in the proof of Lemma
1.26
, the fact that
\varphi^{*}\Lambda_{2}=\Lambda_{1}|_{U_{1}}
, the fact that any point in
\chi(V_{1})\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
can be connected to the origin by a smooth path in
\chi(V_{1})\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
and the fact that (
66
) maps the origin to itself, it follows that (
66
) is the restriction of an element
A\in\textrm{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Z})
. Since
A
is a linear map that maps an open neighbourhood of the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
to another such open, it must map
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
onto
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
. Therefore,
(x_{0},V_{1},A\circ\chi_{1})
is another
\Delta_{1}
-admissible triple. To conclude the proof, notice that:

h_{(\varphi(x_{0}),V_{2},\chi_{2})}\circ(\varphi)_{*}\circ h_{(x_{0},V_{1},A\circ\chi_{1})}^{-1}:(S_{(x_{0},V_{1},A\circ\chi_{1})},\omega_{(x_{0},V_{1},A\circ\chi_{1})})\to(S_{(\varphi(x_{0}),V_{2},\chi_{2})},\omega_{(\varphi(x_{0}),V_{2},\chi_{2})})

is given by the inclusion:

[(t,x,z)]\mapsto[(t,x,z)],

which is clearly smooth and symplectic. Since
x_{0}\in U_{1}
was arbitrary, this shows that
\varphi_{*}
is smooth and symplectic. Since
\varphi_{*}^{-1}=(\varphi^{-1})_{*}
, the inverse of
\varphi_{*}
is smooth and symplectic as well.
∎

Seeing as the remaining properties listed in Subsection
2.2.1
are clearly satisfied, this concludes the construction behind (and hence the proof of) Theorem
2.11
.

3.
The first structure theorem and the splitting theorem

In this part we address Theorems
3
and
4
. In Section
3.1
we introduce the equivariant sheaf appearing in these theorems and provide the necessary background on the cohomology of such sheaves. In particular, we recall a Čech-type model for these cohomology groups that will be used in the proofs of these theorems. The proof of Theorem
3
is given in Section
3.2
. Along the way (see Proposition
3.9
), we make the meaning of the naturality in that theorem precise. The proof of Theorem
4
is the content of Section
3.3
. In that section we also give the definition of the Lagrangian Dixmier-Douady class.

3.1.
On equivariant sheaves for topological groupoids

3.1.1.
Equivariant sheaves and their cohomology

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows X
be a topological groupoid. Recall that a
\mathcal{G}
-sheaf of abelian groups
(simply called
\mathcal{G}
-sheaf throughout) is a sheaf
\mathcal{A}
of abelian groups on
X
equipped with a continuous (right) action of
\mathcal{G}
along the etale map of
\mathcal{A}
by fiberwise group homomorphisms.
The category
\textsf{Sh}(\mathcal{G})
of
\mathcal{G}
-sheaves, with morphisms the
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant maps of the underlying sheaves, is abelian and has enough injectives. The additive functor
\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}:\textsf{Sh}(\mathcal{G})\to\textsf{Ab}
that takes
\mathcal{G}
-invariant global sections is left-exact, and the cohomology groups of a
\mathcal{G}
-sheaf
\mathcal{A}
are defined as the right-derived functors of
\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}
, meaning that:

H^{n}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{A}):=H^{n}(\Gamma^{\mathcal{G}}(I^{\bullet})),

with
0\to\mathcal{A}\to I^{0}\to I^{1}\to\dots
an injective resolution of
\mathcal{A}
in
\textsf{Sh}(\mathcal{G})
.

Example 3.1
.

Our main example of interest is the sheaf
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
appearing in theorems
3
and
4
. This
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
-sheaf is defined for any integral affine orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows(M,\Lambda)
and any Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}
thereof, as follows. Let
\Delta
denote the corresponding invariant subspace of
M
and consider the pre-symplectic torus bundle
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
defined as in Remark
1.21
. For simplicity, suppose first that
\mathcal{B}
is etale, so that the torus bundle
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
is symplectic. Then
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
is the sheaf in Definition
2.2
and, in fact, both sheaves in Definition
2.2
are naturally
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
-sheaves. For
\gamma:x\to y
in
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
and
\tau:V\to\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
a section over an open
V
in
\Delta
around
y
, the action on the germ of
\tau
at
y
is given by:

[\tau]_{y}\cdot\gamma:=[(t\circ\sigma)^{*}(\tau)]_{x},

with
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{B}
any smooth section of
s_{\mathcal{B}}
over an open
U
in
\Delta
around
x
such that
\sigma(x)=\gamma
and
t(\sigma(U))\subset V
. In general (when
\mathcal{B}
is not necessarily etale), we let
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
denote the sheaf of isotropic sections of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}
(i.e. smooth local sections
\tau
of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}
such that
\tau^{*}\Omega_{\Lambda}=0
). This is a subsheaf of the sheaf of
\mathcal{F}
-basic smooth sections of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}
, with
\mathcal{F}
the foliation by connected components of the orbits of
\mathcal{B}
. Both of these are
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
-sheaves, with the action defined as in the etale case.

When working with these cohomology groups we usually reduce to the case in which the groupoid is etale, by restricting to a complete transversal (one that intersects all orbits). This is possible due to Example
1.36
and:

Proposition 3.2
.

A Morita equivalence of Lie groupoids with corners (as in Definition
B.20
):
\mathcal{G}_{1}
X_{1}
P
X_{2}
\mathcal{G}_{2}
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

induces an equivalence of categories:

P_{*}:\textsf{Sh}(\mathcal{G}_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textsf{Sh}(\mathcal{G}_{2}),

and hence, for any
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-sheaf
\mathcal{A}
, it induces an isomorphism:

H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{A})\cong H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}_{2},P_{*}\mathcal{A}).

This is functorial with respect to composition of Morita equivalences.

Corollary 3.3
.

An integral affine Morita equivalence:
\mathcal{B}_{1}
(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})
P
(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})
\mathcal{B}_{2}
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

that relates a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}_{1}\subset\underline{M}_{1}
to
\underline{\Delta}_{2}\subset\underline{M}_{2}
induces an isomorphism:

H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{1})\cong H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B}_{2}|_{\Delta_{2}},\mathcal{L}_{2}),

between the cohomology groups of
\mathcal{L}_{1}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}
(as in Example
3.1
). This is functorial with respect to composition of integral affine Morita equivalences.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
and Corollary
3.3
.

Proposition
3.2
can be proved as for Lie groupoids without corners (see
[
37
, Corollary 3.11]
). The functor
P_{*}
sends a
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-sheaf
\mathcal{A}
with etale space
E\to X_{1}
to the
\mathcal{G}_{2}
-sheaf corresponding to the etale space:

\alpha_{2}\circ\textrm{pr}_{P}:(P\times_{X_{1}}E)/\mathcal{G}_{1}\to X_{2},

equipped with the
\mathcal{G}_{2}
-action given by
[p,e]\cdot g:=[p\cdot g,e]
. The corollary follows from this, because for an integral affine Morita equivalence that relates
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
to
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
the
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta_{2}}
-sheaves
P_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}})
and
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}
are isomorphic, via the isomorphism given as follows. Let us call an open
U
in
M_{i}
simple if it is the domain of a foliation chart
\chi
with
\chi(U)
a product of a leafwise open cube and a transversal open cube centered around the origin, such that
\chi(U\cap\Delta)=\chi(U)\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
and the
\mathcal{F}_{i}
-basic coordinate
1
-forms span
\Lambda_{i}|_{U}
. Suppose that
U
is a simple open in
M_{2}
over which
\alpha_{2}
admits a smooth section
\sigma:U\to P
such that
\alpha_{1}\circ\sigma
maps
U
into a simple open
V
in
M_{1}
and
(\alpha_{1}\circ\sigma)(U)
intersects all plaques of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
in
V
. Since the Morita equivalence is integral affine and sections of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
are
\mathcal{F}_{1}
-basic, pull-back along
\alpha_{1}\circ\sigma
(whose differential at
x
lifts the map
\psi^{-1}_{\sigma(x)}
as in Remark
1.34
) induces an isomorphism:

\mathcal{L}_{1}(V\cap\Delta_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{L}_{2}(U\cap\Delta_{2}),\quad\tau\mapsto(\alpha_{1}\circ\sigma)^{*}(\tau).

On the other hand, we have an isomorphism:

\mathcal{L}_{1}(V\cap\Delta_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}P_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{1})(U\cap\Delta_{2}),\quad\tau\mapsto[\sigma(-),[\tau]_{(\alpha_{1}\circ\sigma)(-)}].

Over opens of the form
U\cap\Delta_{2}
, which form a basis of
\Delta_{2}
, the isomorphism between
P_{*}(\mathcal{L}_{1})
and
\mathcal{L}_{2}
is given by the composition of these, which does not depend on the choice of
\sigma
or
V
.
∎

3.1.2.
Bisections, embedding categories and their cohomology

Recall that a
continuous bisection
of a topological groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows X
is a continuous section
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{G}
of the source-map of
\mathcal{G}
, defined on an open
U
in
X
, with the property that
t\circ\sigma:U\to X
is an open embedding. For the image of this open embedding we use the notation:

\sigma\cdot U:=(t\circ\sigma)(U).

Given two continuous bisections
\sigma_{1}:U_{1}\to\mathcal{G}
and
\sigma_{2}:U_{2}\to\mathcal{G}
such that
\sigma_{2}\cdot U_{2}\subset U_{1}
, we can consider their composition, which is again a continuous bisection of
\mathcal{G}
:

\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}:U_{2}\to\mathcal{G},\quad(\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2})(x):=\sigma_{1}((t\circ\sigma_{2})(x))\sigma_{2}(x).

In the smooth and symplectic setting we consider the following variations of this.

•

By a
smooth bisection
of a Lie groupoid with corners
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows X
(see Definition
B.18
) we mean a continuous bisection
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{G}
with the property that
\sigma
is smooth as map between manifolds with corners and
t\circ\sigma
is a diffeomorphism onto its image.

•

By a
Lagrangian bisection
of a symplectic groupoid with corners
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows X
(see Definition
B.22
) we mean a smooth bisection
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{G}
with the property that
\sigma^{*}\Omega=0
.

The composition of two smooth (resp. Lagrangian) bisections is again smooth (resp. Lagrangian). Moreover, continuous bisections of etale Lie groupoids with corners are automatically smooth.

We will use a Čech-type model from
[
33
,
35
,
10
]
for the cohomology of equivariant sheaves for etale groupoids. To recall this, let
\mathcal{E}\rightrightarrows X
be a topological etale groupoid. Given a basis
\mathcal{U}
for the topology of
X
, the
embedding category
over
\mathcal{U}
is the category
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{E})
with objects the opens in
\mathcal{U}
and arrows
V\leftarrow U
the continuous bisections
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{E}
with
\sigma\cdot U\subset V
. We denote such an arrow as
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
. The composition of two arrows
U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}U_{1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{2}}U_{2}
is the arrow
U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}U_{2}
with underlying bisection the composition of
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
. A
category approximating

\mathcal{E}
over
\mathcal{U}
is a wide subcategory
E
of
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{E})
with the following properties.

i)

For any
U,V\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
with
U\subset V
, the arrow
V\hookleftarrow U
(with bisection the unit map) lies in
E
.

ii)

If
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
belongs to
E
, then so does
V^{\prime}\xleftarrow{\sigma}U^{\prime}
, for any
U^{\prime},V^{\prime}\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
with
\sigma\cdot U^{\prime}\subset V^{\prime}
.

iii)

For any
V\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
and
y\xleftarrow{g}x
in
\mathcal{E}
with
y\in V
, there is an arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
in
E
with
x\in U
and
\sigma(x)=g
.

Give such a category
E
, an
\mathcal{E}
-sheaf
\mathcal{A}
induces a pre-sheaf on
E
(a contravariant functor from
E
to the category of abelian groups) that assigns to a
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
the abelian group
\mathcal{A}(U)
and to an arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
the map
\mathcal{A}(V)\to\mathcal{A}(U)
that sends
a\in\mathcal{A}(V)
to the section
a\cdot\sigma\in\mathcal{A}(V)
given by:

[a\cdot\sigma]_{x}:=[a]_{(t\circ\sigma)(x)}\cdot\sigma(x)\in\mathcal{A}_{x},\quad x\in U.

The cohomology that we will use is that of the small category
E
with values in this pre-sheaf. We denote this as
\check{H}^{\bullet}(\textsf{E},\mathcal{A})
, or as
\check{H}^{\bullet}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A})
when
\textsf{E}=\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{E})
. Explicitly,
\check{H}^{\bullet}(\textsf{E},\mathcal{A})
is the cohomology of the cochain complex
(\check{C}(\textsf{E},\mathcal{A}),\check{\textrm{d}})
, with
n
-cochains:

\check{C}^{n}(\textsf{E},\mathcal{A})=\bigsqcap_{U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n}}\mathcal{A}(U_{n})

where the product runs through all
n
-strings of composable arrows in
E
. So, an
n
-cochain
c
assigns to each such string
U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n}
an element:

c(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n})\in\mathcal{A}(U_{n}).

The differential
\check{d}:\check{C}^{n}(\textsf{E},\mathcal{A})\to\check{C}^{n+1}(\textsf{E},\mathcal{A})
is given by:

\displaystyle\check{d}(c)(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n+1}}U_{n+1})

\displaystyle=c(U_{1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{2}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n+1}}U_{n+1})

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}c(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{i-1}}U_{i-1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{i+1}}U_{i+1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{i+2}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n+1}}U_{n+1})

\displaystyle+(-1)^{n+1}c(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n})\cdot\sigma_{n+1}.

Like for usual Čech cohomology of sheaves on topological spaces, there is a natural morphism:

(67)

\varphi_{\textsf{E}}:\check{H}^{\bullet}(\textsf{E},\mathcal{A})\to H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A}),

induced, for instance, by the double complex obtained by applying
\check{C}^{\bullet}(\textsf{E},-)
to an injective resolution of the
\mathcal{G}
-sheaf
\mathcal{A}
. This is an isomorphism if
\mathcal{A}
is
\mathcal{U}
-acyclic
, meaning that
H^{n}(U,\mathcal{A})=0
for each
n>0
and each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
. If
\text{$\mathcal{U}$}^{\prime}
is another basis and
\textsf{E}^{\prime}
a category approximating
\mathcal{E}
over
\text{$\mathcal{U}$}^{\prime}
, then is
\text{$\mathcal{U}$}\cup\text{$\mathcal{U}$}^{\prime}
is also a basis and the diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}^{\bullet}(\textsf{E},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}\cup\text{$\mathcal{U}$}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{A})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}^{\prime}}^{\bullet}(\textsf{E}^{\prime},\mathcal{A})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\textsf{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\textsf{E}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\textsf{E}^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\textsf{E}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\textsf{E}^{\prime}})^{*}}

commutes, where
\textsf{E}^{\prime\prime}:=\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}\cup\text{$\mathcal{U}$}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{E})
and
i_{\textsf{E}}:\textsf{E}\to\textsf{E}^{\prime\prime}
and
i_{\textsf{E}^{\prime}}:\textsf{E}^{\prime}\to\textsf{E}^{\prime\prime}
are the inclusion functors.

Remark 3.4
.

Given an etale integral affine orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows(M,\Lambda)
and a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}
with corresponding invariant subspace
\Delta\subset M
, there always is a basis
\mathcal{U}
of
\Delta
for which
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
is
\mathcal{U}
-acyclic. This follows from the lemma below.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
(V,\Lambda_{V})
be an integral affine vector space and let
\Delta
be a convex Delzant subspace. Then
H^{\bullet}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})
vanishes in all degrees greater than zero.

Proof.

By the Poincaré lemma for manifolds with corners we have a short exact sequence:

0\to(\mathcal{C}^{\infty})_{\Lambda}\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}\xrightarrow{\textrm{d}}\mathcal{L}\to 0,

where
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}:=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}
and
(\mathcal{C}^{\infty})_{\Lambda}
is the subsheaf of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
of those functions
f
such that
\textrm{d}f
takes value in the lattice bundle
\Lambda\subset T^{*}V
of
(V,\Lambda_{V})
. Secondly, we have a short exact sequence:

0\to\mathbb{R}\to(\mathcal{C}^{\infty})_{\Lambda}\xrightarrow{\textrm{d}}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)\to 0,

where
\mathbb{R}
denotes the sheaf on
\Delta
of locally constant functions with values in
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)
denotes the sheaf of smooth sections of
\Lambda|_{\Delta}
