. Since
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
is a fine sheaf and
H^{\bullet}(\Delta,\mathbb{R})
vanishes in degree greater than zero (
\Delta
being convex), we derive from the resulting long exact sequences that:

H^{p}(\Delta,\mathcal{L})\cong H^{p+1}(\Delta,(\mathcal{C}^{\infty})_{\Lambda})\cong H^{p+1}(\Delta,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda))

for all
p>0
. The lemma follows from this, because
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}^{\infty}(\Lambda)
is the sheaf of locally constant functions with values in
\Lambda_{V}^{*}
(since
\Lambda=\Lambda^{*}_{V}\times V
), so that the cohomology
H^{\bullet}(\Delta,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda))
vanishes in all degrees greater than zero (by convexity of
\Delta
).
∎

3.2.
Proof of the first structure theorem

3.2.1.
Lifting to a Lagrangian cocycle

The aim of this subsection is to prove the lemma below, which will be key in the proofs of both the first structure theorem and the splitting theorem.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid for which the associated orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale, let
\underline{\Delta}
be a Delzant subspace of
\underline{M}
and let
\mathcal{U}
be a basis of
\Delta
for which
\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
is
\mathcal{U}
-acyclic. If
\Delta
is the momentum image of a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space, then for each arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
there is a Lagrangian bisection:

(68)

g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U):U\to(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}

lifting
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
, and these lifts can be chosen so as to satisfy the cocycle condition:

(69)

g(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}U_{2})=g(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}U_{1})g(U_{1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{2}}U_{2})

for any two composable arrows
U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}U_{1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{2}}U_{2}
.

To prove this, the following observation will be key.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be as in Lemma
3.6
, let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space and let
\Delta:=J(S)
. Further, suppose that we are given a continuous (or equivalently, smooth) bisection
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
defined on an open
U
in
\Delta
. Then the following hold.

a)

For any Lagrangian bisection
g_{\sigma}:U\to(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
lifting
\sigma
, the map:

\psi_{g_{\sigma}}:(J^{-1}(U),\omega)\to(J^{-1}(\sigma\cdot U),\omega),\quad\psi_{g_{\sigma}}(p)=g_{\sigma}(J(p))\cdot p,

is a symplectomorphism that fits into a commutative square:

(70)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J^{-1}(U),\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J^{-1}(\sigma\cdot U),\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\cdot U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{g_{\sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t\circ\sigma}

and is compatible with the
\mathcal{T}
-action in the sense that for each
p\in J^{-1}(U)
and
t\in\mathcal{T}_{J(p)}
:

\psi_{g_{\sigma}}(t\cdot p)=(\sigma(J(p))\cdot t)\cdot\psi_{g_{\sigma}}(p).

b)

Conversely, for any symplectomorphism
\psi:(J^{-1}(U),\omega)\to(J^{-1}(\sigma\cdot U),\omega)
that fits into a commutative square and is compatible with the
\mathcal{T}
-action as above, there is a unique Lagrangian bisection
g_{\sigma}:U\to(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
that lifts
\sigma
and is such that
\psi=\psi_{g_{\sigma}}
.

Proof.

Notice first that, given a smooth bisection
g_{\sigma}:U\to\mathcal{G}
lifting
\sigma
, the associated map
\psi_{g_{\sigma}}
is a diffeomorphism, fits into a commutative square as above and is compatible with the
\mathcal{T}
-action. As in Proposition
2.6
one can prove that
\psi_{g_{\sigma}}
is a symplectomorphism if and only if the smooth bisection
g_{\sigma}:U\to(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is Lagrangian. This proves
a
and shows that, to prove
b
, it is enough to prove that for any diffeomorphism
\psi:J^{-1}(U)\to J^{-1}(\sigma\cdot U)
that fits into a commutative square like (
70
) and is compatible with the
\mathcal{T}
-action as above, there is a unique smooth bisection
g_{\sigma}:U\to\mathcal{G}
that lifts
\sigma
and is such that
\psi=\psi_{g_{\sigma}}
. For uniqueness, suppose that
g_{\sigma},h_{\sigma}:U\to\mathcal{G}
are two such smooth bisections. Let
p\in J^{-1}(U)
such that
\mathcal{T}
acts freely at
p
. Then
(g_{\sigma}^{-1}h_{\sigma})(J(p))
belongs to
\mathcal{T}
(since both
g_{\sigma}
and
h_{\sigma}
lift
\sigma
) and it fixes
p
(since
\psi_{g_{\sigma}}=\psi_{h_{\sigma}}
). Hence,
g_{\sigma}(J(p))=h_{\sigma}(J(p))
for all
p\in J^{-1}(U)
at which
\mathcal{T}
acts freely. Since the set of such
p\in S
is dense in
S
, it follows by continuity that
g_{\sigma}=h_{\sigma}
, as claimed. In view of this uniqueness, to prove existence it is enough to show that for every
x\in U
there is an open neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x
in
U
and a smooth section
g_{\sigma}:U_{x}\to\mathcal{G}
of the source map such that
\psi(p)=g_{\sigma}(J(p))\cdot p
for all
p\in J^{-1}(U_{x})
. To this end, let
x\in U
and let
h_{\sigma}:U_{x}\to\mathcal{G}
be any smooth bisection, defined on some open neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x
in
U
. Then
\psi_{h_{\sigma}}^{-1}\circ(\psi|_{J^{-1}(U_{x})})
belongs to
\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J)(U_{x})
, as in (
44
). Hence, it follows from Theorem
2.7
applied to the
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
(which is faithful toric by Lemma
1.47
), that there is a section
\tau\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})(U_{x})
with the property that:

\psi_{h_{\sigma}}^{-1}(\psi(p))=\tau(J(p))\cdot p

for every
p\in J^{-1}(U_{x})
. The smooth bisection
g_{\sigma}=h_{\sigma}\tau:U_{x}\to\mathcal{G}
has the desired property.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

Since
H^{1}(U,\mathcal{L}_{\Delta})=0
for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
, it follows from Theorem
2.8
that there is an isomorphism of Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-spaces:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U),\omega_{\Delta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J^{-1}(U),\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{\Delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}

where
\mathcal{T}
acts along
J_{\Delta}
via the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action in Theorem
2.11
and (
34
). Fix such an isomorphism for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
. Now, let
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
be an arrow in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
. It follows from
\mathcal{B}
-invariance of the lattice
\Lambda
in
T^{*}M
that
(t\circ\sigma)^{*}\Lambda=\Lambda|_{U}
. So, since
t\circ\sigma
is a diffeomorphism of manifolds with corners from
U
onto an open in
\Delta
, by Theorem
2.11
we have an induced symplectomorphism:

(J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U),\omega)\xrightarrow{(t\circ\sigma)_{*}}(J^{-1}_{\Delta}(\sigma\cdot U),\omega).

Now notice that:

\psi_{V}\circ(t\circ\sigma)_{*}\circ\psi_{U}^{-1}:(J^{-1}(U),\omega)\to(J^{-1}(\sigma\cdot U),\omega)

meets the assumptions of Proposition
3.7
b
(as is readily verified using Lemma
1.38
), so that there is a unique Lagrangian bisection:

g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U):U\to(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}

that lifts
\sigma
and satisfies:

g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(J(p))\cdot p=(\psi_{V}\circ(t\circ\sigma)_{*}\circ\psi_{U}^{-1})(p)

for all
p\in J^{-1}(U)
. Using the natural and local dependence in Theorem
2.11
, it is readily verified that these choices of lifts satisfy the cocycle condition (
69
).
∎

3.2.2.
The rest of the proof

We start with a proof of the first structure theorem for the case in which
\mathcal{B}
is etale, excluding naturality with respect to symplectic Morita equivalences. The proof in full generality and the naturality will be addressed afterwards.

Proof of Theorem
3
; definition of the action on (
3
) in the etale case.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid for which the associated orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale and let
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
be a Delzant subspace. To define the action, fix a basis
\mathcal{U}
of
\Delta
such that
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
is
\mathcal{U}
-acyclic. Let
\textrm{c}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
and consider a
1
-cocycle
\tau
representing the class
[\tau]\in\check{H}^{1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
corresponding to
c
via the isomorphism (
67
). Let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with momentum image
\Delta
. First consider the topological space:

(71)

\bigsqcup_{U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}J^{-1}(U).

Given two elements
(p_{1},U_{1})
and
(p_{2},U_{2})
of (
71
), we write
(p_{1},U_{1})\hookleftarrow(p_{2},U_{2})
if
U_{2}\subset U_{1}
and:

\tau(U_{1}\hookleftarrow U_{2})(J(p_{2}))\cdot p_{2}=p_{1},

where
U_{2}\hookleftarrow U_{1}
denotes the arrow in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
with underlying bisection the unit map. This relation is reflexive and transitive (as follows from the fact that
\tau
is a
1
-cocycle), but not necessarily symmetric. The equivalence relation generated by this relation is given by:
(p_{1},U_{1})\sim(p_{2},U_{2})
if and only if there is a pair
(p_{12},U_{12})
, with
U_{12}\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
and
p_{12}\in U_{12}\subset U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, such that
(p_{1},U_{1})\hookleftarrow(p_{12},U_{12})
and
(p_{12},U_{12})\hookrightarrow(p_{2},U_{2})
. Indeed, this relation is clearly reflexive, symmetric and contains the first relation. To prove transitivity, suppose that
(p_{1},U_{1})\sim(p_{2},U_{2})
and
(p_{2},U_{2})\sim(p_{3},U_{3})
. Then there are
(p_{12},U_{12})
and
(p_{23},U_{23})
with
U_{12},U_{23}\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
,
p_{12}\in U_{12}\subset U_{1}\cap U_{2}
and
p_{23}\in U_{23}\subset U_{2}\cap U_{3}
, such that:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{1},U_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{2},U_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{3},U_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{12},U_{12})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p_{23},U_{23})}

Let
U_{123}\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
be such that
J(p_{2})\in U_{123}\subset U_{12}\cap U_{23}
and consider the element

p_{123}:=\tau(U_{2}\hookleftarrow U_{123})(J(p_{2}))^{-1}\cdot p_{2}\in J^{-1}(U_{123}),

which is well-defined since the source and target of
\tau(U_{2}\hookleftarrow U_{123})(J(p_{2}))
coincide. It follows from the cocycle condition (
69
) that
(p_{12},U_{12})\hookleftarrow(p_{123},U_{123})
and
(p_{123},U_{123})\hookrightarrow(p_{23},U_{23})
, and hence that
(p_{1},U_{1})\hookleftarrow(p_{123},U_{123})
and
(p_{123},U_{123})\hookrightarrow(p_{3},U_{3})
. This shows that
(p_{1},U_{1})\sim(p_{3},U_{3})
, which proves transitivity. Now, consider the quotient space:

S_{\tau}:=\frac{\left(\bigsqcup_{U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}J^{-1}(U)\right)}{\sim}.

From the above description of the equivalence relation it is clear that for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
the map:

(72)

j_{U}:J^{-1}(U)\to S_{\tau},\quad p\mapsto[p,U]

is an injection. Since for each inclusion
V\hookleftarrow U
the map:

(J^{-1}(U),\omega)\to(J^{-1}(U),\omega),\quad p\mapsto\tau(V\hookleftarrow U)(J(p))\cdot p

is a symplectomorphism (which follows from Proposition
2.6
), there is a unique structure of symplectic manifold on
S_{\tau}
with the property that for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
the injection (
72
) is a symplectomorphism onto an open in
S_{\tau}
(with respect to the symplectic form
\omega
on
J^{-1}(U)
). Let
\omega_{\tau}
be the corresponding symplectic form on
S_{\tau}
. Next, note that
J:(S,\omega)\to M
induces a smooth map:

J_{\tau}:(S_{\tau},\omega_{\tau})\to M,\quad[p,U]\mapsto J(p),

along which
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
acts in a Hamiltonian fashion, as follows. Given
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
p_{\tau}\in S_{\tau}
such that
s(g)=J_{\tau}(p_{\tau})
, let
(p,U)
be a representative of
p_{\tau}
with
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
small enough such that there is an arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
with the property that
\sigma(s(g))=[g]
, and set:

g\cdot p_{\tau}=[g\cdot\tau(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(s(g))\cdot p,V].

It follows from the fact that
\tau
is a
1
-cocycle that this is independent of the choice of representative
(p,U)
and the choice of arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
, and that this defines a
\mathcal{G}
-action. Furthermore, the fact that
\tau(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)
is Lagrangian implies that the action is Hamiltonian.
It is readily verified that the
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J_{\tau}:(S_{\tau},\omega_{\tau})\to M
is faithful multiplicity-free, has momentum image
\Delta
and that its isomorphism class does not depend on the choice of representative
\tau
or basis
\mathcal{U}
.
So, we can define:

\textrm{c}\cdot[J:(S,\omega)\to M]:=[J_{\tau}:(S_{\tau},\omega_{\tau})\to M].

We leave it for the reader to check that this defines an action of
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3
; freeness and transivity of the action in the etale case.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a basis as above. To see that the action is free, let
\tau
be a
1
-cocycle as above and suppose that:

[J_{\tau}:(S_{\tau},\omega_{\tau})\to M]=[J:(S,\omega)\to M],

meaning that there is an isomorphism of Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{\tau},\omega_{\tau})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S,\omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}

Then for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
we have an automorphism of Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-spaces:

(73)

\psi\circ j_{U}\in\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J,\omega)(U),

and it follows from
\mathcal{G}
-equivariance of
\psi
that the
0
-cochain:

c\in\check{C}^{0}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L}),

that assigns to an open
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
the Lagrangian section corresponding to (
73
) via (
47
) is a primitive for the
1
-cocycle
\tau
(here Theorem
2.1
applies by Lemma
1.47
). So, the class represented by
\tau
is trivial, which proves freeness. For transitivity, suppose
J_{1}:(S_{1},\omega_{1})\to M
and
J_{2}:(S_{2},\omega_{2})\to M
are faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces with momentum image
\Delta
. By Theorem
2.8
(which applies by Lemma
1.47
) there is, for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
, an isomorphism of Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-spaces:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{1}^{-1}(U),\omega_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{2}^{-1}(U),\omega_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{2}}

Furthermore, by Lemma
3.6
we can find for each arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
a Lagrangian bisection (
68
) lifting
\sigma
, chosen so as to satisfy the cocycle condition (
69
). By Proposition
3.7
, this choice provides us with symplectomorphisms:

\psi_{i,V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U}:(J_{i}^{-1}(U),\omega_{i})\to(J_{i}^{-1}(\sigma\cdot U),\omega_{i}),\quad\psi_{i,V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U}(p)=g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(J_{i}(p))\cdot p,

for each
i\in\{1,2\}
and each arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
. Now, consider the
1
-cochain:

\tau\in\check{C}^{1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})

that assigns to an arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
the Lagrangian section corresponding via (
47
) to the automorphism of the
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-space
J_{2}:(J_{2}^{-1}(U),\omega_{2})\to M
given by the composition:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{2}^{-1}(U),\omega_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{1}^{-1}(U),\omega_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{2}^{-1}(\sigma\cdot U),\omega_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{1}^{-1}(\sigma\cdot U),\omega_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{U}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1,V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{2,V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{V}}

From the cocycle condition (
69
) it follows that
\tau
is a
1
-cocycle. Now, consider the unique map
\psi:S_{1}\to S_{2,\tau}
defined by the property that
\psi|_{J_{1}^{-1}(U)}=j_{2,U}\circ\psi_{U}
for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
, with
j_{2,U}
as in (
72
). To see that this is well-defined, notice first that (since
\mathcal{U}
is a basis) this boils down to showing that if
V,U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
such that
U\subset V
, then
j_{2,V}\circ\psi_{V}
restricts to
j_{2,U}\circ\psi_{U}
on
J_{1}^{-1}(U)
, and this in turn readily follows from the fact that
\psi_{U}
is
\mathcal{T}
-equivariant and
g(V\hookleftarrow U)
takes values in
\mathcal{T}
, being a lift of the unit bisection of
\mathcal{B}
. Clearly,
\psi
is a symplectomorphism that intertwines
J_{1}
and
J_{2,\tau}
. Furthermore, it is
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant. To see this, let
g\in\mathcal{G}
with source
x\in M
and target
y\in M
, and let
p\in S_{1}
be such that
J_{1}(p)=x
. Let
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
be an arrow in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
such that
x\in U
and
\sigma(x)=[g]
. Then
J_{1}(p)=x\in U
and
J_{1}(g\cdot p)=y\in V
, so that:

g\cdot\psi(p)=g\cdot[\psi_{U}(p),U]=[g\cdot\tau(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(x)\cdot\psi_{U}(p),V],

whereas:

\psi(g\cdot p)=[\psi_{V}(g\cdot p),V].

To see that these are equal, notice that:

g\cdot\tau(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(x)\cdot\psi_{U}(p)=(g\cdot g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(x)^{-1})\cdot\psi_{V}(g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(x)\cdot p)=\psi_{V}(g\cdot p),

where the first step follows by spelling out the definition of
\tau(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)
and the second step follows from
\mathcal{T}
-equivariance of
\psi_{V}
and the observation that:

g\cdot g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(x)^{-1}\in\mathcal{T},

since both
g
and
g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(x)
project to
[g]\in\mathcal{B}
. So,
\psi
is an isomorphism of Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces, leading us to conclude that:

[J_{1}:(S_{1},\omega_{1})\to M]=\textrm{c}\cdot[J_{2}:(S_{2},\omega_{2})\to M],

where
\textrm{c}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
is the class represented by
\tau
. This proves transitivity of the action.
∎

Next, we address the proof of the first structure theorem in full generality, including the naturality. For this we will use the fact (due to
[
49
]
) that a symplectic Morita equivalence
((P,\omega_{P}),\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
between two symplectic groupoids
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
induces an equivalence:

(74)

P_{*}:\textsf{Ham}(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textsf{Ham}(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})

between the categories of Hamiltonian spaces of the respective symplectic groupoids, and hence it induces a bijection:

(75)

P_{*}:\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\text{ }\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of }\\
&\text{ }\text{ }\textrm{Hamiltonian $(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})$-spaces }\end{aligned}\right\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\text{ }\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\text{ }\text{ }\textrm{Hamiltonian $(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})$-spaces }\end{aligned}\right\}

This associates to a Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}
the Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
-space:

P_{*}(J):(P\ast_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}S,\omega_{PS})\to M_{2},

with
P\ast_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}S
the orbit space of the diagonal
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-action on
P\times_{M_{1}}S
,
\omega_{PS}
the symplectic form inherited from
\omega_{P}
and
\omega_{S}
, and
P_{*}(J)
the map induced by
\alpha_{2}\circ\textrm{pr}_{P}
. Our notation and conventions for this are as in
[
38
, Subsection 1.4.2]
, where further details can also be found. We leave it for the reader to verify:

Proposition 3.8
.

Given a symplectic Morita equivalence
((P,\omega_{P}),\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
between regular and proper symplectic groupoids
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
that relates a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}_{1}\subset\underline{M}_{1}
to
\underline{\Delta}_{2}\subset\underline{M}_{2}
, (
75
) restricts to a bijection:

(76)

P_{*}:\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of }\\
&\textrm{faithful multiplicity-free $(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})$-spaces}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\textrm{with momentum image $\Delta_{1}$}\end{aligned}\right\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{faithful multiplicity-free $(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})$-spaces}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\textrm{with momentum image $\Delta_{2}$}\end{aligned}\right\}

Proof of Theorem
3
; the torsor structure in full generality.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid. To define the action of
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
, choose a complete transversal
\Sigma
. By the etale case, we have a free and transitive action of
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma\cap\Delta},\mathcal{L}_{\Sigma\cap\Delta})
on the set of isomorphism classes of faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Sigma}
-spaces with momentum image
\Sigma\cap\Delta
. Via the isomorphism between
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma\cap\Delta},\mathcal{L}_{\Sigma\cap\Delta})
and
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
obtained by applying Corollary
3.3
to Example
1.36
, combined with the bijection:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\textrm{Isomorphism classes of }\\
&\textrm{{faithful multiplicity-free} $(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Sigma}$-spaces}\\
&\quad\text{ }\textrm{ with momentum image $\Sigma\cap\Delta$}\end{aligned}\right\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\textrm{Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{{faithful multiplicity-free} $(\mathcal{G},\Omega)$-spaces}\\
&\quad\quad\textrm{ with momentum image $\Delta$}\end{aligned}\right\}

induced (as in Proposition
3.8
) by the canonical symplectic Morita equivalence between
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
and its restriction to
\Sigma
(like in Example
1.36
), we obtain a free and transitive action of
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
on the right-hand set. In Proposition
3.9
we show that this does not depend on the choice of
\Sigma
.
∎

To conclude the proof of Theorem
3
, it remains to address the naturality, which is made precise in the proposition below. Suppose that we are given a symplectic Morita equivalence and Delzant subspaces as in Proposition
3.8
.
In view of Corollary
3.3
the integral affine Morita equivalence
(\underline{P},\underline{\alpha}_{1},\underline{\alpha}_{2})
associated to
((P,\omega_{P}),\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
(see Example
1.37
) induces an isomorphism:

(77)

\underline{P}_{*}:{H^{\bullet}}(\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}{H^{\bullet}}(\mathcal{B}_{2}|_{\Delta_{2}},\mathcal{L}_{2}).

Proposition 3.9
.

The action of
{H^{1}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
defined above is natural, in the following sense.

a)

It does not depend on the choice of complete transversal
\Sigma
.

b)

For any symplectic Morita equivalence as above, it holds that:

P_{*}({\emph{\textrm{c}}}\cdot[J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}])=\underline{P}_{*}({\emph{\textrm{c}}})\cdot P_{*}([J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}])

for each
\emph{\textrm{c}}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{1})
and each faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}
with momentum image
\Delta_{1}
.

Proof.

First, suppose that
\mathcal{B}
is etale. In this case, we defined the action for the choice of complete transversal
\Sigma:=M
, and it suffices to prove part
b
for this action with
\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}
etale. Let
\textrm{c}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{1})
and let
J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}
be a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
-space with momentum image
\Delta_{1}
. Choose a basis
\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{2}
of
\Delta_{2}
for which
\mathcal{L}_{2}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{2}}
is
\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{2}
-acyclic, such that each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{2}
admits a smooth local section
\underline{\zeta}_{U}:U_{M_{2}}\to\underline{P}
of
\underline{\alpha}_{2}
, defined on an open
U_{M_{2}}
in
M_{2}
satisfying
U=U_{M_{2}}\cap\Delta_{2}
, with the property that:

f_{U}:=\alpha_{1}\circ\zeta_{U}=\underline{\alpha_{1}}\circ\underline{\zeta}_{U}:U_{M_{2}}\to M_{1}

is a smooth open embedding. Here
\underline{\zeta}_{U}
denotes the induced section
U_{M_{2}}\to\underline{P}
of
\underline{\alpha}_{2}
. Fix such a collection
\{\zeta_{U}\mid U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{2}\}
. Since the Morita equivalence
(\underline{P},\underline{\alpha}_{1},\underline{\alpha}_{2})
is integral affine, pull-back along
f_{U}
induces an isomorphism of sheaves:

(f_{U})^{*}:\mathcal{L}_{1}|_{f_{U}(U)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{L}_{2}|_{U}

covering the homeomorphism
f_{U}:U\to f_{U}(U)
. In particular, for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{2}
and each
n>0
:

H^{n}(f_{U}(U),\mathcal{L}_{1})\cong H^{n}(U,\mathcal{L}_{2})=0,

and so we can extend
\{f_{U}(U)\mid U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{2}\}
to a basis
\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{1}
of
\Delta_{1}
for which
\mathcal{L}_{1}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{1}}
is
\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{1}
-acyclic.
The composition of isomorphisms:

(78)

\check{H}^{\bullet}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{1}}(\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{1})\xrightarrow{(\ref{eqn:cechtoderivedmorph:equivshf})}H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{1})\xrightarrow{\underline{P}_{*}}H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B}_{2}|_{\Delta_{2}},\mathcal{L}_{2})\xrightarrow{(\ref{eqn:cechtoderivedmorph:equivshf})}\check{H}^{\bullet}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{2}}(\mathcal{B}_{2}|_{\Delta_{2}},\mathcal{L}_{2})

is induced by the map of complexes:

\check{C}^{\bullet}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{1}}(\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{1})\to\check{C}^{\bullet}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{2}}(\mathcal{B}_{2}|_{\Delta_{2}},\mathcal{L}_{2}),

that associates to an
n
-cochain
\tau_{1}
the
n
-cochain
\tau_{2}
given by:

\tau_{2}(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n})=(f_{U_{n}})^{*}\tau_{1}(f_{U_{0}}(U_{0})\xleftarrow{f(\sigma_{1})}...\xleftarrow{f(\sigma_{n})}f_{U_{n}}(U_{n})),

where we denote by
f(\sigma_{j})
the unique continuous bisection of
\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{\Delta_{1}}
on
f_{U_{j}}(U_{j})
satisfying:

f(\sigma_{j})(f_{U_{j}}(x))\cdot\underline{\zeta}_{U_{j}}(x)=\underline{\zeta}_{U_{j-1}}((t\circ\sigma_{j})(x))\cdot\sigma_{j}(x),

for each
x\in U_{j}
.
Consider a
1
-cochain
\tau_{1}
representing the class
[\tau_{1}]\in\check{H}^{1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{1}}(\mathcal{B}_{1}|_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{1})
corresponding to
c
via (
67
). From the above it follows that the image of
[\tau_{1}]
under (
78
) is represented by the unique
1
-cocycle
\tau_{2}
with the property that:

(79)

\tau_{2}(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(x)=\varphi_{{\zeta_{U}(x)}}\left(\tau_{1}(f_{V}(V)\xleftarrow{f(\sigma)}f_{U}(U))(f_{U}(x))\right)

for each arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}_{2}}(\mathcal{B}_{2}|_{\Delta_{2}})
and each
x\in U
. Here
\varphi_{\zeta_{U}(x)}:(\mathcal{T}_{1})_{f_{U}(x)}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{T}_{2})_{x}
denotes the group isomorphism given by (
37
). Consider the map:

\displaystyle\psi:((P\ast_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}S)_{\tau_{2}},(\omega_{PS})_{\tau_{2}})

\displaystyle\to(P\ast_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}S_{\tau_{1}},\omega_{PS_{\tau_{1}}}),

\displaystyle\left[[p_{P},p_{S}],U\right]

\displaystyle\mapsto\left[{\zeta}_{U}(\alpha_{2}(p_{P})),[[{\zeta}_{U}(\alpha_{2}(p_{P})):p_{P}]\cdot p_{S},f_{U}(U)]\right],

where
[{\zeta}_{U}(\alpha_{2}(p_{P})):p_{P}]\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
denotes the unique element satisfying:

[{\zeta}_{U}(\alpha_{2}(p_{P})):p_{P}]\cdot p_{P}={\zeta}_{U}(\alpha_{2}(p_{P})).

Using (
79
) a somewhat tedious, but straightforward verification shows that
\psi
is well-defined,
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant and bijective. Moreover,
\psi
is readily verified to be smooth and symplectic (and hence immersive), so that for dimensional reasons it must be a symplectomorphism. So, it is an isomorphism of Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
-spaces. Therefore:

\displaystyle P_{*}({\textrm{c}}\cdot[J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}])

\displaystyle=[P_{*}(J_{\tau_{1}}):(P\ast_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}S_{\tau_{1}},\omega_{PS_{\tau_{1}}})\to M_{2}]

\displaystyle=[P_{*}(J)_{\tau_{2}}:((P\ast_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}S)_{\tau_{2}},(\omega_{PS})_{\tau_{2}})\to M_{2}]

\displaystyle=\underline{P}_{*}({\textrm{c}})\cdot P_{*}([J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}]),

as claimed. This concludes the proof of the etale case.
In the general setting both parts
a
and
b
readily follow from the case treated above by using the fact that the bijection (
76
) only depends on the isomorphism class of the Hamiltonian bibundle
((P,\omega_{P}),\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
and the fact that (
76
) and (
77
) are both functorial with respect to composition of symplectic Morita equivalences.
∎

3.3.
Proof of the splitting theorem

3.3.1.
Overview of the proof

In this section we prove Theorem
4
. In Subsection
3.3.2
we show that, if
\mathcal{B}
is etale, then for any Delzant subspace the semi-direct product groupoid appearing in Theorem
4
admits a faithful multiplicity-free space with momentum image the given Delzant subspace. In Subsection
3.3.3
we prove that a symplectic Morita equivalence between the restrictions of two proper and regular symplectic groupoids to Delzant subspaces induces an equivalence between their categories of faithful multiplicity-free spaces with momentum image equal to the respective Delzant subspaces. Together with the result of Subsection
3.3.2
, this leads to the implication from
b)
to
a)
in Theorem
4
. After this, in Subsection
3.3.4
we turn to the construction of the Lagrangian Dixmier-Douady class and explain the implication from
a)
to
c)
. The proof of the implication from
c)
to
b)
is the content of Subsection
3.3.5
.

3.3.2.
Existence of a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega)
-space

The aim of this subsection is to prove:

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows(M,\Lambda)
be an etale integral affine orbifold groupoid and
\underline{\Delta}
a Delzant subspace. Consider the corresponding invariant subspace
\Delta
of
(M,\Lambda)
(which is a Delzant subspace) and a faithful toric
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-space
J_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M
as in Theorem
2.11
. This action extends to a faithful multiplicity-free action along
J_{\Delta}:(S_{\Delta},\omega_{\Delta})\to M
of the semi-direct product symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}}^{*}\Omega_{\Lambda})
(where we view
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
as
\mathcal{B}
-space, as in Remark
1.21
).

For this we will use the following lemma, the proof of which is straightforward.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})\rightrightarrows M
be a symplectic torus bundle equipped with a symplectic action of an etale Lie groupoid
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows M
by fiberwise group automorphisms, where by the action being symplectic we mean that
(m^{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathcal{T}})^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}=\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
. Given a smooth map
J:(S,\omega)\to M
from a symplectic manifold into
M
, there is a bijection between:

i)

left Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-actions along
J:(S,\omega)\to M
,

ii)

pairs consisting of a left Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
-action along
J:(S,\omega)\to M
and a left symplectic
\mathcal{B}
-action along
J:(S,\omega)\to M
satisfying:

(80)

\gamma\cdot(t\cdot p)=(t\cdot\gamma^{-1})\cdot(\gamma\cdot p),

for all
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
and
p\in S
such that
s_{\mathcal{B}}(\gamma)=\pi_{\mathcal{T}}(t)=J(p)
,

via which the two actions are related as:

(\gamma,t)\cdot p=\gamma\cdot(t\cdot p),

for all
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}
,
t\in\mathcal{T}
and
p\in S
such that
s_{\mathcal{B}}(\gamma)=\pi_{\mathcal{T}}(t)=J(p)
. The same holds in the setting of symplectic groupoids with corners.

Proof of Proposition
3.10
.

First, we define an action of
\mathcal{B}
along
J_{\Delta}
, as follows. Given a
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}
and
p\in S_{\Delta}
such that
s_{\mathcal{B}}(\gamma)=J_{\Delta}(p)
, choose a smooth bisection
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{B}
defined on an open
U
in
M
around
J_{\Delta}(p)
such that
\sigma(J_{\Delta}(p))=\gamma
. Then
t\circ\sigma:U\cap\Delta\to(\sigma\cdot U)\cap\Delta
is a diffeomorphism of manifolds with corners such that
(t\circ\sigma)^{*}\Lambda=\Lambda|_{U\cap\Delta}
. So, we can consider the associated symplectomorphism (as in Theorem
2.2.1
):

(81)

(t\circ\sigma)_{*}:(J_{\Delta}^{-1}(U),\omega_{\Delta})\to(J_{\Delta}^{-1}(\sigma\cdot U),\omega_{\Delta}),

and set:

\gamma\cdot p:=(t\circ\sigma)_{*}(p).

It follows from the local dependence in Theorem
2.2.1
that this does not depend on the choice of bisection, because the germ of the bisection chosen above is uniquely determined by the fact that it maps
J_{\Delta}(p)
to
\gamma
(since
\mathcal{B}
is etale). Moreover, natural dependence and the compatibility of
(t\circ\sigma)_{*}
with
J_{\Delta}
imply that this indeed defines an action. From the facts that a bisection
\sigma
as above is an open embedding and (
81
) is a symplectomorphism it follows that this action is smooth and symplectic. The compatibilty of (
81
) with the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action implies that this
\mathcal{B}
-action and the
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
-action along
J_{\Delta}
satisfy the compatibility condition (
80
). So, in view of Lemma
3.11
these actions define a Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-action along
J_{\Delta}
. Because the
(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})
-action is faithful multiplicity-free, so is the
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}}^{*}\Omega_{\Lambda})
-action.
∎

3.3.3.
Morita equivalences between pre-symplectic groupoids with corners

Next, we will prove:

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})\rightrightarrows M_{1}
and
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})\rightrightarrows M_{2}
be regular and proper symplectic groupoids and let
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
and
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
be Delzant subspaces of
\underline{M}_{1}
and
\underline{M}_{2}
, respectively. A symplectic Morita equivalence
(P,\omega_{P},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
between the restriction of
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
to
\Delta_{1}
and the restriction of
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
to
\Delta_{2}
induces an equivalence between the category of faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
-spaces with momentum map image
\Delta_{1}
and that of faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
-spaces with momentum map image
\Delta_{2}
.

Proof.

When
\Delta_{1}=M_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}=M_{2}
, the equivalence is given by the restriction of (
74
). The construction of this equivalence extends to general Delzant subspaces, as follows. To define the functor from left to right, let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
-space. In view of Corollary
B.16
c
, the fiber product
P\times_{\Delta_{1}}S
is an embedded submanifold of
P\times S

without
corners, since
S
has no corners. So, since the diagonal
\mathcal{G}_{1}
-action along
\alpha_{1}\circ\textrm{pr}_{P}:P\times_{\Delta_{1}}S\to M_{1}
is smooth, free and proper, the quotient:

P\ast_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}S:=\frac{(P\times_{\Delta_{1}}S)}{\mathcal{G}_{1}}

is naturally a manifold without corners. Consider the left action of
\mathcal{G}_{2}
along:

P_{*}(J):P\ast_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}S\to M_{2},\quad[p_{P},p_{S}]\mapsto\alpha_{2}(p_{P}),

given by:

g\cdot[p_{P},p_{S}]=[p_{P}\cdot g^{-1},p_{S}].

The symplectic form
(-\omega_{P})\oplus\omega_{S}
descends to a symplectic form
\omega_{PS}
on
P\ast_{\mathcal{G}_{1}}S
and the
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
-action along
P_{*}(J)
is Hamiltonian with respect to this. It is straightforward to verify that this
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
-space is faithful multiplicity-free. Since this construction is clearly functorial, it yields a functor
P_{*}
. An entirely analogous construction from right to left gives an inverse functor.
∎

With this at hand, we can prove one implication in the splitting theorem.

Proof of Theorem
4
: implication from
b)
to
a)
.

The restriction of the pre-symplectic groupoid:

(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}}^{*}\Omega_{\Lambda})

to a complete transversal
\Sigma
for
\mathcal{G}
is canonically isomorphic to the symplectic groupoid:

(\mathcal{B}_{|_{\Sigma}}\Join\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\Sigma}},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\Sigma}}}^{*}\Omega_{\Lambda_{\Sigma}})

associated to the integral affine etale orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma}\rightrightarrows(\Sigma,\Lambda_{\Sigma})
(cf. Example
1.36
). Hence, by Proposition
3.10
there exists a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{B}\ltimes\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}}^{*}\Omega_{\Lambda})|_{\Sigma}
-space with momentum map image
\Delta\cap\Sigma
. If
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
is pre-symplectic Morita equivalent to
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}}^{*}\Omega_{\Lambda})|_{\Delta}
, it follows from Proposition
B.24
and Remark
B.23
that
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
is symplectic Morita equivalent to
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}}^{*}\Omega_{\Lambda})|_{\Delta\cap\Sigma}
. So, in view of Proposition
3.12
, there exists a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with momentum map image
\Delta
as well.
∎

3.3.4.
The Lagrangian Dixmier-Douady class

We will now extend the definition of the Lagrangian Dixmier-Douady class of a regular and proper symplectic groupoid in
[
7
]
to a version of this class for the restriction of the symplectic groupoid to a Delzant subspace. Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be such a symplectic groupoid and
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
a Delzant subspace. First suppose that
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale. Fix a basis
\mathcal{U}
of
\Delta
for which
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
is
\mathcal{U}
-acyclic and consider the wide subcategory
\textsf{E}_{\text{lift}}
of
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
consisting of those arrows
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
that admit a Lagrangian bisection
U\to(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
lifting the underlying bisection
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
.

Proposition 3.13
.

This is a category approximating the etale groupoid
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
(see Subsection
3.1.2
).

Proof.

The first axiom holds because the unit section of a symplectic groupoid is Lagrangian, the second axiom is immediate and the third axiom follows from the fact that for any
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}
the quotient map
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\to\mathcal{B}
admits a local Lagrangian section defined around
\gamma
, since it is a proper Lagrangian fibration (in the sense of Example
2
).
∎

In view of this,
H^{2}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
is isomorphic to
\check{H}^{2}(\textsf{E}_{\text{lift}},\mathcal{L})
via (
67
). For each arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
in
\textsf{E}_{\text{lift}}
, choose a Lagrangian bisection:

(82)

g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U):U\to(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}

lifting
\sigma:U\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
. Consider the
2
-cochain
c\in\check{C}^{2}(\textsf{E}_{\text{lift}},\mathcal{L})
that assigns to a pair of composable arrows
U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}U_{1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{2}}U_{2}
the unique Lagrangian section
c(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}U_{1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{2}}U_{2})\in\mathcal{L}(U_{2})
such that:

g(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}U_{1})g(U_{1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{2}}U_{2})=g(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}}U_{2})c(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}U_{1}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{2}}U_{2}).

As is readily verified, this defines a
2
-cocycle whose cohomology class
[c]\in\check{H}^{2}(\textsf{E}_{\text{lift}},\mathcal{L})
does not depend on the choice of lifts (
82
). Moreover, this cohomology class vanishes if and only if there is a choice of such lifts for which the cocycle condition (
69
) is satisfied.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid and let
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
be a Delzant subspace. The
Lagrangian Dixmier-Douady class
:

(83)

\textrm{c}_{2}(\mathcal{G},\Omega,\underline{\Delta})\in H^{2}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})

is defined as follows. When
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale, it is the cohomology class corresponding via (
67
) to the class in
\check{H}^{2}(\textsf{E}_{\text{lift}},\mathcal{L})
represented by the
2
-cocycle defined above. In general, consider a complete transversal
\Sigma
and define (
83
) to be the class corresponding to
\textrm{c}_{2}((\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Sigma},\underline{\Delta})
via the isomorphism between
H^{2}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
and
H^{2}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta\cap\Sigma},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta\cap\Sigma})
induced (as in Corollary
3.3
) by the canonical Morita equivalence between
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
and its restriction to
\Sigma
.

This does not depend on the choice of complete transversal.

Proof of Theorem
4
: implication from
a)
to
c)
.

After restricting to a complete transversal
\Sigma
, the proof reduces to the case in which
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale. The etale case follows from Lemma
3.6
.
∎

3.3.5.
End of the proof: constructing a principal Hamiltonian bundle out of a Lagrangian cocycle

To prove the forward implication in the splitting theorem, we first show the following.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid for which the associated orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale and let
\underline{\Delta}
be a Delzant space of
\underline{M}
. Further, let
\mathcal{U}
be a basis of the corresponding invariant subspace
\Delta
of
M
and suppose that for each arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
there is a Lagrangian bisection (
82
) lifting
\sigma
, such that the collection of these Lagrangian bisections satisfies the cocycle condition (
69
). Then there is a principal Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
-bundle ‘fibered over
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
’. That is, there are:

•

a
(\mathcal{G}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
-bibundle
(P,\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
(of Lie groupoids with corners),

•

a symplectic form
\omega_{P}
on
P
,

•

a smooth map
j:P\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
,

that form a map of bi-bundles:
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
\Delta
(P,\omega_{P})
\Delta
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
\Delta
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
\Delta
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
pr

Id

Id

Id
j\quad\quad
\quad\text{ }\beta_{1}
\beta_{2}

\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }\text{ }t_{\mathcal{B}}
s_{\mathcal{B}}

in which the lower Morita equivalence is the identity equivalence, the upper left bundle is principal and the action is Hamiltonian, whereas the upper right action is free and symplectic (meaning that
(m_{P})^{*}\omega_{P}=(\textrm{pr}_{P})^{*}\omega_{P}
, where
m_{P},\textrm{pr}_{P}:P\rtimes\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}\to P
denote the action and projection maps).

Proof.

Consider the topological space:

(84)

\bigsqcup_{U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}s_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(U)

where
s_{\mathcal{G}}:\mathcal{G}\to M
denotes the source map. Given two elements
(g_{1},U_{1})
and
(g_{2},U_{2})
of (
84
), we write
(g_{1},U_{1})\hookleftarrow(g_{2},U_{2})
if
U_{2}\subset U_{1}
and:

g(U_{1}\hookleftarrow U_{2})(J(p_{2}))\cdot g_{2}=g_{1},

where
U_{2}\hookleftarrow U_{1}
denotes the arrow in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
with underlying bisection the unit map. This relation is reflexive and transitive (by the cocycle condition (
69
)), but not necessarily symmetric. The equivalence relation generated by this relation is given by:
(g_{1},U_{1})\sim(g_{2},U_{2})
if and only if there is a pair
(g_{12},U_{12})
, with
U_{12}\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
and
s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{12})\in U_{12}\subset U_{1}\cap U_{2}
, such that
(g_{1},U_{1})\hookleftarrow(g_{12},U_{12})
and
(g_{12},U_{12})\hookrightarrow(g_{2},U_{2})
. Indeed, this relation is clearly reflexive, symmetric and contains the first relation. To prove transitivity, suppose that
(g_{1},U_{1})\sim(g_{2},U_{2})
and
(g_{2},U_{2})\sim(g_{3},U_{3})
. Then there are
(g_{12},U_{12})
and
(g_{23},U_{23})
with
U_{12},U_{23}\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
,
s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{12})\in U_{12}\subset U_{1}\cap U_{2}
and
s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{23})\in U_{23}\subset U_{2}\cap U_{3}
, such that:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{1},U_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{2},U_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{3},U_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{12},U_{12})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(g_{23},U_{23})}

Let
U_{123}\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
be such that
s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2})\in U_{123}\subset U_{12}\cap U_{23}
and consider the element

g_{123}:=g(U_{2}\hookleftarrow U_{123})(s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2}))^{-1}\cdot g_{2}\in s_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(U_{123}),

which is well-defined since the source and target of
g(U_{2}\hookleftarrow U_{123})(s_{\mathcal{G}}(g_{2}))
coincide, being a lift of the unit map of
\mathcal{B}
. It follows from the cocycle condition (
69
) that
(g_{12},U_{12})\hookleftarrow(g_{123},U_{123})
and
(g_{123},U_{123})\hookrightarrow(g_{23},U_{23})
, and hence that
(g_{1},U_{1})\hookleftarrow(g_{123},U_{123})
and
(g_{123},U_{123})\hookrightarrow(g_{3},U_{3})
. This shows that
(g_{1},U_{1})\sim(g_{3},U_{3})
, which proves transitivity. Now, consider the quotient space:

P:=\frac{\left(\bigsqcup_{U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}s_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(U)\right)}{\sim}.

From the explicit description of the equivalence relation it is clear that for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
the map:

(85)

s_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(U)\to P,\quad g\mapsto[g,U]

is an injection. Since for each inclusion
V\hookleftarrow U
the map:

(s_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(U),\Omega)\to(s_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(U),\Omega),\quad h\mapsto g(V\hookleftarrow U)(s_{\mathcal{G}}(h))\cdot h

is a symplectomorphism (which follows from the same type of arguments as for Proposition
3.7
a
), there is a unique structure of symplectic manifold with corners on the topological space
P
with the property that for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
the injection (
85
) is a symplectomorphism onto an open in
P
(with respect to the symplectic form
\Omega
on
s_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(U)
). Let
\omega_{P}
be the corresponding symplectic form on
P
. Next, notice that the projection
\textrm{pr}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{B}
and the target and source map of
\mathcal{G}
induce tame submersions:

j:P\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\quad\quad\beta_{1},\beta_{2}:P\to\Delta.

Furthermore, the canonical left Hamiltonian action of
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
along its target maps induces a left Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
-action along
\beta_{1}:(P,\omega_{P})\to\Delta
, given by:

g\cdot[h,U]=[g\cdot h,U].

On the other hand,
\mathcal{B}
acts along
\beta_{2}
from the right, as follows. Given
[h,V]\in P
and
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}
such that
\beta_{2}([h,V])=t_{\mathcal{B}}(\gamma)
, let
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
be an arrow in
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
such that
s(\gamma)\in U
and
\sigma(s_{\mathcal{B}}(\gamma))=\gamma
, and set:

[h,V]\cdot\gamma=[h\cdot g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)(s_{\mathcal{B}}(\gamma)),U].

It follows from the cocycle condition (
69
) that this depends neither on the choice of arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
nor on that of the representative of
[h,V]\in P
, and that this defines an action. Furthermore, the fact that
g(V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U)
is Lagrangian implies that this
\mathcal{B}
-action is symplectic. These two actions and the map
j:P\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
define a fibered principal Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
-bundle over
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4
: implication from
c)
to
b)
.

Restricting to a complete transversal for
\mathcal{G}
reduces the proof to the case in which
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
is etale. In that case, the vanishing of the Lagrangian Dixmier-Douady class implies that there is a basis
\mathcal{U}
of
\Delta
as in Proposition
3.15
, and so there is a fibered principal Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
-bundle over
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
. From this we can construct the desired Morita equivalence, as follows. Consider the left Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})|_{\Delta}
-action along
\beta_{2}:(P,\omega_{P})\to\Delta
given by:

t\ast p=(j(p)\cdot t)\cdot p,

where the second action on the right denotes the
\mathcal{T}
-action induced by the
\mathcal{G}
-action on
P
, and the left symplectic
\mathcal{B}
-action along
\beta_{2}:(P,\omega_{P})\to\Delta
given by:

\gamma\cdot p=p\cdot\gamma^{-1}.

These form a pair as in Lemma
3.11
. So, they encode a left Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})|_{\Delta}
-action along
\beta_{2}
. This commutes with the left
\mathcal{G}
-action since both of the above actions do so. Furthermore, the action is free and its orbits coincide with the
\beta_{1}
-fibers. Passing to a right action along
\beta_{2}
via the groupoid inversion of
\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}
and the groupoid automorphism of
\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}
that maps
(\gamma,t)
to
(\gamma,t^{-1})
(which are both anti-symplectic with respect to
\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
) we obtain a right Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})|_{\Delta}
-action along
\beta_{2}
, which completes the left principal Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}
-bundle
\beta_{2}:(P,\omega_{P})\to\Delta
to the desired symplectic Morita equivalence. ∎

Remark 3.16
.

This proof shows that, in the language of
[
7
]
, the existence of a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with momentum image
\Delta
is also equivalent to the condition that the symplectic gerbe represented by the symplectic central extension:

(86)

1\to(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})|_{\Delta}\to(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}\to 1

is trivial. This means that (
86
) is Morita equivalent as pre-symplectic central extension to the trivial such extension:

1\to(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})|_{\Delta}\to(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})|_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}\to 1.

Here Morita equivalence of pre-symplectic extensions is meant in the sense of
[
7
]
, extended to the setting with corners using Definition
B.22
.

4.
The second and third structure theorems

In this part we address the second and third structure theorem. These theorems result naturally from a more classical approach to the classification of faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces than that taken in the previous part, which only involves the cohomology of sheaves on the topological space
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
, rather than that of equivariant sheaves. The key ingredients in this approach are a generalization of Theorem
2.1
for the sheaf of
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant automorphisms (Theorem
4.1
) and the ext-invariant, both of which are introduced in Section
4.1
. In that section we also prove the second structure theorem. The third structure theorem is proved in Section
4.2
.

4.1.
The ext-invariant and the second structure theorem

4.1.1.
The sheaves of automorphisms and invariant isotropic sections

The main point of this subsection is to prove the following generalization of Theorem
2.1
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid with associated orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
and let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with associated Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}:=\underline{J}(\underline{S})
. There is an isomorphism of sheaves on
\underline{\Delta}
—defined as in (
45
) —between the sheaf
\underline{\textrm{Aut}}_{\mathcal{G}}(J,\omega)
of automorphisms of the
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
and the sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{\Delta}
of
\mathcal{B}
-invariant isotropic sections of
\mathcal{T}|_{\Delta}
(as in Definition
4.2
below).

The sheaf
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{\Delta}
appearing in this theorem is defined as follows.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{B}\rightrightarrows(M,\Lambda)
be an integral affine orbifold groupoid and let
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
be a Delzant subspace. We denote by:

•

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda})
the sheaf on
\Delta
of smooth local section of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}|_{\Delta}\to\Delta
,

•

\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda})
the sheaf on
\underline{\Delta}
that assigns to an open
\underline{U}
the subgroup of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})(U)
consisting of
\mathcal{B}
-invariant sections, with
U
the invariant open in
\Delta
corresponding to
\underline{U}
,

•

\underline{\mathcal{L}}:=\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{\Delta}
the subsheaf of
\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda})
consisting of those sections that are isotropic (in the same sense as in Example
3.1
).

Here
\mathcal{B}
-invariance of a section
\tau:U\to\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
means that for all
\gamma:x\to y
in
\mathcal{B}
:

\tau(y)\cdot\gamma=\tau(x).

When the integral affine orbifold is that associated to a regular and proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
, we will usually view the sheaves in Definition
4.2
as consisting of sections
{\tau}:U\to\mathcal{T}
via the isomorphism (
34
), without further notice.

Remark 4.3
.

As for any topological groupoid, there is a canonical functor:

(87)

(q_{\Delta})_{*}:\textsf{Sh}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})\to\textsf{Sh}(\underline{\Delta})

from the abelian category of
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
-sheaves into that of sheaves on the topological space
\underline{\Delta}
. This is the push-forward along the canonical map of topological groupoids
q_{\Delta}:\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}\to U(\underline{\Delta})
into the unit groupoid over
\underline{\Delta}
. Explicitly,
(q_{\Delta})_{*}
associates to a
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
-sheaf
\mathcal{A}
the sheaf on
\underline{\Delta}
that assigns to an open
\underline{U}
the group of
\mathcal{B}
-invariant sections of
\mathcal{A}
over the invariant open
U
in
\Delta
corresponding to
\underline{U}
. Here
\mathcal{B}
-invariance of a section
a\in\mathcal{A}(U)
means that for all
\gamma:x\to y
in
\mathcal{B}
:

[a]_{y}\cdot\gamma=[a]_{x}\in\mathcal{A}_{x}.

The sheaves in Definition
4.2
arise like this from the respective
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
-sheaves in Example
3.1
.

To explain the isomorphism in Theorem
4.1
, note that there is an injective map of sheaves:

(88)

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})\to\textrm{Aut}_{\mathcal{T}}(J),\quad{\tau}\mapsto\psi_{{\tau}},

defined as in (
45
), using the induced
\mathcal{T}
-action. Proposition
2.6
generalizes (by the same arguments):

Proposition 4.4
.

A section
{\tau}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})(U)
is isotropic if and only if
\psi_{{\tau}}
is a symplectomorphism.

It further holds that:

Proposition 4.5
.

A section
{\tau}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})(U)
is
\mathcal{B}|_{U}
-invariant if and only if
\psi_{{\tau}}
is
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
-equivariant.

Proof.

Notice that, for any arrow
g:x\to y
in
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
and any
p\in J^{-1}({x})
:

(89)

({\tau}({y})\cdot{[g]})\cdot p={g^{-1}}\cdot\psi_{{\tau}}({g}\cdot p).

It is clear from this that if
{\tau}
is
\mathcal{B}|_{U}
-invariant, then
\psi_{{\tau}}
is
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
-equivariant. On the other hand, the converse follows from (
89
) by a density argument. Indeed, suppose that
\psi_{{\tau}}
is
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
-equivariant. By (
89
): if
\mathcal{T}
acts freely at
p
, then
{\tau(y)\cdot[g]}={\tau(x)}
. Now, let an arrow
g
as above be given and pick
p\in J^{-1}({x})
. Let
{\sigma}
be a smooth section of
{s:\mathcal{G}|_{U}\to U}
, defined on an open
V
in
U
around
{x}
, such that
{\sigma(x)}=g
. Since
\mathcal{T}
acts freely on a dense subset, there is a sequence of
p_{n}\in J^{-1}(V)
at which
\mathcal{T}
acts freely, such that
p_{n}\to p
. Set
g_{n}={\sigma}(J(p_{n})):x_{n}\to y_{n}
. By the discussion above we have
{\tau(y_{n})\cdot[g_{n}]}={\tau}(x_{n})
. So, taking
n\to\infty
, we find
{\tau(y)\cdot[g]}={\tau(x)}
.
∎

In view of these propositions, the map (
88
) induces injective maps of sheaves on
\underline{\Delta}
:

(90)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{T})

\displaystyle\to\underline{\textrm{Aut}}_{\mathcal{G}}(J),

(91)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{\mathcal{L}}}_{\Delta}

\displaystyle\to\underline{\textrm{Aut}}_{\mathcal{G}}(J,\omega).

where
\underline{\textrm{Aut}}_{\mathcal{G}}(J)
assigns to an open
\underline{U}
the group of
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant self-diffeomorphisms of
J^{-1}(U)
that preserve
J
and
\underline{\textrm{Aut}}_{\mathcal{G}}(J,\omega)
is the subsheaf consisting those diffeomorphisms that preserve
\omega
. Theorem
4.1
states that (
91
) is an isomorphism of sheaves. To prove this, by Proposition
4.4
it is enough to show:

Theorem 4.6
.

The map (
90
) is an isomorphism of sheaves.

In the remainder of this subsection we give a proof of this, by reducing to Theorem
2.7
via:

Proposition 4.7
.

An integral affine Morita equivalence between two integral affine orbifold groupoids
\mathcal{B}_{1}\rightrightarrows(M_{1},\Lambda_{1})
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}\rightrightarrows(M_{2},\Lambda_{2})
that relates a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}_{1}\subset\underline{M}_{1}
with
\underline{\Delta}_{2}\subset\underline{M}_{2}
induces an isomorphism of sheaves:

\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\Delta_{1}}(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{1}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}_{\Delta_{2}}(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{2}})

covering the induced homeomorphism between
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
and
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
. This restricts to an isomorphism:

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{\Delta_{1}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{\Delta_{2}}.

The proof of this proposition is straightforward (cf. the proof of Corollary
3.3
).

Example 4.8
.

In the setting of Example
1.36
there is a
\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma}
-equivariant isomorphism:

(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda},\Omega_{\Lambda})|_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\Sigma}},\Omega_{\Lambda_{\Sigma}}),\quad[(x,\alpha)]\mapsto[(x,\alpha|_{T_{x}\Sigma})],

For that example the isomorphisms of sheaves in Proposition
4.7
are given by restriction of sections.

Example 4.9
.

For an integral affine Morita equivalence arising from a symplectic Morita equivalence as in Example
1.37
, via (
34
) we obtain an isomorphism:

\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{\Delta_{1}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{\Delta_{2}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{2})

from Proposition
4.7
, that restricts to an isomorphism:

\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{\Delta_{1}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{\Delta_{2}}.

This associates to
\tau_{1}\in\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{\Delta_{1}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{1})(\underline{U}_{1})
the section
\tau_{2}\in\underline{\mathcal{C}}_{\Delta_{2}}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T}_{2})(\underline{U}_{2})
given by:

\tau_{2}(x_{2})=\varphi_{p}(\tau_{1}(x_{1})),

where
p\in P
is any choice of element such that
\alpha_{2}(p)=x_{2}
,
x_{1}:=\alpha_{1}(p)
and
\varphi_{p}
is as in (
37
).

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

Let
x_{0}\in\Delta
and let
\psi\in\underline{\textrm{Aut}}_{\mathcal{G}}(J)(\underline{U})
for some
\mathcal{G}
-invariant open
U
around
x_{0}
in
\Delta
. We have to show that there is a
\mathcal{B}
-invariant smooth section
{\tau}
, defined in a
\mathcal{G}
-invariant open neighbourhood of
x_{0}
in
\Delta
, such that the germ
\psi_{{\tau}}
at
x_{0}
coincides with that of
\psi
. To find such a section, choose a transversal
\Sigma
for
\mathcal{B}
through
x
such that
\Delta\cap\Sigma\subset U
. As in the proof of Theorem
2
and Proposition
1.43
, the given
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J
restricts to a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\Sigma}
-space:

(92)

J_{\Sigma}:(J^{-1}(\Sigma),\omega|_{J^{-1}(\Sigma)})\to\Sigma.

After possibly shrinking
U
, we can assume that
U
is the
\mathcal{G}
-saturation of
\Sigma\cap\Delta
. Combining Example
4.8
and Example
4.9
, we find that any smooth
\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma}
-invariant section
{{\tau}}_{\Sigma}:\Delta\cap\Sigma\to\mathcal{T}
extends uniquely to a smooth
\mathcal{B}
-invariant section
{\tau}:U\to\mathcal{T}
, defined by the property that for any arrow
g:x\to y
in
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
with
{y}\in\Sigma
:

{\tau(x)}={{\tau}_{\Sigma}(y)\cdot[g]}.

Further, notice that if
\psi|_{J^{-1}(\Sigma)}=\psi_{{{\tau}}_{\Sigma}}
, then
\psi=\psi_{{\tau}}
. So, to conclude the proof of the theorem it remains to find (after possibly shrinking
\Sigma
) a smooth
\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma}
-invariant section
{{\tau}}_{\Sigma}:\Delta\cap\Sigma\to\mathcal{T}
with the property that
\psi|_{J^{-1}(\Sigma)}=\psi_{{{\tau}}_{\Sigma}}
. Since
(\mathcal{T},\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})|_{\Sigma}
is a symplectic torus bundle and (by Lemma
1.47
) its action along (
92
) is faithful toric, it follows from Theorem
2.7
that, after possibly shrinking
\Sigma
, we can find a smooth section
{{\tau}}_{\Sigma}:\Delta\cap\Sigma\to\mathcal{T}
with the property that
\psi|_{J^{-1}(\Sigma)}=\psi_{{{\tau}}_{\Sigma}}
. In view of Proposition
4.5
, this section must also be
\mathcal{B}|_{\Sigma}
-invariant.
∎

4.1.2.
The point-wise ext-invariant and proof of the second structure theorem

The proof of Theorem
5
will be analogous to that for the case of symplectic torus bundles (Theorem
2.8
). The remaining ingredient for this is the following generalization of Proposition
2.9
.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid and suppose that we are given a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-actions along
J_{1}:(S_{1},\omega_{1})\to M
and
J_{2}:(S_{2},\omega_{2})\to M
. Further, let
x\in J_{1}(S_{1})\cap J_{2}(S_{2})
. Then there is a
\mathcal{G}
-invariant open neighbourhood
U
of
x\in M
and a
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant symplectomorphism:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{1}^{-1}(U),\omega_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(J_{2}^{-1}(U),\omega_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{2}}

if and only if both of the following hold.

i)

The germs at
x
of
J_{1}(S_{1})
and
J_{2}(S_{2})
are equal.

ii)

The ext-classes
e(J_{1})_{x}
and
e(J_{2})_{x}
at
x
are equal.

The ext-classes at
x
in this proposition are certain elements of
I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})
(as in Definition
1.4
). To define these, let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid and
J:(S,\omega)\to M
a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space. For
x\in M
and
p\in J^{-1}(x)
, consider the canonical extensions:

(93)

\displaystyle 1\to\mathcal{T}_{p}\to\mathcal{G}_{p}\to\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{p}}\to 1,

(94)

\displaystyle 1\to\mathcal{T}_{x}\to\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}\to 1.

Any
1
-cocycle
{c}_{p}:\mathcal{G}_{p}\to\mathcal{T}_{p}
that restricts to the identity map on
\mathcal{T}_{p}
extends uniquely to a
1
-cocycle
{c}_{x}:\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{T}_{x}
that restricts to the identity map on
\mathcal{T}_{x}
. Indeed, let
\varGamma_{{c}}
be the subgroup of
\mathcal{G}_{p}
corresponding to
{c}_{p}
via the bijection in Proposition
1.11
, applied to (
93
). Then
\varGamma_{{c}}\to\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{p}}
is an isomorphism. So, since
\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{p}}\to\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is an isomorphism (Proposition
1.43
b
),
\varGamma_{{c}}
is also a subgroup of
\mathcal{G}_{x}
with the property that
\varGamma_{{c}}\to\varGamma_{\mathcal{G}_{x}}
is an isomorphism. Therefore
\varGamma_{{c}}
corresponds to a
1
-cocycle
{c}_{x}:\mathcal{G}_{x}\to\mathcal{T}_{x}
via the bijection in Proposition
1.11
, applied to (
94
). This is the desired cocycle extending
{c}_{p}
. The association of
{c}_{p}
to
{c}_{x}
descends to a map:

(95)

I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{p},\mathcal{T}_{p})\to I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x}),

as is readily verified. Since the symplectic normal representation
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})
is maximally toric (Proposition
1.43
a
), we can consider its ext-class (as in Definition
1.13
):

(96)

e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})\in I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{p},\mathcal{T}_{p}).

Proposition 4.11
.

For any
p_{1},p_{2}\in J^{-1}(x)
, the ext-classes
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{1}},\omega_{p_{1}})
and
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{2}},\omega_{p_{2}})
are mapped to the same class in
I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})
.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid and suppose that we are given a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-action along
J:(S,\omega)\to M
with momentum image
\Delta:=J(S)
. The
ext-class
of the action
at a point

x\in\Delta
:

e(J)_{x}\in I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x}),

is the image under the map (
95
) of the ext-class (
96
) at any point
p\in S
in the fiber of
J
over
x
.

Proof of Proposition
4.11
.

Since the fibers of
J
coincide with the
\mathcal{T}
-orbits, it holds that
p_{2}=t\cdot p_{1}
for some
t\in\mathcal{T}_{x}
. Writing
p:=p_{1}
, we have
\mathcal{G}_{t\cdot p}=t\mathcal{G}_{p}t^{-1}
and the pair:

(C_{t},\psi):(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})\to(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{t\cdot p},\omega_{t\cdot p}),\quad[v]\mapsto[\textrm{d}(m_{S})_{(t,p)}(0,v)],

is an equivalence of symplectic representations, where
C_{t}:\mathcal{G}_{p}\to\mathcal{G}_{t\cdot p}
denotes conjugation by
t
. Appealing to Lemma
1.15
, it follows that:

e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{t\cdot p},\omega_{t\cdot p})=(C_{t})_{*}(e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})).

As is readily verified, the square:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{p},\mathcal{T}_{p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{t\cdot p},\mathcal{T}_{t\cdot p})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{t})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{t})_{*}}

commutes. By Lemma
4.13
below the lower arrow is the identity map. So, the respective images of
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})
and
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{t\cdot p},\omega_{t\cdot p})
under the vertical maps are equal.
∎

Here we used the lemma below, which is readily verified.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
H
be an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group with identity component
T
. For any
h\in H
, conjugation by
h
induces the identity map in
H^{1}(H,T)
.

Having defined the invariants appearing in Proposition
4.10
, we now turn to its proof.

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

The implication from left to right is straightforward to verify. For the other implication, let
x\in J_{1}(S_{1})\cap J_{2}(S_{2})
such that the germs at
x
of
J_{1}(S_{1})
and
J_{2}(S_{2})
coincide and such that
e(J_{1})_{x}=e(J_{2})_{x}
. Since
\underline{J}:\underline{S}\to\underline{M}
is a topological embedding, every invariant
\mathcal{G}
-invariant open in
S
is of the form
J^{-1}(U)
where
U
is some
\mathcal{G}
-invariant open in
M
. So, in view of Theorem
2.10
, it remains to show that there are
p_{1}\in J_{1}^{-1}(x)
and
p_{2}\in J_{2}^{-1}(x)
such that
\mathcal{G}_{p_{1}}=\mathcal{G}_{p_{2}}
and the symplectic representations
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{1}},\omega_{p_{1}})
and
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p_{2}},\omega_{p_{2}})
are isomorphic. Fix any
p\in J_{1}^{-1}(x)
and
q\in J_{2}^{-1}(x)
. The same argument as in the proof of Proposition
2.9
shows that
\mathcal{T}_{p}=\mathcal{T}_{q}
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p}}=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{q}}
. Let
\varGamma_{p}
and
\varGamma_{q}
be the respective subgroups of
\mathcal{G}_{p}
and
\mathcal{G}_{q}
corresponding (as in Proposition
1.11
) to a choice of
1
-cocycles representing
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})
and
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{q},\omega_{q})
. By assumption,
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})
and
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{q},\omega_{q})
are mapped to the same cohomology class in
I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})
. So, there is a
t\in\mathcal{T}_{x}
such that:

(97)

t\varGamma_{p}t^{-1}=\varGamma_{q}.

Since any element of
\mathcal{G}_{p}
can be written as product of an element of
\varGamma_{p}
and one of
\mathcal{T}_{p}
, while any element of
\mathcal{G}_{q}
can be written as product of an element of
\varGamma_{q}
and one of
\mathcal{T}_{q}
, it follows that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{t\cdot p}

\displaystyle=t\mathcal{G}_{p}t^{-1}

\displaystyle=(t\varGamma_{p}t^{-1})\mathcal{T}_{p}

\displaystyle=\varGamma_{q}\mathcal{T}_{q}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{G}_{q}.

Moreover, as in the proof of Proposition
4.11
, we find that
\mathcal{G}_{t\cdot p}=t\mathcal{G}_{p}t^{-1}
and the subgroup
t\varGamma_{p}t^{-1}
of
\mathcal{G}_{t\cdot p}
corresponds to the
1
-cocycle representing the cohomology class
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{t\cdot p},\omega_{t\cdot p})
. In light of (
97
), this is the same as the
1
-cocycle representing the class
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{q},\omega_{q})
, hence:

e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{t\cdot p},\omega_{t\cdot p})=e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{q},\omega_{q}).

In combination with the fact that:

\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{t\cdot p}}=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p}}=\Delta_{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{q}}.

we conclude from Theorem
1.3
that
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{t\cdot p},\omega_{t\cdot p})
and
(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{q},\omega_{q})
are isomorphic as symplectic representations. Thus,
p_{1}:=t\cdot p\in J_{1}^{-1}(x)
and
p_{2}:=q\in J_{2}^{-1}(x)
are as required.
∎

As mentioned in the introduction, the ext-invariant appearing in Theorem
5
is a global section of a certain sheaf (
8
) on
\underline{\Delta}
(the ext-sheaf) with stalk at the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{x}\in\underline{\Delta}
through
x\in\Delta
given by
I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})
. To understand the proof of Theorem
5
, it is not necessary to know the precise definition of the ext-sheaf or the ext-invariant. Rather, the only relevant fact for the proof is that the germ at
x
of the ext-invariant of
J
is the ext-class
e(J)_{x}
, so that two faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces with momentum image
\Delta
have the same ext-invariant if and only if they have the same ext-class at every
x\in\Delta
. With this in mind, we give the proof of Theorem
5
now and give the definition of the ext-sheaf and ext-invariant in the next subsection.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

The
H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})
-action is defined just as in the proof of Theorem
2.8
and freeness and transitivity follow as in that proof, using Theorem
4.1
and Proposition
4.10
. By naturality of the action with respect to Morita equivalences we mean the following. Let
((P,\omega_{P}),\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
be a symplectic Morita equivalence between regular and proper symplectic groupoids
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
and
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
with related Delzant subspaces
\underline{\Delta}_{1}\subset\underline{M}_{1}
and
\underline{\Delta}_{2}\subset\underline{M}_{2}
. Consider the bijection
P_{*}
in Proposition
3.8
. By an argument similar to that for Proposition
4.11
, it follows that if
x_{2}\in\Delta_{2}
and
p\in\alpha_{2}^{-1}(x_{2})
, then for any faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
-space
J
with momentum map image
\Delta_{1}
, it holds that:

(98)

e(P_{*}(J))_{x_{2}}=(\varphi_{p})_{*}(e(J)_{x_{1}}),

where
x_{1}:=\alpha_{1}(p)
and
\varphi_{p}:(\mathcal{G}_{1})_{x_{1}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{G}_{2})_{x_{2}}
is the induced isomorphism of Lie groups (
37
). So, given a global section
e_{1}
of the ext-sheaf
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{1})}
, the bijection
P_{*}
restricts to a bijection:

P_{*}:\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of }\\
&\textrm{faithful multiplicity-free $(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})$-spaces}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\textrm{with momentum image $\Delta_{1}$}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\textrm{ and with ext-invariant $e_{1}$}\end{aligned}\right\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\quad\quad\quad\textrm{ Isomorphism classes of}\\
&\textrm{faithful multiplicity-free $(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})$-spaces}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\textrm{with momentum image $\Delta_{2}$}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\textrm{ and with ext-invariant $e_{2}$}\end{aligned}\right\}

for a unique global section
e_{2}
of the ext-sheaf
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2},\underline{\Delta}_{2})}
. By the aforementioned naturality, we mean that for any
\underline{c}\in H^{1}(\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{1})
and any faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}
with momentum image
\Delta_{1}
and ext-invariant
e_{1}
, it holds that:

(99)

P_{*}(\underline{c}\cdot[J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}])=\underline{P}_{*}(\underline{c})\cdot P_{*}([J:(S,\omega)\to M_{1}]),

where

\underline{P}_{*}:\check{H}^{1}(\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\check{H}^{1}(\underline{\Delta}_{2},\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{2})

denotes the isomorphism induced by the associated isomorphism of sheaves as in Example
4.9
. The validity of (
99
) is straightforward to verify and left to the reader.
∎

4.1.3.
The ext-invariant as global section of the ext-sheaf

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid and
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
be a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
with corresponding invariant subspace
\Delta\subset M
. Consider the set-theoretic bundle:

(100)

\bigsqcup_{x\in\Delta}I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})\to\Delta.

Viewed as section of this bundle, the collection of ext-classes of a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with momentum map image
\Delta
turns out to have the property that (in a sense to be made precise) its value at any given point
x\in\Delta
determines its values in some invariant open neighbourhood of
x
. Roughly speaking, the ext-sheaf will be the sheaf of sections of (
100
) with this property. This sheaf and the fact that the ext-classes form a global section of it are both of conceptual value (see Theorem
4.27
), as well as of use when applying the second and third structure theorem in concrete examples (see Example
4.23
below).

In order to define the ext-sheaf, note first that the groupoid
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
acts along (
100
): a given
[g]:x\to y
in
\mathcal{B}
acts as the bijection

I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})\xrightarrow{\sim}I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{y},\mathcal{T}_{y})

induced by the conjugation map
C_{g}:\mathcal{G}_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{G}_{y}
(cf. Lemma
4.13
).

Definition 4.14
.

We let
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}=\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set},(\mathcal{G},\Omega,\underline{\Delta})}
denote the sheaf on
\underline{\Delta}
consisting of
\mathcal{B}
-invariant set-theoretic local sections of (
100
). That is,
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{U})
consists of set-theoretic sections
\sigma
of (
100
) defined on
U\subset\Delta
, with the property that for every
[g]:x\to y
in
\mathcal{B}|_{U}
:

\sigma(y)=[g]\cdot\sigma(x).

An argument similar to that for Proposition
4.11
shows:

Proposition 4.15
.

Given a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with momentum map image
\Delta
, the section of (
100
) formed by its ext-classes is
\mathcal{B}
-invariant.

The ext-sheaf will be the subsheaf of
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}
consisting of what we call flat sections. To define the notion of flatness, first consider:

Lemma 4.16
.

A Morita equivalence between regular and proper symplectic groupoids
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})\rightrightarrows M_{1}
and
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})\rightrightarrows M_{2}
that relates a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}_{1}\subset\underline{M}_{1}
to a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}_{2}\subset\underline{M}_{2}
induces an isomorphism of sheaves:

(101)

\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set,1}}:=\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set},(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{1})}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set},(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2},\underline{\Delta}_{2})}=:\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set,2}}

covering the induced homeomorphism between
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
and
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
. This is functorial with respect to composition of Morita equivalences.

Proof.

Let a Morita equivalence:
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})
M_{1}
(P,\omega_{P})
M_{2}
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

be given. Given an open
\underline{U}_{1}
in
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
, let
\underline{U}_{2}
be its image under the induced homeomorphism between
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
and
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
. This means that the respective invariant opens
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
in
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
satisfy:

\alpha_{1}^{-1}(U_{1})=\alpha_{2}^{-1}(U_{2}).

Given
\sigma_{1}\in\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set,1}}(\underline{U}_{1})
and
x_{2}\in U_{2}
, choose a
p\in P
such that
\alpha_{2}(p)=x_{2}
and define:

\sigma_{2}(x_{2}):=(\varphi_{p})_{*}(\sigma_{1}(x_{1}))\in I^{1}((\mathcal{G}_{2})_{x_{2}},\mathcal{T}_{x_{2}}),

where
x_{1}=\alpha_{1}(p)
and
\varphi_{p}:(\mathcal{G}_{1})_{x_{1}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{G}_{2})_{x_{2}}
is the induced isomorphism of Lie groups (
37
). It follows from
\mathcal{B}_{1}
-invariance of
\sigma_{1}
that this does not depend on the choice of
p
. Indeed, any other element of
\widetilde{p}\in\alpha_{2}^{-1}(x_{2})
is of the form
g\cdot p
for some
g\in\mathcal{G}_{1}
, and it holds that
\varphi_{g\cdot p}=\varphi_{p}\circ C_{g^{-1}}
, from which it follows that:

(\varphi_{\widetilde{p}})_{*}(\sigma_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{1}))=(\varphi_{p})_{*}(C_{g^{-1}})_{*}(\sigma_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{1}))=(\varphi_{p})_{*}(\sigma_{1}(x_{1})),

where
\widetilde{x}_{1}=\alpha_{1}(\widetilde{p})
. A similar argument shows that
\sigma_{2}
is
\mathcal{B}_{2}
-invariant. Hence, this defines a section
\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set,2}}(\underline{U}_{2})
. In this way we obtain a map:

(102)

\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set,1}}(\underline{U}_{1})\to\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set,2}}(\underline{U}_{2}).

This is functorial with respect to composition of Morita equivalences, since the isomorphisms
\varphi_{p}
are. A completely analogous construction gives an inverse to (
102
). Moreover, (
102
) is compatible with restrictions to smaller opens. So, we have constructed the desired isomorphism of sheaves.
∎

Due to the linearization theorem for proper symplectic groupoids
[
52
,
9
,
6
,
8
]
, for every orbit
\mathcal{O}
of
\mathcal{G}
in
M
, there is an invariant open neighbourhood
{W}
of
\mathcal{O}
together with an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group
G
, a
G
-invariant open neighbourhood
{W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}}
of the origin in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and a symplectic Morita equivalence:
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{W}
W
(P,\omega_{P})
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})|_{W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}}
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

that relates
\mathcal{O}
to the origin in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Let
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
denote the invariant subspace of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
related to
W\cap\Delta
by this Morita equivalence. Then, via the induced isomorphism of sheaves of Lemma
4.16
, to each local section of
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set},(\mathcal{G},\Omega,\underline{\Delta})}|_{\underline{W}\cap\underline{\Delta}}
