corresponds an invariant local section of the set-theoretic bundle:

(103)

\bigsqcup_{\alpha\in\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}}I^{1}(G_{\alpha},T)\to\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}.

This is because the isotropy group of
G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}
at
\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is the isotropy group
G_{\alpha}
of the coadjoint action and (since
G
is infinitesimally abelian) the identity component of
G_{\alpha}
is the identity component
T
of
G
. Further notice that for each
\alpha\in\mathfrak{g}^{*}
there is a restriction map
I^{1}(G,T)\to I^{1}(G_{\alpha},T)
.

Definition 4.17
.

We will call a local section
\sigma
of (
103
)
centered
if the origin belongs to its domain and for each
\alpha
in its domain it holds that:

\sigma(\alpha)=\sigma(0)|_{G_{\alpha}}\in I^{1}(G_{\alpha},T).

Definition 4.18
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid together with a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
. Given an open
\underline{V}
in
\underline{\Delta}
and an orbit
\mathcal{O}\in\underline{V}
, we call a section:

(104)

\sigma\in\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{V})

flat at
\mathcal{O}
if there is an open neighbourhood
\underline{W}
of
\mathcal{O}
in
\underline{M}
and a symplectic Morita equivalence as above, such that the invariant local section of (
103
) corresponding to
\sigma|_{\underline{W}\cap\underline{V}}
(via the induced isomorphism of sheaves of Lemma
4.16
) is centered. We call a section (
104
) flat if it is so at all
\mathcal{O}\in\underline{V}
. The flat sections form a subsheaf of
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}
on
\underline{\Delta}
, that we denote as:

(105)

\mathcal{I}^{1}=\mathcal{I}^{1}_{(\mathcal{G},\Omega,\underline{\Delta})}

and call the
ext-sheaf
of
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
on
\underline{\Delta}
.

Remark 4.19
.

Given
x\in\Delta
, evaluation at
x
defines a bijection between the stalk of (
105
) at the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{x}
and the set
I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})
.

Having defined the ext-sheaf, we now turn to the ext-invariant. The theorem below shows not only that the collection of ext-classes of a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space is flat, but that flatness is also a sufficient condition for there to be local solutions to the problem of existence of a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with momentum map image
\Delta
and with collection of ext-classes a prescribed
\mathcal{B}
-invariant section of (
100
). More precisely:

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid with associated orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
. Further, let
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
be a Delzant subspace and let
\sigma
be a
\mathcal{B}
-invariant section of the set-theoretic bundle (
100
). Then
\sigma
is flat at
\mathcal{O}\in\underline{\Delta}
if and only if there is an invariant open
U
in
M
around
\mathcal{O}
and a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
with
J(S)=U\cap\Delta
and with
\sigma|_{\underline{U}\cap\underline{\Delta}}
as the collection of its ext-classes. In particular, the collection of ext-classes of a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space is flat.

Definition 4.21
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid. The
ext-invariant

e(J)
of a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
with momentum map image
\Delta
is the global section of (
105
) given by the collection of its ext-classes.

Remark 4.22
.

From Theorem
4.20
one can derive that if a section (
104
) is flat at
\mathcal{O}
, then for
every
symplectic Morita equivalence as above the invariant local section of (
103
) corresponding to
\sigma|_{{\underline{W}}\cap\underline{V}}
(via the induced isomorphism of sheaves of Lemma
4.16
) is centered on some neighbourhood of the origin.

A proof of Theorem
4.20
and Remark
4.22
will be given in the next subsection. Before that, we illustrate their use in a concrete example.

Example 4.23
.

Consider the standard integral affine cylinder
(M,\Lambda):=(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1},\mathbb{Z}\textrm{d}x\oplus\mathbb{Z}\textrm{d}\theta)
with Delzant subspace
\Delta:=[-1,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
. Let
\Gamma:=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and consider the
\Gamma
-action given by
\varepsilon\cdot(x,\mu)=(\varepsilon_{1}x,\mu^{\varepsilon_{2}})
, for
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
(x,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
(where we view
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
as
\{\pm 1\}
). Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be the semi-direct product symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T},\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}}^{*}\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})
of the etale integral affine orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}:=\Gamma\ltimes M
over
(M,\Lambda)
with its canonical action on
\mathcal{T}:=\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
. There are eight isomorphism classes of faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces with momentum map image
\Delta
. These are represented by
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces with the same underlying symplectic manifold
(S,\omega):=(\mathbb{S}^{2}\times\mathbb{T}^{2},\omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\oplus\omega_{\mathbb{T}^{2}})
(where
\omega_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
and
\omega_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}
denote the standard area forms) and momentum map
J:=h\times\textrm{pr}_{\mathbb{S}^{1},2}
(the product of the height function
h
on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with the second coordinate projection from
\mathbb{T}^{2}
to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
), but with different actions indexed by the eight group
1
-cocycles
\tau:\Gamma\to\mathcal{L}(\Delta)
determined by the values listed in the table below. Here we canonically identify
\mathcal{T}
with
\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

\textrm{pr}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\left(\tau(1,-1)(x,\mu)\right)=

(1,1)

(-1,1)

(1,1)

(-1,1)

(
\mu
,
e^{2\pi ix}
)

(-
\mu
,
e^{2\pi ix}
)

(
\mu
,
e^{2\pi ix}
)

(-
\mu
,
e^{2\pi ix}
)

\textrm{pr}_{\mathbb{T}^{2}}\left(\tau(-1,1)(x,\mu)\right)=

(1,1)

(1,1)

(1,-1)

(1,-1)

(
\mu
,
e^{2\pi ix}
)

(
\mu
,
e^{2\pi ix}
)

(
\mu
,-
e^{2\pi ix}
)

(
\mu
,-
e^{2\pi ix}
)

The corresponding actions are given by:

\left(\varepsilon,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},z,\mu_{2}\right)\cdot_{\tau}\left(x+iy,z,\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\right)=\left((\lambda_{1}\,\tau_{1}(\varepsilon)(z,\mu_{2}))^{\varepsilon_{1}}\,(x+i\varepsilon_{1}y),\varepsilon_{1}z,(\lambda_{2}\,\tau_{2}(\varepsilon)(z,\mu_{2})\,\mu_{1})^{\varepsilon_{2}},\mu_{2}^{\varepsilon_{2}}\right),

for
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\Gamma
,
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}
,
(x+iy,z)\in\mathbb{S}^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{R}
and
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, where we canonically identify the space of arrows of
\mathcal{B}\Join\mathcal{T}
with
\Gamma\times\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

This claim can be verified using Theorem
5
, Theorem
4.20
and Remark
4.22
, as follows. Consider the section of
\mathcal{I}^{1}
, with germ at the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{x}\in\underline{\Delta}
through
(x,\mu)\in\Delta
represented by the
1
-cocycle:

\textrm{pr}_{\mathcal{T}_{(x,\mu)}}:\mathcal{G}_{(x,\mu)}=\Gamma_{(x,\mu)}\ltimes\mathcal{T}_{(x,\mu)}\to\mathcal{T}_{(x,\mu)}.

Using this and Remark
1.5
to identify the stalk of
\mathcal{I}^{1}
at an orbit through
(x,\mu)
with the degree one group cohomology of the
\Gamma_{(x,\mu)}
-module
\mathcal{T}_{(x,\mu)}
, one can compute the stalks of
\mathcal{I}^{1}
to be as in the following figure.

Figure 2.
A fundamental domain for the
\Gamma
-action on
\Delta
, partitioned by collecting points with the same isotropy group. For connected components of members with non-trivial isotropy groups, the corresponding stalks of
\mathcal{I}^{1}
are depicted.

Using Remark
4.22
one readily sees that any global section of
\mathcal{I}^{1}
is determined by its germs at the two
\Gamma
-fixed points
(0,1)
and
(0,-1)
via the maps in this figure, and moreover, the second
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-components of these two germs must coincide. Therefore, there are at most eight global sections of
\mathcal{I}^{1}
. Each of these possibilities is realized by the ext-invariants of the
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces listed above. So, to verify our claim, it remains to show that
H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})=0
. This will be done in Example
4.32
, in the next section.

4.1.4.
Proof of Theorem
4.20
and Remark
4.22

In the proof we will use the following construction, which is a particular case of a well-known construction used in the Marle-Guillemin-Sternberg normal form for Hamiltonian Lie group actions
[
19
,
29
]
. Consider the data of:

•

an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group
G
,

•

a closed subgroup
H
of
G
for which the inclusion induces an isomorphism:

{\varGamma}_{H}\xrightarrow{\sim}{\varGamma_{G}}

between the groups of connected components of
H
and
G
,

•

a maximally toric
H
-representation
(V,\omega_{V})
.

Associated to this is a Hamiltonian
G
-space:

(106)

J_{\textrm{mod}}:(S_{\textrm{mod}},\omega_{\textrm{mod}})\to\mathfrak{g}^{*},

where:

•

(S_{\textrm{mod}},\omega_{\textrm{mod}})
is the symplectic manifold obtained from symplectic reduction (at value
0
) of the (right) Hamiltonian
H
-space:

(G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})\times(V,\omega_{V})\to\mathfrak{h}^{*},\quad(g,\alpha,v)\mapsto\alpha|_{\mathfrak{h}}-J_{V}(v),

with
J_{V}
as in (
16
) and with the
H
-action given by
(g,\alpha,v)\cdot h=(gh,h^{-1}\cdot\alpha,h^{-1}\cdot v)
,

•

the
G
-action on
S_{\textrm{mod}}
is that inherited from the
G
-action on
G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times V
by left translation on the
G
-component,

•

the momentum map
J_{\textrm{mod}}
is the map induced by the projection map
\textrm{pr}_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}:G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}\times V\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

It follows from a straighforward verification that:

Proposition 4.24
.

Given the data above, consider the regular and proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega):=(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
(the cotangent groupoid, as in Example
3
). The Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space corresponding to the Hamiltonian
G
-space (
106
) is a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space.

Up to Morita equivalence, this construction provides a local model for faithful multiplicity-free actions of general regular proper symplectic groupoids. In view of this, it will be useful to first single out the following case of the backward implication in Theorem
4.20
.

Proposition 4.25
.

Suppose that we are given
G
,
H
and
(V,\omega_{V})
as above, so that as for (
95
) there is an induced map:

(107)

I^{1}(H,T_{H})\to I^{1}(G,T_{G}),

where
T_{H}
and
T_{G}
denote the respective identity components of
H
and
G
. Consider the associated faithful multiplicity-free
(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
-space
{J_{\textrm{mod}}}
(as in Proposition
4.24
). The following hold.

a)

The image of
{J_{\textrm{mod}}}
is
\pi_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}^{-1}(\Delta_{V})
, where
\Delta_{V}
is the image of the momentum map (
16
) and
\pi_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}
is the canonical projection.

b)

The map (
107
) sends
e(V,\omega_{V})\in I^{1}(H,T_{H})
to the ext-invariant
e({J_{\textrm{mod}}})_{0}\in I^{1}(G,T_{G})
of
{J_{\textrm{mod}}}
at the origin in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.

c)

The collection of ext-classes of
{J_{\textrm{mod}}}
is centered (in the sense of Definition
4.17
).

Proof.

Part
a
readily follows from surjectivity of
{\varGamma}_{H}\to{\varGamma}_{G}
. For the remainder, let:

q:(G\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})\times_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}V\to{S_{\textrm{mod}}}

denote the quotient map. Part
b
follows from the observation that the symplectic normal representation at
[1,0,0]\in{J_{\textrm{mod}}^{-1}(0)}
is canonically isomorphic to
(V,\omega_{V})
, via the
H
-equivariant linear symplectomorphism:

(V,\omega_{V})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{[1,0,0]},({\omega_{\textrm{mod}}})_{[1,0,0]}),\quad v\mapsto[\textrm{d}q_{(1,0,0)}(0,0,v)].

We turn to part
c
. Fix an isomorphism of symplectic
T_{H}
-representations:

\psi:(V,\omega)\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{1}},\omega_{\textrm{st}})\oplus...\oplus(\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{n}},\omega_{\textrm{st}}),

and let
\mathcal{W}=\{\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}\}
denote the set of weights. First notice that (by part
b
) the class
e({J_{\textrm{mod}}})_{0}
is represented by the unique
1
-cocycle
{c}_{0}:G\to T_{G}
that restricts to the identity map on
T_{G}
and that restricts to
{c}_{\psi}
on
H
. Next, let
\alpha\in{J_{\textrm{mod}}(S_{\textrm{mod}})}
. Then
\alpha|_{\mathfrak{h}}\in\Delta_{V}
by part
a
. From the description (
21
) it is clear that:

\alpha|_{\mathfrak{h}}=\sum_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}}t_{\alpha_{i}}\alpha_{i}

for unique
t_{\alpha_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}=\{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}\mid t_{\alpha_{i}}=0\}
and let
p\in J_{V}^{-1}(\alpha|_{\mathfrak{h}})
be the element with component
\psi(p)_{\alpha_{i}}\in\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{i}}
equal to
\sqrt{t_{\alpha_{i}}}
for each
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}
. Consider
[1,\alpha,p]\in J_{\textrm{mod}}^{-1}(\alpha)
. As is readily verified, the map:

(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})\to(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{[1,\alpha,p]},({\omega_{\textrm{mod}}})_{[1,\alpha,p]}),\quad[v]\mapsto[\textrm{d}q_{(1,\alpha,p)}(0,0,v)],

is a symplectic linear isomorphism. Furthermore, it is equivariant with respect to the action of
H_{[1,\alpha,p]}=H_{\alpha}\cap H_{p}
. As the identity component of
H_{\alpha}\cap H_{p}
is the identity component
T_{H_{p}}
of
H_{p}
, it follows that:

e\left(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{[1,\alpha,p]},({\omega_{\textrm{mod}}})_{[1,\alpha,p]}\right)=[{c}_{\psi_{p}}|_{H_{\alpha}\cap H_{p}}]\in I^{1}(H_{\alpha}\cap H_{p},T_{H_{p}}).

Therefore,
e({J_{\textrm{mod}}})_{\alpha}
is represented by the unique
1
-cocycle
{c}_{\alpha}:G_{\alpha}\to T_{G}
that restricts to the identity map on
T_{G}
and to
{c}_{\psi_{p}}|_{H_{\alpha}\cap H_{p}}
on
H_{\alpha}\cap H_{p}
. To complete the proof, we will now show that:

(108)

{c}_{0}|_{G_{\alpha}}={c}_{\alpha}.

Using (
21
) one computes that:

\psi(\textrm{Ker}(\textrm{d}J_{V})_{p})=\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}}\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{i}}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{W_{\alpha}}}\sqrt{-1}\cdot\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{i}}\right).

On the other hand, it is clear that:

\psi(T_{p}\mathcal{O})=\bigoplus_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{W_{\alpha}}}\sqrt{-1}\cdot\mathbb{R}_{\alpha_{i}}.

It follows from this that
\psi
induces a
(T_{H})_{p}
-equivariant symplectic linear isomorphism:

\psi_{p}:(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})\xrightarrow{\sim}\bigoplus_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}}\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{i}},\quad\psi_{p}([v])_{\alpha_{i}}=\psi(v)_{\alpha_{i}}.

Hence, the weights of the symplectic normal representation at
p
are given by
\alpha_{i}|_{\mathfrak{t}_{p}}\in\mathfrak{t}_{p}^{*}
for
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
and from (
27
) it follows that the
1
-cocycle
{c}_{\psi_{p}}:H_{p}\to T_{H_{p}}
representing
e(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p})\in I^{1}(H_{p},T_{H_{p}})
has the property that for each
h\in H_{p}
,
v\in\psi^{-1}(\bigoplus_{\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}}\mathbb{C}_{\alpha_{i}})
and each
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
:

\chi_{\alpha_{i}}({c}_{\psi_{p}}(h))\cdot\psi(v)_{\alpha_{i}}=\psi(h\cdot v)_{[h]\cdot\alpha_{i}}.

The same property is satisfied by the
1
-cocycle
{c}_{\psi}:H\to T_{H}
representing
e(V,\omega)\in I^{1}(H,T_{H})
. Therefore, we conclude that:

(109)

\chi_{\alpha_{i}}\circ{c}_{\psi_{p}}=\chi_{\alpha_{i}}\circ{c}_{\psi}|_{H_{p}},\quad\forall\text{ }\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}.

On the other hand, it holds that:

(110)

\chi_{\alpha_{i}}\circ{c}_{\psi_{p}}=1=\chi_{\alpha_{i}}\circ{c}_{\psi}|_{H_{p}},\quad\forall\text{ }\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}.

Indeed, the first equality in (
110
) follows from the observation that, because
{c}_{\psi_{p}}
takes values in
(T_{H})_{p}
, for all
h\in H_{p}
and
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}
we have:

\chi_{\alpha_{i}}({c}_{\psi_{p}}(h))\cdot\psi(p)_{\alpha_{i}}=({c}_{\psi_{p}}(h)\cdot\psi(p))_{\alpha_{i}}=\psi(p)_{\alpha_{i}}.

For the latter equality, notice first that, since each
h\in H_{p}
fixes
\alpha
(as
J_{V}
is
H
-equivariant), it holds that
t_{[h]\cdot\alpha_{i}}=t_{\alpha_{i}}
, and so
\psi(p)_{[h]\cdot\alpha_{i}}=\psi(p)_{\alpha_{i}}
for each
\alpha_{i}\in\mathcal{W}
. Using this and (
27
), we find that for such
h
and
\alpha_{i}
:

\chi_{\alpha_{i}}(c_{\psi}(h))\cdot\psi(p)_{\alpha_{i}}=\psi(h\cdot p)_{[h]\cdot\alpha_{i}}=\psi(p)_{[h]\cdot\alpha_{i}}=\psi(p)_{\alpha_{i}},

from which we conclude that the second equality in (
110
) holds. Together, (
109
) and (
110
) imply that:

(111)

{c}_{\psi_{p}}={c}_{\psi}|_{H_{p}}.

We conclude from this that (
108
) indeed holds, since
{c}_{0}|_{G_{\alpha}}:G_{\alpha}\to T_{G}
is a
1
-cocycle that restricts to the identity on
T_{G}
, and that restricts to
{c}_{\psi_{p}}|_{H_{\alpha}\cap H_{p}}
on
H_{\alpha}\cap H_{p}
by (
111
).
∎

We are now ready to complete the proof.

End of the proof of Theorem
4.20
and Remark
4.22
.

First, in the setting of Theorem
4.20
, suppose that there is an invariant open
U
in
M
around
\mathcal{O}\in\underline{\Delta}
and a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to M
such that
J(S)=U\cap\Delta
and the collection of its ext-invariants is
\sigma|_{\underline{U}\cap\underline{\Delta}}
. We will not only show that
\sigma
is flat at
\mathcal{O}
, but that for
every
symplectic Morita equivalence as after Lemma
4.16
, with
W\subset U
, the invariant local section of (
103
) corresponding to
\sigma|_{\underline{W}\cap\underline{\Delta}}
via (
101
) is centered on some neighbourhood of the origin. Suppose that we are given any such symplectic Morita equivalence:
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{W}
W
(P,\omega_{P})
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})|_{W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}}
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

After choosing a
p\in\alpha_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{O})
and composing the Morita equivalence with the automorphism of
(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
associated to the isomorphism of isotropy groups
\varphi_{p}:G\to G
induced, as in (
37
), by the Morita equivalence, we can assume without loss of generality that there is a
p\in\alpha_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{O})
such that
G
is the isotropy group of
\mathcal{G}
at
x:=\alpha_{1}(p)
and such that
\varphi_{p}
is the identity map of
G
. Then the set-germ at the origin of the invariant subset of
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
corresponding to
W\cap\Delta
is that of the
G
-invariant smooth polyhedral cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}:=((\rho_{\Omega})_{x}^{*})^{-1}(C_{x}(\Delta))
. This follows by combining Lemma
1.38
, Proposition
1.39
and the observation that
(\rho_{-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}}})_{0}^{*}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to T_{0}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(which is the canonical isomorphism of the vector space
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
with its tangent space at
0
) represents the map germ
\log_{0}
. Note here that the polyhedral cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
is indeed
G
-invariant, as follows from Corollary
1.41
and
G
-equivariance of
(\rho_{\Omega})_{x}^{*}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathcal{N}_{x}{\mathcal{O}}
with respect to the coadjoint action of
G
on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(which follows from Lemma
1.38
, applied to the identity equivalence). Let
H
and
(V,\omega_{V})
be the isotropy group and the symplectic normal representation of the
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space
J
at some point in
J^{-1}(x)
, and consider the faithful multiplicity-free
(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
-space
J_{\textrm{mod}}
associated to this data, as in Proposition
4.24
(which applies because of Proposition
1.43
). In view of the above, (
98
), Proposition
1.43
c
and Proposition
4.25
, the
(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
-spaces
J_{\textrm{mod}}
and
P_{*}(J)
satisfy the criteria in Proposition
4.10
at the origin in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. Hence, there is an invariant open neighbourhood of the origin in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
such that the restrictions of the
(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
-spaces
J_{\textrm{mod}}
and
P_{*}(J)
to this open neighbourhood are isomorphic. Since the ext-invariant of
J_{\textrm{mod}}
is centered (Proposition
4.25
), the same holds for the restriction of
P_{*}(J)
to this open neighbourhood. Because of (
98
), the collection of ext-invariants of
P_{*}(J)
is the invariant local section of (
103
) corresponding to
\sigma|_{\underline{W}\cap\underline{\Delta}}
via (
101
), which is therefore centered on a neighbourhood of the origin, as we wanted to show.

Having shown this, to complete the proof of both Theorem
4.20
and Remark
4.22
, it remains to prove the forward implication in Theorem
4.20
. To this end, suppose that
\sigma
is flat at
\mathcal{O}
. By flatness and the same reasoning as above, there is a symplectic Morita equivalence
((P,\omega_{P}),\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
with a
p\in\alpha_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{O})
as above, with the additional property that the invariant local section corresponding to
\sigma|_{\underline{W}\cap\underline{\Delta}}
via (
101
) is centered. Since the set-germ at the origin of the invariant subset of
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
corresponding to
W\cap\Delta
is that of the
G
-invariant smooth polyhedral cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
(defined as above), after possibly shrinking
W
and
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
we can further arrange that
W\cap\Delta
is related to the invariant subset
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}\cap C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
. To construct a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with momentum image
W\cap\Delta
and ext-invariant
\sigma|_{\underline{W}\cap\underline{\Delta}}
, we will construct:

•

a closed subgroup
H
of
G
with the property that
{\varGamma}_{H}\to{\varGamma}_{G}
is bijective,

•

a maximally toric
H
-representation
(V,\omega_{V})
such that
\pi_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}^{-1}(\Delta_{V})=C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
and such that
e(V,\omega_{V})\in I^{1}(H,T_{H})
is mapped to
\sigma(x)\in I^{1}(G,T_{G})
by (
107
) (for
x:=\alpha_{1}(p)
as above).

The restriction to
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
of the faithful multiplicity-free space
(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
-space
J_{\textrm{mod}}
associated to this data (as in Proposition
4.24
) would have momentum map image
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}\cap C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
and collection of ext-invariants equal to the invariant local section corresponding to
\sigma|_{\underline{W}\cap\underline{\Delta}}
via (
101
) (because both are centered and they have the same value at the origin). Therefore, the
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{W}
-space associated to this by the symplectic Morita equivalence
((P,\omega_{P}),\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
would have momentum image
W\cap\Delta
and ext-invariant
\sigma|_{\underline{W}\cap\underline{\Delta}}
.

So, to complete the proof it remains to construct
H
and
(V,\omega_{V})
satisfying the requirements listed above. For this, let
{c}:G\to T_{G}
be a
1
-cocycle such that
[{c}]=\sigma(x)\in I^{1}(G,T_{G})
. Further, let us (suggestively) denote by
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
the largest linear subspace of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
that is contained in
C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
and let
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
be the annihilator of
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
. Since the polyhedral cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
is smooth in
(\mathfrak{g}^{*},\Lambda_{T_{G}}^{*})
, the lattice
\mathfrak{h}\cap\Lambda_{T_{G}}
has full rank in
\mathfrak{h}
and so
T_{H}:=\exp_{G}(\mathfrak{h})
is a subtorus of
T_{G}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
. Since
C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
is invariant under the coadjoint action of
G
,
T_{H}
is invariant under conjugation by elements of
G
. From this it readily follows that
{\varGamma}_{G}\times T_{H}
is a subgroup of
{\varGamma}_{G}\ltimes T_{G}
. Now, let
H
be the subgroup of
G
corresponding to
{\varGamma}_{G}\times T_{H}
under the isomorphism of Lie groups:

G\xrightarrow{\sim}{\varGamma}_{G}\ltimes T_{G},\quad g\mapsto([g],{c}(g)).

Then
H
is a closed Lie subgroup of
G
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
and
{\varGamma}_{H}\to{\varGamma}_{G}
is bijective. Further notice that the
1
-cocycle
{c}
restricts to a
1
-cocycle
{c}|_{H}:H\to T_{H}
, that restricts to the identity map on
T_{H}
. Next, we construct the desired representation of
H
. Let us (suggestively) denote by
\Delta_{V}
the image of
C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
under the projection
\pi_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}:\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. By construction,
\Delta_{V}
is a smooth and pointed polyhedral cone in the integral affine vector space
(\mathfrak{h}^{*},\Lambda_{T_{H}}^{*})
. Furthermore,
\Delta_{V}
is
{\varGamma}_{H}
-invariant, because
\pi_{\mathfrak{h}^{*}}
is
H
-equivariant and
C_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
is
{\varGamma}_{G}
-invariant. Hence, by Theorem
1.3
there is a maximally toric
H
-representation
(V,\omega_{V})
with momentum image
\Delta_{V}
and
e(V,\omega_{V})=[{c}|_{H}]\in I^{1}(H,T_{H})
. As is clear from their construction,
H
and
(V,\omega_{V})
satisfy the requirements.
∎

4.2.
The third structure theorem

4.2.1.
Proof of the third structure theorem

We now turn to the proof of Theorem
6
, starting with:

Proposition 4.26
.

The injection (
12
) is compatible with the actions in the first and second structure theorems.

Proof.

After restricting to a complete transversal, the proof reduces to the case in which
\mathcal{B}
is etale. Suppose we are in that case. Let
\underline{\textrm{c}}\in H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})
and let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with momentum image
\Delta
. Via the natural isomorphism from Čech to sheaf cohomology we can represent
\underline{\textrm{c}}
by a Čech
1
-cocycle
\underline{\tau}\in\check{C}^{1}_{q(\text{$\mathcal{U}$})}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})
with respect to an open cover of the form
q(\text{$\mathcal{U}$}):=\{q(U)\mid U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}\}
, where
q_{\Delta}:\Delta\to\underline{\Delta}
is the orbit projection and
\mathcal{U}
is some basis of
\Delta
for which
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
is
\mathcal{U}
-acyclic. The composition:

\check{H}^{1}_{q(\text{$\mathcal{U}$})}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})\to\check{H}^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})\to H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})\to\check{H}^{1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})

is induced by the map of complexes given in degree
n
by:

(q_{\Delta})^{*}:\check{C}^{n}_{q(\text{$\mathcal{U}$})}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})\to\check{C}^{n}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L}),\quad(q_{\Delta})^{*}(c)(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}...\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n}):=c(q(U_{1}),...,q(U_{n}))|_{U_{n}}\in\mathcal{L}(U_{n}).

So, denoting
\tau:=(q_{\Delta})^{*}(\underline{\tau})\in\check{C}^{1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
, to prove the proposition we ought to give an isomorphism of faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{\tau},\omega_{\tau})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S_{\underline{\tau}},\omega_{\underline{\tau}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J_{\underline{\tau}}}

where the left-hand
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space is that defined in the proof of Theorem
3
, whereas the right-hand one is that defined as in the proof of Theorem
2.8
, but with
\mathcal{L}
replaced by
\underline{\mathcal{L}}
. Consider the map
\psi:S_{\tau}\to S_{\underline{\tau}}
given by
\psi([p,U])=[p,\widehat{U}]
, where
\widehat{U}
denotes the
\mathcal{G}
-saturation of
U
. A straightforward verification shows that this is well-defined, bijective and intertwines the maps
J_{\tau}
and
J_{\underline{\tau}}
. Further notice that, for each
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
, the pre-composition of
\psi
with the inclusion (
72
) coincides with the composition:

(J^{-1}(U),\omega)\hookrightarrow(J^{-1}(\widehat{U}),\omega)\hookrightarrow(S_{\underline{\tau}},\omega_{\underline{\tau}}),

which is a symplectomorphism onto an open in
(S_{\underline{\tau}},\omega_{\underline{\tau}})
. Therefore,
\psi
is a symplectomorphism. Finally, the fact that
{\underline{\tau}}(q(U),q(V))
is
\mathcal{B}
-invariant for all
U,V\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
implies that
\psi
is
\mathcal{G}
-equivariant. So,
\psi
is an isomorphism of faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-spaces.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6
.

The second structure theorem and Proposition
4.26
imply that the action in the first structure theorem descends to an action of the quotient group (
14
) on the image of (
13
), uniquely determined by the fact that (
13
) becomes equivariant with respect to the
\check{H}^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
-action on its image corresponding to the quotient group action. It follows by combining the first and second structure theorem with Proposition
4.26
that this action is free and transitive.
∎

4.2.2.
On the action in the third structure theorem

In this subsection we give a more direct description of the action in the third structure theorem and show that the ext-sheaf comes with a free and transitive action of a more familiar sheaf on
\underline{\Delta}
: the first right-derived functor
\mathcal{H}^{1}:=R^{1}(q_{\Delta})_{*}(\mathcal{L})
of the push-forward
(q_{\Delta})_{*}
(as in Remark
4.3
) applied to
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{\Delta}
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid with associated orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}
and let
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}
be a Delzant subspace. There is a Grothendieck spectral sequence
[
17
]
associated to the commutative triangle of functors:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{Sh}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})}
Ab
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{Sh}(\underline{\Delta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\cdot)^{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(q_{\Delta})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\cdot)}

which is a version of the usual Leray spectral sequence for the canonical map of topological groupoids
q_{\Delta}
from
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
to the unit groupoid of
\underline{\Delta}
. The associated exact sequence in low degrees (
15
) has as first few terms:

(112)

0\to H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})\to H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})\to\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{\Delta}).

Concretely,
\mathcal{H}^{1}
is the sheafification of the pre-sheaf on
\underline{\Delta}
that assigns to an open
\underline{U}
the abelian group
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{U},\mathcal{L})
(with the restriction maps induced by pull-back along the inclusion
\mathcal{B}|_{V}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}|_{U}
for opens
\underline{V}\subset\underline{U}
) and the map:

(113)

H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})\to\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})

in the above sequence takes a global section of this pre-sheaf to the induced global section of its sheafification. To define the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-action on the ext-sheaf
\mathcal{I}^{1}:=\mathcal{I}^{1}_{((\mathcal{G},\Omega),\underline{\Delta})}
, let
x\in\Delta
. Given an open
\underline{U}
in
\underline{\Delta}
such that
x\in U
, we consider the map:

(114)

\Phi_{x}:=(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}\circ(i_{x})^{*}:H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B}|_{U},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{(i_{x})^{*}}H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{L}_{x})\xrightarrow{(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}}H^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x}),

where
i_{x}:\mathcal{B}_{x}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{B}
is the inclusion of the isotropy group
\mathcal{B}_{x}
at
x
(viewed as groupoid over
\{x\}
) and
\textrm{ev}_{x}:\mathcal{L}_{x}\to\mathcal{T}_{x}
is the evaluation map from the stalk of
\mathcal{L}
at
x
into the fiber of
\mathcal{T}
over
x
. The last two groups denote group cohomology. Combining the map
\Phi_{x}
(in degree
1
) and the action in Remark
1.5
, we obtain an
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{U},\mathcal{L})
-action on the set
I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})
—the stalk of
\mathcal{I}^{1}
at the orbit
\mathcal{O}_{x}
of
\mathcal{G}
through
x
(see Remark
4.19
). For varying
\underline{U}
the actions on this set are compatible with restriction of opens and so descend to an action of the stalk of
\mathcal{H}^{1}
at
\mathcal{O}_{x}
.

Theorem 4.27
.

The stalk-wise actions given above define an action of the sheaf of abelian groups
\mathcal{H}^{1}
on the ext-sheaf
\mathcal{I}^{1}
that is free and transitive (meaning that for each open the associated group action is free and transitive). Moreover, via the map (
113
), the induced
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})
-action on
\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})
restricts to the action in Theorem
6
on the image of (
13
).

To prove this we will use the lemma below.

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
G
be an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group and consider the regular and proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega):=(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
over
M:=\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, with associated orbifold groupoid
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{T}=\Gamma\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, where
\Gamma:=G/T
with
T
the identity component of
G
. Suppose that
\underline{\Delta}\subset\underline{M}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}/G
is a Delzant subspace with the property that the corresponding invariant subspace
\Delta
in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is convex. Let
x\in\Delta^{\Gamma}
be a
\Gamma
-fixed point in
\Delta
.

a)

The map as in (
114
):

\Phi_{x}:H^{n}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{L})\to H^{n}(\Gamma,T)

is an isomorphism in all degrees
n>0
.

b)

For any
y\in\Delta
, the maps
\Phi_{x}
and
\Phi_{y}
are related via restriction, meaning that these fit into a commutative diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma_{y},T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Gamma_{y}})^{*}}

for each
n>0
, where
i_{\Gamma_{y}}:\Gamma_{y}\hookrightarrow{\Gamma}
denotes the inclusion.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be the basis of
\Delta
consisting of all convex opens. By assumption,
\Delta\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
. Moreover, by Lemma
3.5
,
\mathcal{L}
is
\mathcal{U}
-acyclic. To prove part
a
, we will show that the composition:

(115)

\check{H}^{n}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{(\ref{eqn:cechtoderivedmorph:equivshf})}H^{n}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{\Phi_{x}}H^{n}(\Gamma,T)

is an isomorphism. Consider the
\Gamma
-module
\mathcal{L}(\Delta)
of global section of
\mathcal{L}
, with action given by:

(\tau\cdot\gamma)(y)=\tau(\gamma\cdot y)\cdot(\gamma,y)\in\mathcal{T},\quad\gamma\in\Gamma,\quad y\in\Delta.

The map (
115
) is equal to the composition:

\check{H}^{n}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{(i_{\Delta})^{*}}H^{n}(\Gamma,\mathcal{L}(\Delta))\xrightarrow{(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}}H^{n}(\Gamma,T),

where
\textrm{ev}_{x}:\mathcal{L}(\Delta)\to T
is the map of
\Gamma
-modules given by evaluation at
x
and
i_{\Delta}
is the functor:

\Gamma\to\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta),\quad\gamma\mapsto(\Delta\xleftarrow{\sigma_{\gamma}}\Delta),

where we view
\Gamma
as category with a single object and
\sigma_{\gamma}
denotes the bisection with
\Gamma
-component constantly equal to
\gamma
. Consider the short exact sequence of
(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta)
-sheaves:

(116)

0\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)\to\Omega^{1}_{\textrm{cl}}\xrightarrow{(\ref{exptorbun})}\mathcal{L}\to 0,

where
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)
is the sheaf of smooth local sections of
\Lambda|_{\Delta}
, with
\Lambda
the lattice bundle associated to
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
, and
\Omega^{1}_{\textrm{cl}}
is the sheaf of closed
1
-forms on the manifold with corners
\Delta
, each equipped with the
(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta)
-action defined like that on
\mathcal{L}
(see Example
3.1
).
Since
\mathcal{U}
consists of convex opens, any section of
\mathcal{L}
over an open
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
lifts to a closed
1
-form on
U
along the universal covering map
T^{*}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\to\mathcal{T}
. It follows from this that global sections of (
116
) form an exact sequence of
\Gamma
-modules and the sequence of pre-sheaves on
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta)
associated to (
116
) is exact too. Moreover, we have a short exact sequence of
\Gamma
-modules:

0\to\Lambda_{T}\to\mathfrak{g}\to T\to 0.

The associated long exact sequences in cohomology give rise to a commutative diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}^{n}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\Omega^{1}_{\textrm{cl}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}^{n}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}^{n+1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{H}^{n+1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\Omega^{1}_{\textrm{cl}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma,\Omega^{1}_{\textrm{cl}}(\Delta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma,\mathcal{L}(\Delta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n+1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)(\Delta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n+1}(\Gamma,\Omega^{1}_{\textrm{cl}}(\Delta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n+1}(\Gamma,\Lambda_{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n+1}(\Gamma,\mathfrak{g})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Delta})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Delta})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Delta})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Delta})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}}

with exact rows. The outer left and right groups in the middle and bottom row vanish, because the group cohomology of an
\mathbb{R}
-linear representation of a finite group vanishes in all degrees
n>0
. The outer left and right groups in the top row vanish as well. One way to see this is to note that an explicit null-homotopy in strictly positive degree is given by:

h:\check{C}^{\bullet+1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\Omega^{1}_{\textrm{cl}})\to\check{C}^{\bullet}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\Omega^{1}_{\textrm{cl}}),\quad h(c)(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}\dots\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n})=\frac{1}{|\Gamma|}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}c(\Delta\xleftarrow{\sigma_{\gamma}}U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}\dots\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n}).

In view of this, it is enough to show that each of the maps in the composition:

\check{H}^{n+1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda))\xrightarrow{(i_{\Delta})^{*}}H^{n+1}(\Gamma,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)(\Delta))\xrightarrow{(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}}H^{n+1}(\Gamma,\Lambda_{T})

is an isomorphism. For this, note that
\Lambda=\Lambda_{T}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
(viewed as subset of
\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}=T^{*}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
). In view of this, the map of
\Gamma
-modules
\textrm{ev}_{x}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)(\Delta)\to\Lambda_{T}
is an isomorphism (since
\Delta
is connected), hence so is the induced map of cohomology groups
(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}
. Moreover, the pre-sheaf on
\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta)
induced by
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)
is the pull-back of the
\Gamma
-module
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)(\Delta)
along the functor
r_{\Delta}:\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta)\to\Gamma
that sends an arrow
V\xleftarrow{\sigma}U
to the value of the
\Gamma
-component of
\sigma
(which is constant as
U
is connected). So, there is the morphism:

(r_{\Delta})^{*}:H^{\bullet}(\Gamma,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)(\Delta))\to\check{H}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}^{\bullet}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\Lambda)).

This is inverse to
(i_{\Delta})^{*}
. Indeed, on the level of complexes
(i_{\Delta})^{*}\circ(r_{\Delta})^{*}
is the identity map (since
r_{\Delta}\circ i_{\Delta}=\textrm{id}_{\Gamma}
) and
(r_{\Delta})^{*}\circ(i_{\Delta})^{*}
is homotopic to the identity map via the homotopy
H
given by:

H(c)(U_{0}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{1}}\dots\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n})=\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}c(\Delta\xleftarrow{\sigma_{\gamma_{1}}}\dots\xleftarrow{\sigma_{\gamma_{i}}}\Delta\hookleftarrow U_{i}\xleftarrow{\sigma_{i+1}}\dots\xleftarrow{\sigma_{n}}U_{n}),

where
\Delta\hookleftarrow U_{i}
is the arrow with underlying bisection the unit map and
\gamma_{i}
is the constant value of the
\Gamma
-component of
\sigma_{i}
(so that
\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{\gamma_{i}}|_{U_{i}}
). So, both
(i_{\Delta})^{*}
and
(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}
are isomorphisms. This proves part
a
. For part
b
, note that the composition:

\check{H}^{n}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{(\ref{eqn:cechtoderivedmorph:equivshf})}H^{n}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{\Phi_{y}}H^{n}(\Gamma_{y},T)

is equal to the composition:

\check{H}^{n}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{(i_{\Delta})^{*}}H^{n}(\Gamma,\mathcal{L}(\Delta))\xrightarrow{(i_{\Gamma_{y}})^{*}}H^{n}(\Gamma_{y},\mathcal{L}(\Delta))\xrightarrow{(\textrm{ev}_{y})_{*}}H^{n}(\Gamma_{y},T),

where
\textrm{ev}_{y}:\mathcal{L}(\Delta)\to T
is the map of
\Gamma_{y}
-modules given by evaluation at
y
and
i_{\Delta}
is as before. So, it suffices to show that:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma,\mathcal{L}(\Delta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma_{y},\mathcal{L}(\Delta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma_{y},T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Gamma_{y}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Gamma_{y}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textrm{ev}_{y})_{*}}

commutes. Consider the map of
\Gamma
-modules
\tau_{\Delta}:T\to\mathcal{L}(\Delta)
that sends
t\in T
to the section of
\mathcal{T}=T\times\mathfrak{g}^{*}
with
T
-component constantly equal to
t
. Since
\textrm{ev}_{x}\circ\tau_{\Delta}=\textrm{id}_{T}
, the induced map:

(\tau_{\Delta})_{*}:H^{n}(\Gamma,T)\to H^{n}(\Gamma,\mathcal{L}(\Delta))

is the inverse of the isomorphism
(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}
. Therefore, it is enough to check the commutativity of:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma,\mathcal{L}(\Delta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma_{y},\mathcal{L}(\Delta))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{n}(\Gamma_{y},T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Gamma_{y}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Gamma_{y}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tau_{\Delta})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\textrm{ev}_{y})_{*}}

and this is readily verified on the level of cocycles, using the fact that
\textrm{ev}_{y}\circ\tau_{\Delta}=\textrm{id}_{T}
.
∎

We will also use the lemma below, which we leave for the reader to verify.

Lemma 4.29
.

Suppose we are given a symplectic Morita equivalence between regular and proper symplectic groupoids
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\Omega_{1})\rightrightarrows M_{1}
and
(\mathcal{G}_{2},\Omega_{2})\rightrightarrows M_{2}
that relates a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}_{1}\subset\underline{M}_{1}
to a Delzant subspace
\underline{\Delta}_{2}\subset\underline{M}_{2}
. For any
p\in P
, the isomorphism in cohomology induced (as in Corollary
3.3
) by the associated integral affine Morita equivalence (Example
1.37
) fits into a commutative square:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta_{1}},\mathcal{L}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{x_{1}},\mathcal{T}_{x_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{2}|_{\Delta_{2}},\mathcal{L}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{x_{2}},\mathcal{T}_{x_{2}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{x_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{p})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{x_{2}}}

where
x_{1}=\alpha_{1}(p)
,
x_{2}=\alpha_{2}(p)
and
\varphi_{p}:\mathcal{G}_{x_{1}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{G}_{x_{2}}
is the isomorphism of isotropy groups (
37
).

Proof of Theorem
4.27
.

Given an open
\underline{U}
in
\underline{\Delta}
, the
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{U},\mathcal{L})
-actions on the sets
I^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})
for
x\in U
(described before the statement of Theorem
4.27
)
induce an action on the set
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{U})
(denoting
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}:=\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set},(\mathcal{G},\Omega,\underline{\Delta})}
as in Definition
4.14
) given by:

(\textrm{c}\cdot\sigma)(x):=\Phi_{x}(\textrm{c})\cdot\sigma(x),\quad\textrm{c}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{U},\mathcal{L}),\quad\sigma\in\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{U}),\quad x\in U,

where on the right we use the action in Remark
1.5
. Note here that
\textrm{c}\cdot\sigma
is indeed
\mathcal{B}
-invariant and hence defines an element of
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{U})
. This can be seen using Lemma
4.29
, applied to the identity equivalence. For varying
\underline{U}
, these actions are compatible with restriction of opens and so descend to an action of the sheaf
\mathcal{H}^{1}
on the sheaf
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}
. As we will now show, this action is free in the sense that the
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{U})
-action on
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{U})
is free for each open
\underline{U}
in
\underline{\Delta}
. For this it is enough to show that each
\mathcal{O}\in\underline{U}
admits an open neighbourhood
\underline{V}
in
\underline{U}
such that the
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{V})
-action on
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{V})
is free. To this end, note that for each such
\mathcal{O}
there is (as in Subsection
4.1.3
) an invariant open
W
around
\mathcal{O}
in
M
, together with an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group
G
, a
G
-invariant open
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
around the origin in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, and a symplectic Morita equivalence:
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{W}
W
(P,\omega_{P})
W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})|_{W_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}}
\text{ }\text{ }\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{2}

that relates the orbit
\mathcal{O}
of
\mathcal{G}
to the origin in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
. After possibly shrinking
W
, we can further arrange that
V:=W\cap\Delta\subset U
and that the invariant subset
\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}
of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
related to
V
is convex. By Lemma
4.29
, for any choice of
x\in\mathcal{O}
and
p\in\alpha_{1}^{-1}(x)
we have a commutative square:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{V},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\Gamma\ltimes\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}},\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}^{*}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\Gamma,T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{p})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{x}}

in which the vertical maps are isomorphisms. This and part
a
of Lemma
4.28
show that:

(117)

\Phi_{x}:H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{V},\mathcal{L})\to H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})

is an isomorphism as well. It follows that if
\textrm{c}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{V},\mathcal{L})
and
\sigma\in\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{V})
are such that
\textrm{c}\cdot\sigma=\sigma
, then
\Phi_{x}(\textrm{c})\cdot\sigma(x)=\sigma(x)
, hence
\Phi_{x}(\textrm{c})=0
(since the action in Remark
1.5
is free), and so
\textrm{c}=0
. So, the
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{V})
-action on
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{V})
is free. This shows freeness of the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-action on
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}
.

Next, we will show that it restricts to a transitive action on
\mathcal{I}^{1}
. This would prove the first part of the theorem, since stalk-wise this
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-action on
\mathcal{I}^{1}
would be given by the actions described before the statement of Theorem
4.27
. To show that the action restricts, it is enough to show that for each open
\underline{U}
the
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{U},\mathcal{L})
-action on
\mathcal{I}^{1}_{\textrm{Set}}(\underline{U})
preserves
\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{U})
. Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{I}(\underline{U})
and
\mathcal{O}\in\underline{U}
. Since
\sigma
is flat, there is a symplectic Morita equivalence as above that relates
\sigma|_{\underline{V}}
(via Lemma
4.16
) to a centered section (Definition
4.17
). In view of Lemma
4.29
and part
b
of Lemma
4.28
, for any choice of
x\in\mathcal{O}
,
p\in\alpha_{1}^{-1}(x)
and
q\in Q
the diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{V},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\Gamma,T)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{y},\mathcal{T}_{y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\Gamma_{\alpha},T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{p})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(i_{\Gamma_{\alpha}})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\varphi_{q})_{*}}

commutes, where
y:=\alpha_{1}(q)
and
\alpha:=\alpha_{2}(q)
. Because of this, the section related to
(\textrm{c}\cdot\sigma)|_{\underline{V}}
by the Morita equivalence is centered as well, and so
\textrm{c}\cdot\sigma
is flat at
\mathcal{O}
, for any
\textrm{c}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{U},\mathcal{L})
. So, the
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{U},\mathcal{L})
-action indeed preserves
\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{U})
. For transitivitity, let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{U})
and
\mathcal{O}\in\underline{U}
. In view of Remark
4.22
, there is a symplectic Morita equivalence as above that relates both
\sigma_{1}|_{\underline{V}}
and
\sigma_{2}|_{\underline{V}}
to centered sections. Consider an
x\in\mathcal{O}
. By surjectivity of (
117
) and transitivity of the
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})
-action on
I^{1}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{T}_{x})
, there is a
\textrm{c}_{V}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{V},\mathcal{L})
such that
\Phi_{x}(\textrm{c}_{V})\cdot\sigma_{1}(x)=\sigma_{2}(x)
. From commutativity of the last diagram above and the fact that both
\sigma_{1}|_{\underline{V}}
and
\sigma_{2}|_{\underline{V}}
are related to centered sections by the Morita equivalence, it follows that in fact
\textrm{c}_{V}\cdot{\sigma_{1}}|_{\underline{V}}=\sigma_{2}|_{\underline{V}}
. This shows that there is an open cover
\underline{\mathcal{V}}
of
\underline{U}
with a collection
\{\textrm{c}_{V}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{V},\mathcal{L})\mid\underline{V}\in\underline{\mathcal{V}}\}
such that
\textrm{c}_{V}\cdot{\sigma_{1}}|_{\underline{V}}=\sigma_{2}|_{\underline{V}}
for each
\underline{V}\in\underline{\mathcal{V}}
. Since the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-action is free, for any
\underline{V}_{1},\underline{V}_{2}
the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-germs of
\textrm{c}_{V_{1}}
and
\textrm{c}_{V_{2}}
must coincide at all points in
\underline{V}_{1}\cap\underline{V}_{2}
. So, these glue to a section
\textrm{c}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{U})
such that
\textrm{c}\cdot\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}
. This shows that the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-action on
\mathcal{I}^{1}
is indeed transitive.

It remains to prove the second part of the theorem. We ought to show that the map (
13
) is
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
-equivariant with respect to the action in the first structure theorem and that on
\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})
obtained from the
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})
-action via the group homomorphism (
113
). After restricting to a complete transversal the proof reduces to the case in which
\mathcal{B}
is etale. To prove the equivariance in that case, suppose that
\mathcal{B}
is etale, that
\textrm{c}\in H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
and that
J:(S,\omega)\to M
is a faithful multiplicity-free
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space with momentum map image
\Delta
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a basis of
\Delta
for which
\mathcal{L}
is
\mathcal{U}
-acyclic, consisting of connected opens. Consider a
1
-cocycle
\tau
representing the class
[\tau]\in\check{H}^{1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
corresponding to
c
via the isomorphism (
67
). Let
x\in\Delta
. In view of Lemma
1.46
, there is a
U\in\text{$\mathcal{U}$}
with the property that for each
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}_{x}
there is a (necessarily unique) smooth bisection
\sigma_{\gamma}:U\to\mathcal{B}|_{U}
such that
\sigma(x)=\gamma
, and the map
\mathcal{B}_{x}\times U\to\mathcal{B}|_{U}
,
(\gamma,y)\mapsto\sigma_{\gamma}(y)
is surjective. Then, in fact, for each
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}|_{U}
there is a unique smooth bisection
\sigma_{\gamma}:U\to\mathcal{B}|_{U}
that maps
s(\gamma)
to
\gamma
. The composition:

\check{H}^{1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{(\ref{eqn:cechtoderivedmorph:equivshf})}H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{\Phi_{x}}H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x})

is equal to the composition:

\check{\Phi}_{x}:=(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}\circ(i_{U})^{*}:\check{H}^{1}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{(i_{U})^{*}}H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{L}(U))\xrightarrow{(\textrm{ev}_{x})_{*}}H^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{x},\mathcal{T}_{x}),

with
i_{U}:\mathcal{B}_{x}\to\textsf{Emb}_{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta})
the functor (where we view
\mathcal{B}_{x}
as category over
\{x\}
) that sends
\{x\}
to
U
and that sends an arrow
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}_{x}
to the arrow
U\xleftarrow{\sigma_{\gamma}}U
. So, we have to show that:

(118)

\check{\Phi}_{x}([\tau])\cdot e(J)_{x}=e(J_{\tau})_{x}.

Consider the map:

\varphi_{\tau}:(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{U}\to(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{U},\quad g\mapsto g\tau(U\xleftarrow{\sigma_{{[g]}}}U)(s(g))^{-1}.

This is an automorphism of symplectic groupoids with corners. To see this, let
\gamma\in\mathcal{B}|_{U}
. The image
\sigma_{\gamma}(U)
in
\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
is open, hence the pre-image of this under the projection
\mathcal{G}|_{\Delta}\to\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta}
is an open in
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
on which the map
\varphi_{\tau}
is given by the smooth map:

g\mapsto g\tau(U\xleftarrow{\sigma_{\gamma}}U)(s(g))^{-1}.

Since
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
can be covered by such opens,
\varphi_{\tau}
is smooth. Furthermore, the fact that each
\tau(U\xleftarrow{\sigma_{\gamma}}U)
is Lagrangian implies that
\varphi_{\tau}
is symplectic, and
\tau
being a
1
-cocycle implies that
\varphi_{\tau}
a morphism of groupoids. Moreover,
\varphi_{-\tau}
is inverse to
\varphi_{\tau}
. So,
\varphi_{\tau}
is indeed an automorphism. The pair
(\varphi_{\tau},j_{U})
consisting of
\varphi_{\tau}
and the symplectomorphism (
72
) is compatible with the
\mathcal{G}|_{U}
-actions, in the sense that for all
g\in\mathcal{G}|_{U}
and
p\in J^{-1}(U)
such that
s(g)=J(p)
it holds that:

j_{U}(g\cdot p)=\varphi_{\tau}(g)\cdot j_{U}(p).

Consequently, this pair induces an equivalence of symplectic representations:

(\varphi_{\tau},(\textrm{d}j_{U})_{p}):(\mathcal{G}_{p},(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{p},\omega_{p}))\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{G}_{[p,U]},(\mathcal{S}\mathcal{N}_{[p,U]},(\omega_{\tau})_{[p,U]}))

for each
p\in S
such that
J(p)=x
. Using Lemma
1.15
and the observation that
\varphi_{\tau}
restricts to the identity on
\mathcal{T}
, (
118
) readily follows from this.
∎

4.2.3.
The structure groups for the case of group actions

In this subsection we give the remaining details for Examples
5
and
6
. The facts used in Example
5
that remain to be proved are collected in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.30
.

Let
G
be an infinitesimally abelian compact Lie group with identity component
T
and group of connected components
\Gamma:=G/T
. Consider the cotangent groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega):=(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
and let
\underline{\Delta}
be a non-empty Delzant subspace of
\underline{M}=\mathfrak{g}^{*}/G
such that the corresponding invariant subspace
\Delta
of
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is convex. Then the following hold.

a)

For each
n>0
, there is a canonical group isomorphism:

H^{n}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{\sim}H^{n}(\Gamma,T)

b)

H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})=0
.

c)

There is a canonical bijection between the set
\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})
of global sections of the ext-sheaf (
8
) and
I^{1}(G,T)
, which together with the above group isomorphism is compatible with the action of
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
on
\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})
and the canonical action of
H^{1}(\varGamma,T)
on
I^{1}(G,T)
.

Proof.

Since the finite group
\Gamma
acts linearly on
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
\Delta
is convex and non-empty, there is a
\Gamma
-fixed point
x\in\Delta^{\Gamma}
(as follows by averaging over
\Gamma
). The map
\Phi_{x}:H^{n}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})\to H^{n}(\Gamma,T)
is an isomorphism by part
a
of Lemma
4.28
, and by part
b
of the same lemma this does not depend on the choice of
x\in\Delta^{\Gamma}
. This proves part
a
. For part
b
, note that
\Phi_{x}
factors as:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\Gamma,T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\Phi}_{x}}

with the horizontal map as in the sequence (
112
) and with
\widehat{\Phi}_{x}
given by composition of the evaluation at the stalk of
\mathcal{H}^{1}
at
\mathcal{O}_{x}
with the map from this stalk into
H^{1}(\Gamma,T)
induced by the maps (
114
). As (
112
) is exact, part
b
follows. For part
c
, consider the evaluation map:

(119)

\textrm{ev}_{x}:\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})\to I^{1}(G,T).

Clearly, this and the map
\Phi_{x}
are compatible with the action of
H^{1}(\mathcal{B}|_{\Delta},\mathcal{L})
and that of
H^{1}(\Gamma,T)
. To prove part
c
, we will show that this is a bijection that does not depend on the choice of
x\in\Delta^{\Gamma}
. For injectivity, suppose that
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})
are such that
\sigma_{1}(x)=\sigma_{2}(x)
. Let
y\in\Delta
. Then
(1-t)x+ty\in\Delta
for all
t\in[0,1]
, by convexity. Since
x
is fixed by
\Gamma
and the action is linear, it holds that
\Gamma_{(1-t)x+ty}=\Gamma_{y}
for all
t\in]0,1]
. Hence, the interval
]0,1]
is partitioned by the sets:

S([c])=\{t\in]0,1]\mid\sigma_{1}((1-t)x+ty)=[c]\},\quad[c]\in I^{1}(\Gamma_{y},T).

From flatness of
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
and the fact that
\sigma_{1}(x)=\sigma_{2}(x)
, it follows that
S(\sigma_{2}(y))
is non-empty. Moreover,
S([c])
is open in
]0,1]
for each
[c]\in I^{1}(G,T)
due to flatness of
\sigma_{1}
. So, by connectedness of
]0,1]
it must hold that
\sigma_{1}(y)=\sigma_{2}(y)
, which shows that (
119
) is indeed injective. For the remainder, consider the map:

I^{1}(G,T)\to\mathcal{I}^{1}(\underline{\Delta})

that sends
[c]\in I^{1}(G,T)
to the global section of
\mathcal{I}^{1}
defined by the section of (
103
) with value at
y\in\Delta
given by
[c|_{G_{y}}]\in I^{1}(G_{y},T)
(which is indeed invariant, as follows from Lemma
4.13
). Since this is a section of (
119
), we conclude that (
119
) is a bijection with this map as its inverse. As its inverse is independent of the choice of
x\in\Delta^{\Gamma}
, so is the map (
119
).
∎

Next, we return to Example
6
.

Example 4.31
.

Let
G
be any compact Lie group. Fix a maximal torus
T
and an open Weyl chamber
\mathfrak{c}
in
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. Further, let
N(\mathfrak{c})
be the normalizer of
\mathfrak{c}
in
G
. Consider the regular and proper symplectic groupoid
(\mathcal{G},\Omega):=(G\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}},-\textrm{d}\lambda_{\textrm{can}})
over
M:=\mathfrak{g}^{*}_{\textrm{reg}}
. Since
\mathfrak{c}
is a complete transversal,
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is canonically Morita equivalent to its restriction
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{\mathfrak{c}}
, which coincides with the restriction to
\mathfrak{c}
of cotangent symplectic groupoid of
N(\mathfrak{c})
. This Morita equivalence induces a bijection, as in (
75
), between isomorphism classes of compact, connected, regular, multiplicity-free Hamiltonian
G
-spaces (in the sense of Example
6
) and isomorphism classes of compact, connected, multiplicity-free Hamiltonian
N(\mathfrak{c})
-spaces with momentum map image contained in
\mathfrak{c}
. Explicitly, this is given by associating to a regular Hamiltonian
G
-space its restriction to
\mathfrak{c}
. To a Hamiltonian
N(\mathfrak{c})
-space
J:(S,\omega)\to\mathfrak{t}^{*}
as above we can associate the triple
(K,\Delta,e)
consisting of:

•

the kernel
K
of the morphism
T\to\textrm{Diff}(S)
obtained by restricting the
N(\mathfrak{c})
-action to
T
,

•

the image
\Delta:=J(S)
,

•

the ext-invariant
e
of the induced maximally toric Hamiltonian
N(\mathfrak{c})/K
-action (as in Example
5
), with momentum map
\mu-x:(S,\omega)\to(\mathfrak{t}/\mathfrak{k})^{*}
, for any
N(\mathfrak{c})
-fixed point
x\in\Delta
.

Note here that
e
does not depend on the choice of
N(\mathfrak{c})
-fixed point. All together, this gives a bijection between isomorphism classes of compact, connected, regular, multiplicity-free Hamiltonian
G
-spaces and triples as in Example
6
.

Finally, using the above proposition we can also complete the argument in Example
4.23
.

Example 4.32
.

To show that
H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})
vanishes in Example
4.23
, consider the open cover
\underline{\text{$\mathcal{U}$}}=\{\underline{U}^{+},\underline{U}^{-}\}
with
U^{\pm}:=W^{\pm}\cap\Delta
for
W^{\pm}:=\{(x,\mu)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\mid\mu\neq\pm 1\}
. Since
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{W^{+}}
and
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)|_{W^{-}}
are both isomorphic to restrictions of symplectic groupoids of the form in Proposition
4.30
via isomorphisms that identify
U^{+}
and
U^{-}
with convex sets, the cohomology groups
H^{1}(\underline{U}^{+},\underline{\mathcal{L}})
and
H^{1}(\underline{U}^{-},\underline{\mathcal{L}})
vanish. So, in view of the Mayer-Vietoris sequence, to show that
H^{1}(\underline{\Delta},\underline{\mathcal{L}})=0
it suffices to show that every
\tau\in\underline{\mathcal{L}}(\underline{U}^{+}\cap\underline{U}^{-})
can be written as the difference of a section of
\underline{\mathcal{L}}
on
\underline{U}^{+}
and one on
\underline{U}^{-}
. For this, note that by
\Gamma
-invariance of
\tau
, after possibly subtracting the section
[\frac{1}{2}\textrm{d}x]\in\underline{\mathcal{L}}(\underline{\Delta})
(obtained from the closed
1
-form
\frac{1}{2}\textrm{d}x
by projection along
T^{*}M\to\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
), we can assume that
\tau(0,i)=[c\,\textrm{d}\theta_{(0,i)}]
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
V^{\pm}:=\{(x,\mu)\in\Delta\mid\pm\textrm{Im}(\mu)>0\}
denote the connected components of
U^{+}\cap U^{-}
. Since
V^{+}
is simply-connected, there is a unique
f\in C^{\infty}(V^{+})
such that
\textrm{d}f
projects to
\tau|_{V^{+}}
along the covering map
T^{*}M\to\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda}
and such that
\textrm{d}f_{(0,i)}=c\,\textrm{d}\theta_{(0,i)}
and
f(0,i)=0
. Since
(0,i)
and
\textrm{d}f_{(0,i)}
are fixed by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\{1\}
, the function
f
is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\{1\}
-invariant. Hence, the function
\widehat{f}\in C^{\infty}(U^{+}\cap U^{-})
given by:

\widehat{f}(x,\mu)=\begin{cases}f(x,\mu)\quad&\text{ if }(x,\mu)\in V^{+}\\
f(x,\mu^{-1})\quad&\text{ if }(x,\mu)\in V^{-}\end{cases}

is
\Gamma
-invariant. Choose a smooth function
\rho\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
that has constant value
1
on a neighbourhood of
1
, that is supported in
\mathbb{S}^{1}-\{-1\}
and that is
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-invariant with respect to the action given by
\varepsilon\cdot\mu=\mu^{\varepsilon}
, for
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mu\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
. The respective functions:

(x,\mu)\mapsto\rho(\mu)\widehat{f}(x,\mu)\quad\&\quad(x,\mu)\mapsto(\rho(\mu)-1)\widehat{f}(x,\mu)

on
U^{+}\cap U^{-}
extend by zero to
\Gamma
-invariant smooth functions
f_{+}
and
f_{-}
on
U^{+}
and
U^{-}
, and the closed
1
-forms
\textrm{d}f_{+}
and
\textrm{d}f_{-}
project to respective sections
\tau_{+}\in\underline{\mathcal{L}}(\underline{U}^{+})
and
\tau_{-}\in\underline{\mathcal{L}}(\underline{U}^{-})
with the property that
\tau_{+}|_{U^{+}\cap U^{-}}-\tau_{-}|_{U^{+}\cap U^{-}}=\tau
. This proves the claim.

Appendix A
On the multiplicity-free condition

In this appendix we use results on the Poisson geometry of the orbit space of a Hamiltonian action to derive the following (in the same spirit as
[
48
, Proposition A.1]
).

Proposition A.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)\rightrightarrows M
be a regular and proper symplectic groupoid. A Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-action along
J:(S,\omega)\to M
is faithful multiplicity-free if and only if the following four conditions hold.

i)

The induced action of
\mathcal{T}
is free on a dense subset of
S
.

ii)

The equality:

(120)

\text{dim}(S)=2\text{dim}(M)-\textrm{rank}(\pi)

holds, where
\pi
is the Poisson structure on
M
induced by
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
.

iii)

The momentum map
J
has connected fibers.

iv)

The transverse momentum map
\underline{J}:\underline{S}\to\underline{M}
is closed as a map into its image.

To prove Proposition
A.1
, let us recall some facts on the Poisson geometry of Hamiltonian actions. Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a proper symplectic groupoid and let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space.

•

The sheaf of smooth functions
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\underline{S}}
on the orbit space
\underline{S}:=S/\mathcal{G}
is the sheaf of
\mathbb{R}
-algebras consisting of
\mathcal{G}
-invariant smooth functions on invariant opens in
S
. This can naturally be viewed as a subsheaf of the sheaf of continuous functions on
\underline{S}
. For each invariant open
U
in
S
, the subalgebra
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\underline{S}}(\underline{U})
of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{S}(U)
is a Poisson subalgebra with respect to the Poisson bracket associated to the symplectic form
\omega
on
S
. These Poisson brackets make
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\underline{S}}
into a sheaf of Poisson algebras, and as for smooth manifolds these brackets are uniquely determined by the single Poisson bracket on the algebra of global smooth functions on
\underline{S}
. The stratification
\mathcal{S}_{\textrm{Gp}}(\underline{S})
of
\underline{S}
induced by the
\mathcal{G}
-action has the property that each stratum
\underline{\Sigma}
admits a natural structure of Poisson manifold, uniquely determined by the fact that restriction along the inclusion
i:\underline{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow\underline{S}
induces a surjective map of sheaves
i^{*}:\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\underline{S}}|_{\underline{\Sigma}}\to\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\underline{\Sigma}}
that respects the Poisson brackets.

•

The
complexity
C(
J
) of the Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-action is, by definition, half of the maximum of the dimensions of the symplectic leaves on all of these strata. The dimension of the symplectic leaves in
\underline{S}
is locally non-decreasing (
[
38
, Proposition 2.92]
). Therefore, the union of the symplectic leaves in
\underline{S}
of maximal dimension is open in
\underline{S}
. This can be used to deduce (using for instance
[
38
, Proposition 2.69 and Remark 2.93]
) that, if the set of points in
S
at which the momentum map
J
is a submersion is dense in
S
, then the complexity of the Hamiltonian action is:

(121)

\textrm{C}(J)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{dim}(S)-2\text{dim}(M)+\max_{x\in J(S)}\textrm{rank}(\pi_{x})\right),

where
\pi
is the Poisson structure on
M
induced by
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
.

•

The symplectic leaves of the orbit space
\underline{S}
can be described in terms of the symplectic reduced spaces of the Hamiltonian action. As topological spaces, the reduced spaces are the subspaces of
\underline{S}
of the form:

J^{-1}(\mathcal{O})/\mathcal{G}=\underline{J}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}),

where
\mathcal{O}
is an orbit of
\mathcal{G}
in
M
. The reduced spaces can naturally be stratified into symplectic manifolds (generalizing the main result of
[
26
]
) and the symplectic leaves of the orbit space
\underline{S}
coincide with the symplectic strata of the reduced spaces.

The facts mentioned above are probably well-known for Hamiltonian actions of compact Lie groups. Such Hamiltonian actions that satisfy the equivalent conditions in the proposition below are called
multiplicity-free

[
18
]
.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
be a proper symplectic groupoid and let
J:(S,\omega)\to M
be a Hamiltonian
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
-space. Then the following are equivalent.

a)

The induced Poisson bracket on
C^{\infty}(\underline{S})
is the zero bracket.

b)

The Hamiltonian action has complexity zero.

c)

All of the reduced spaces are discrete topological subspaces of
\underline{S}
.

Proof.

As mentioned above, the Poisson brackets on the algebras associated to the sheaf of Poisson algebras
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\underline{S}}
are uniquely determined by the single Poisson bracket on the algebra
C^{\infty}(\underline{S})
. So, if
a
holds, then the Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{\underline{S}}(\underline{U})
is zero for each invariant open
U
in
S
. The Poisson structure on each stratum of
\mathcal{S}_{\textrm{Gp}}(\underline{S})
must then also be zero, so that
b
holds. Conversely, from
b
it is immediate that the Poisson bracket on each stratum is zero. Since the inclusion of each stratum is a Poisson map, the bracket on
C^{\infty}(\underline{S})
is zero as well, as follows from pointwise evaluation. So,
b
implies
a
. This proves the equivalence between
a
and
b
. The equivalence between
b
and
c
is clear from the description of the symplectic leaves as strata of the symplectic reduced spaces.
∎

We now turn to the proof of the main result of this appendix.

Proof of Proposition
A.1
.

First notice that both sets of conditions contain the assumption that the induced
\mathcal{T}
-action is free on a dense subset of
S
. The momentum map
J
is a submersion at all points in
S
at which the
\mathcal{T}
-action is free. Therefore, under both sets of conditions, the set of points in
S
at which
J
is a submersion is dense in
S
, which implies that the complexity of the Hamiltonian action is given by (
121
). Since
(\mathcal{G},\Omega)
is regular, this means that:

\textrm{C}(J)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\text{dim}(S)-2\text{dim}(M)+\textrm{rank}(\pi)\right).

Now, suppose that all properties in Proposition
A.1
are satisfied. By the above equation for the complexity of the Hamiltonian action, the second condition in Proposition
A.1
means that the action has complexity zero, which by Proposition
A.2
means that for every
x\in M
, the subspace
\underline{J}^{-1}({\mathcal{O}}_{x})
of
\underline{S}
is discrete. Since
\underline{J}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{x})
is (canonically) homeomorphic to the quotient
J^{-1}(x)/\mathcal{G}_{x}
and the
J
-fibers are assumed to be connected, it follows that both
J^{-1}(x)/\mathcal{G}_{x}
and
\underline{J}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{x})
consist of a single point. Firstly, it follows from this that the
\mathcal{G}_{x}
-orbit (which is embedded, seeing as
\mathcal{G}_{x}
is compact) is the subspace
J^{-1}(x)
of
S
. Since
J^{-1}(x)
is connected, the
\mathcal{T}_{x}
-orbit must then also be the entire space
J^{-1}(x)
. Secondly, it follows that
\underline{J}
is injective (its fibers being points). So, since it is assumed to be closed as map into its image, it must be a topological embedding. This proves that the Hamiltonian action is faithful multiplicity-free.

Next, suppose that the action is faithful multiplicity-free. Then clearly the transverse momentum map is closed as map into its image. Furthermore, since the
J
-fibers coincide with the
\mathcal{T}
-orbits, they are connected and for each
x\in M
the quotient
J^{-1}(x)/\mathcal{G}_{x}
consists of a single point. So, for every
x\in M
the set
\underline{J}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{x})
consists of a single point as well, which by Proposition
A.2
implies that the Hamiltonian action has complexity zero. By the above equation for the complexity, this proves that the Hamiltonian action satisfies all conditions in Proposition
A.1
.
∎

Appendix B
Background on manifolds with corners

Let
X
be a topological space. Given integers
n\geq 0
and
k\in\{0,...,n\}
, we use the notation:

\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}:=[0,\infty[^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k}.

By an
n
-dimensional chart with corners
for
X
we mean a pair
(U,\chi)
consisting of an open
U
in
X
and a homeomorphism
\chi
from
U
onto an open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
, for some
k\in\{0,...,n\}
. Given two subsets
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we say that a map
f:A\to B
is smooth if for every
x\in A
there is an open
U_{x}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
around
x
and a smooth map
U_{x}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
that coincides with
f
on
U_{x}\cap A
. Two charts with corners
(U,\chi)
and
(V,\varphi)
on
X
are called smoothly compatible if both transition maps between them are smooth maps in this sense. Just as for manifolds without corners, this leads to a notion of smooth atlas consisting of charts with corners for
X
(that we require to consist of charts of a fixed dimension) and every such atlas is contained in a unique maximal one. We refer to a maximal such atlas as a
smooth structure with corners
on
X
.

Definition B.1
.

A
smooth manifold with corners
is a second countable and Hausdorff space
X
together with a smooth structure with corners on
X
. Henceforth, by a manifold with corners we always mean a
smooth
manifold with corners and we omit the smooth structure from the notation. Furthermore, we use the following terminology and notation.

•

The common dimension of the charts for
X
is called the
dimension
of
X
, denoted
\text{dim}(X)
.

•

By a
smooth map

f:X\to Y
between two manifolds with corners we will mean a continuous map with the property that for any chart with corners
(U,\chi)
for
X
and any chart with corners
(V,\varphi)
for
Y
, the coordinate representation:

\varphi\circ f\circ\chi^{-1}:\chi(U\cap f^{-1}(V))\to\varphi(V)

is smooth in the sense above.

•

A homeomorphism
f:X\to Y
between manifolds with corners is called a
diffeomorphism
if
f
and
f^{-1}
are smooth.

Remark B.2
.

The above definition of smooth map coincides with that used in
[
40
,
21
,
39
]
. For a further comparison to the literature on manifolds with corners and maps between them, see
[
20
]
(where a smooth map in the above sense is called weakly smooth).

Remark B.3
.

Any open subspace of a manifold with corners
X
inherits a smooth structure with corners and the
\mathbb{R}
-valued smooth functions on these opens form a sheaf of algebras
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{X}
on
X
. Two smooth structures with corners coincide if their associated sheaves of smooth functions coincide.

Remark B.4
.

A manifold with corners
X
is a reduced differentiable space with structure sheaf
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{X}
, in the sense of
[
16
]
(also see
[
38
]
for a more direct introduction to these). Furthermore, a continuous map
f:X\to Y
between manifolds with corners is smooth if and only if it is a morphism of the underlying reduced differentiable spaces (meaning that for every smooth function on an open in
Y
, the pull-back along
f
is again smooth). As for any second countable and Hausdorff reduced differentiable space, there exist
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{X}
-partitions of unity subordinate to any open cover.

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional manifold with corners. Given
x\in X
, let
\textrm{Charts}_{x}(X)
denote the set of charts for
X
around
x
. As for manifolds without corners, one can define the tangent space
T_{x}X
of
X
at
x
as the
n
-dimensional real vector space of maps:

\textrm{Charts}_{x}(X)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}

that are compatible with coordinate changes (so that any such map is determined by its value on a single chart), and for every smooth map
f:X\to Y
between manifolds with corners one can define its differential
\textrm{d}f_{x}:T_{x}X\to T_{f(x)}Y
at
x
. The composition of two smooth maps is again smooth, and the chain rule still holds.

Remark B.5
.

The tangent space
T_{x}X
is naturally isomorphic to the vector space of derivations at
x
of the stalk of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{X}
at
x
, and to that of the algebra of global smooth functions on
X
.

Remark B.6
.

On manifolds with corners one can define smooth vector fields and differential forms, the pull-back of differential forms along smooth maps, their wedge-product and their exterior derivative, as for manifolds without corners. The Poincaré Lemma still holds: every closed differential form is locally exact, by arguments as in the case without corners (e.g. as in
[
4
]
).

Unlike for manifolds without corners, there may be tangent vectors that cannot be realized as the derivative of a smooth curve in
X
. A tangent vector
v\in T_{x}X
is called
inward pointing
if there is an
\varepsilon>0
and a smooth curve
\gamma:[0,\varepsilon[\to X
such that
v=\dot{\gamma}(0)
. The inward pointing tangent vectors form a polyhedral cone
C_{x}X
in
T_{x}X
, that we call the
tangent cone
of
X
at
x
. We let
F_{x}X
denote the largest linear subspace of
T_{x}X
that is contained in
C_{x}X
. This consists of those
v\in T_{x}X
for which there is an
\varepsilon>0
and a smooth curve
\gamma:]-\varepsilon,\varepsilon[\to X
such that
v=\dot{\gamma}(0)
. For any smooth map
f:X\to Y
and
x\in X
, it holds that:

(122)

\textrm{d}f_{x}(C_{x}X)\subset C_{f(x)}Y\quad\&\quad\textrm{d}f_{x}(F_{x}X)\subset F_{f(x)}Y.

For any chart
(U,\chi)
for
X
onto an open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
that sends
x\in U
to the origin, the differential
\textrm{d}\chi_{x}:T_{x}X\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{R}^{n}
identifies
C_{x}X
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
and
F_{x}X
with
\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-k}
. The
depth
of
x\in X
is:

\textrm{depth}_{X}(x):=\text{dim}(X)-\text{dim}(F_{x}X).

For any chart
(U,\chi)
for
X
around
x
mapping onto an open in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{k}
, the depth of
x
equals the number of
j\in\{1,...,k\}
such that
\chi^{j}(x)=0
.

Next, we turn to embeddings. Following
[
21
]
, we use the definition below.

Definition B.7
.

We call a topological embedding
i:X\to Y
between manifolds with corners a
smooth embedding
if it is smooth and at each point in
X
its differential is injective.

Example B.8
.

Given manifolds with corners
X
and
Y
, the product
X\times Y
inherits a natural structure of smooth manifold with corners. For any smooth map
f:X\to Y
, the graph map:

X\to X\times Y,\quad x\mapsto(x,f(x)),

is a smooth embedding.

Remark B.9
.

A topological embedding
i:X\to Y
between manifolds with corners is a smooth embedding if and only if
i:(X,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{X})\to(Y,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{Y})
is an embedding of reduced differentiable spaces, meaning that
i
is smooth and for every smooth function
g
on an open
U
in
X
and every
x\in U
there is a smooth function
\widehat{g}
on an open
U_{x}
in
Y
around
i(x)
such that
\widehat{g}\circ i
coincides with
g
on
U\cap i^{-1}(U_{x})
. Here, the forward implication follows using the immersion theorem for smooth maps between opens in Euclidean spaces, while the backwards implication is clear from Remark
B.5
. In view of Remark
B.4
we conclude from this characterization that: if
i:X\to Y
is a smooth embedding of manifolds with corners, then a map
f:Z\to X
from another manifold with corners into
X
